Thu Nov 25 2021 15:28:39 PST

‘Hurt Me! No, Not Like That!’ A Conversation with Rhea Myers

Maya B. Kronic  Your work is obviously about more than technology. It’s full of formulations like ‘Blockchain Aesthetics’ and ‘Blockchain Poetics’, and I was struck by your description of Bitcoin as a ‘development in cyberculture’ rather than just a technology. Many of your works exhibit a sensitivity to the charm and energy of the cultural scenes that assemble around technological platforms and indeed play a part in their growth. Dogecoin, Ethereum, Rare Pepes, these are all instances of the collective charisma that can burgeon around an online project, even something as apparently nakedly financial as a cryptocurrency. In an article that you cite in ‘Blockchain Poetics’, Brett Scott argues that a rational account of the emergence of money is always inadequate because, in order for exchangeable objects to accrue social value, they have to already be imbued with some kind of cultural energy, something akin to artistic value.1

Rhea Myers  Prior to Bitcoin I was looking at bronies, and before that at more general internet culture, I just immerse myself in these things. I describe culture as Dogecoin’s secret sauce, because it didn’t have a particularly better algorithm than Bitcoin, although it is slightly faster. The thing that was different was its community’s Bill-and-Ted-esque spirit of excellence and charity and support and welcoming and inclusion. Something which I assume was calculated to begin with, but as far as you could tell, was pure and in earnest. Even today, if you dropped the Bitcoin and Ethereum and Doge communities in a barren wasteland somewhere and came back in two years time, I know which one I would put my money on still having any kind of functioning social fabric. That in part is what ‘Bad Shibe’ is about. Unfortunately the social is all but invisible to the crypto bros who believe that conventions just appear and communities just spring up. That wilful ignorance of the social labour of reproducing crypto culture is just fascinating, not to mention that it’s transparently and ridiculously gendered too. So looking at those more social aspects does give you a critical way into the culture. And gaps in the culture give you clawholds on the sloping glass roof of the nice happy libertarian-to-VC pipeline of crypto technology.

MBK   The word ‘currency’ understood not just in terms of money but also as involving a cultural current, a nowness which has a social and emotional dimension.

RM  Yes, which allows the bootstrapping of a socially valued currency—and makes FOMO, the fear of missing out (e.g. on a token drop), the original source of value! If we take Marx’s view of the evolution of money—treated very trollishly in ‘Why Bitcoin is Money According to Marx’—there’s no mysterious meteor that hits the earth and imbues everything with value, there’s a base layer of things, people want one thing more than any other for some irrational reason, and it becomes a medium of exchange precisely because of this charm. This then spins up into financial hyperspace and turns into money.

MBK  As Scott points out, Bitcoin, for example, is imbued with the mystique of Satoshi Nakamoto.

RM  And he’s absolutely right there. Technology is of course socially embedded and produced, and technology has a cultural history and is a part of the history of ideas. The mythology of Satoshi is incredible, they’re the absent founder who has, not ascended to heaven, but returned to perfect cypherpunk secrecy, and that’s so generative for Bitcoin. Just as the figure of the doge for Dogecoin means that you have this sort of non-human benevolent avatar, and for Ethereum Vitalik Buterin is this monklike figure who does his best not to get drawn into squabbles whether they come from outside or within the Ethereum project. So those sort of founding myths or prophet figures are there.

But the choice of technology also shapes the kind of culture you’re engaged with. Like the representation schemes used for different cryptocurrency addresses—Bitcoin addresses are easy to recognise and contain a checksum to ensure you type them correctly, Ethereum uses a format that is very easy to get wrong. That generates different social conversations. There is a culture to coding, to the choice of technology, to the mixing and prioritising and valuing of different technological elements. I may be overdoing it, this may be only one percent and the culture around it is the other ninety-nine, but the dream of the cypherpunks is that you can somehow match technologies to your political objectives. And in the case of Bitcoin, that succeeded beyond anyone’s wildest dreams.

MBK  That suggests that there may be a politics embedded in the technology, and yet you seem to appeal to your readers to pare Bitcoin, and blockchain technology more generally, away from the various political ideologies that have appropriated it, or with which it’s associated by its denigrators. Is ideology baked into Bitcoin as a project, or not?

RM  Bitcoin is a practical solution to an open problem in the cypherpunk political project. The cypherpunks wanted private communication, and they got it, and that’s amazing—that’s what the Crypto Wars were fought for, and we’re all very grateful to them for that, it was a positive outcome for society. But beyond that, what they wanted was state-free everything, because they have a strong anarcho-capitalist bent. They wanted state-free money, and there were various sketches for that which almost worked, but if they involved the technical necessity for a central authority, then that can be compromised and the state or some other party can interfere in the social exchange of value at the moment of transaction. The thing that Bitcoin does is to make it impossible to interfere at the moment of transaction. This is a very modest achievement. There are many other things that you need to do in order to have a free society, however conceived. But within the cypherpunk political project, Bitcoin is a genuine solution to a problem in how they wish to justly organise social relations.

So to the people who get so mad about Bitcoin as a product of ideology: you’re absolutely right, it’s the product of cypherpunk ideology, it is antistatist, and it does not take a stance against the commodity form of either property or money. That’s something I talk about in great detail in ‘Blockchain Poetics’ because, as far as I can tell, the difference between anarcho-capitalists and anarcho-socialists comes down to whether they believe it is possible or desirable to abolish the commodity form. But when it comes to Bitcoin, the Left in general has been very upset that, with this exclusion of a potential moment for injustice at the moment of the transaction, there’s also an exclusion of a potential moment for redistributive justice. No redistribution of wealth can happen here. But whether you’re a left- or right-wing anarchist, you want the state out of people’s social relations. And after the abolition of the bourgeois/neoliberal/etc. state there won’t be a state to redistribute anything anymore anyway, so why are you getting so upset about this? In ‘Blockchain Poetics’ I go into a lot of detail mapping from anarcho-capitalism and the cypherpunk program to the specific design choices that Bitcoin makes, to try and inform imagined socialist interventions in the technology. There is a history of projects that take cryptocurrency and say, Okay, we’re gonna change this and make it work in a more benevolent way, and in doing so change the very finely tuned engineering constraints on Bitcoin and end up with something that is exploitative, unstable, or gameable in ways they simply wouldn’t want. Unfortunately there’s a belief that your good heart will get you there and you won’t need to get your hands dirty with the engineering. But however problematic the figure of the engineer is for socialist theory, and however little you may like these ideological underpinnings, you have to make an attempt to understand them.

I’m not exactly shocked when people who think all technology is fascism decide that Bitcoin is fascism. But it’s not, it is a genuinely anarchist technology, it is an anti-statist technology. I can fully understand how uncomfortable it is for the reformist ‘the best we can hope for is trivial modifications to the state until we all die baking in the anthropocene sun’ tendency to encounter something that actually operationalises a political programme, but this technology precisely excludes the possibility of an actual fascist state intruding into your ability to carry out exchanges. Take a look at the United States today, where Bitcoin might come in extremely useful if you want to pay for an abortion, pay for hormones, pay to flee the country. These are all things we’ve been told no one would ever need to use money to do, or need to do without the state watching. Lo and behold, it’s 2022 and people are almost beginning to put together the idea that a state panopticon of money and a government that is not your friend may not be the best combination.

None of this means that you can or have to ignore the large pools of capital involved, which are very easy to examine—precisely because current public blockchains are transparent. Actually taking the time to find out how to perform that kind of examination is another part of the demand that I make for competence in critical engagement with this technology. With Bitcoin you are beholden to those who came before you because they mined Bitcoin back when it was possible to do so with a modest amount of computing power. Or, in the case of Ethereum and other later currencies, they were smart enough and/or privileged and/or well-informed enough to get in on the pre-sale. So there is a tendency for there to be a hierarchy of early versus late adopters, something which is obviously not friendly to left anarchism. It’s very friendly to right anarchism because right anarchism, anarcho-capitalism, views the state as an impediment to your personal power and particularly to the power of your money. In contrast to this, left anarchists view the state as an impediment to a more equal society. These are very different views on what removing the state will achieve for society.

So Bitcoin has that first-mover advantage, not quite by accident but certainly with less of a programme than the later cryptocurrencies—those that followed it do really bake in first-mover advantage in a way that I’m tempted to say wouldn’t be such a problem for the Left if they had been mining from day one.

MBK  That brings us to your relation to left-wing politics, which is obviously not without its complexities. In many of these texts you seem to be espousing an extreme accelerationism—removing all the brakes, whether social, technological, or psychological, on the tendencies unleashed by blockchain technology. Your writing deliberately makes it difficult to evaluate the level of irony at play, something that has been a constant source of amusingly heated Twitter exchanges.

RM  I’ve had a lifelong feeling of being an outsider. I’ve certainly been socio-economically privileged: I’m from the end of the post-war settlement, the state paid for my education, I’m white-passing, I know people who had to work far harder than me to be part of the technology industry in England, and some of those people gave me a chance in that industry as a newly graduated art student. But that didn’t give me any secure or lasting sense of belonging or security. And so, to a degree, since I was chronically unsuccessful at ‘finding my people’, my work tended to be addressed to whatever group of people I was annoyed with at the time. Usually a group that I was or am a part of. For example, I describe ‘Bad Shibe’ as my exasperated love letter to the Vancouver crypto scene.

One of the techniques I often use came originally from a conversation on an old mailing list with one of the writers of the Twilight 2000 role-playing game. One thing I liked about the imagined future historical scenario of that game was that it disappoints everyone’s expectations for a Third World War equally. If you’re a hawk, the military effort just grinds down to a degrading and shameful standstill; if you’re a political wonk, the American government breaks down to the point where there are running gun battles in Congress; if you’re someone who’s anti-nuclear, well, there’s a nuclear conflict with horrific effects but civilisation is crawling on and things are really only about as bad as they were in the mid-twentieth century, and so on. Nobody gets to say Aha, I told you so! This is bathos, the deflation of grand claims.

Rhea Myers, Secret Artwork, 2018. ERC-721 NFT.

This movement of bathos—which is something I also find in the work of the artist collective Art & Language—is present in everything I do in these projects, where however grand the claim made at the start, there’s a moment where you have to say, Okay, but is that it? There’s got to be more to this…and there is always more to it, but you have to be able to make the jump to those coordinates, to be able to say, for Secret Artwork (2018) for instance, Possibly the world’s most ridiculously secure unknowable secret isn’t just there for me to go wow, they put a hash on the blockchain. Or for Tokens Equals Text (2019), Maybe there’s more going on in this little vaporwave typography display than meets the eye….

Rhea Myers, Tokens Equal Text, 2019. Ethereum ERC-998 and ERC-721 tokens.

However ironic the work, I am reflexively left-wing. I trust the Left to at least kill me more slowly than the Right. I feel no joy (and didn’t from a very early age) in anything restrictively traditional or in any kind of reproductive ideal that you have to live up to. And I’m certainly a very boring atheist. But I really felt the critique of Nick Srnicek and Alex Williams’s ‘Manifesto for Accelerationist Politics’.2 I know that you can make a very good career from beating up on identity politics, and as someone who ‘has an identity’, I would like to be very clear that I do not wish to do that. But at the same time, the genteel libidinal security of merely symbolic resistance is essentially a ritual blessing for whatever it is supposedly against. The insufficiency of the institutionalised Left and of always-already-defeated mediatised leftist politics, which the Manifesto really nails, is something that’s always deeply irritated me.

So what you see here is my exasperation and heartbreak and my aching wish for a better world—which I don’t have the economic or psychological or rhetorical or discursive security to put into mere dreamwork. I am unable to say I’m starting this artist’s cooperative, we’re gonna pay artists a living wage and everything will be great without also thinking Okay, where does the money come from for this?

MBK  Asking questions about money is particularly unwelcome in the contemporary art community, which is simultaneously deeply complicit with capital, and requires everyone present to affirm it as a singular site of criticality and resistance against capitalism. What does it mean to introduce into that world artworks made in a medium that is transparently (in every sense) a monetary instrument?

RM  Remember that old Ad Reinhardt cartoon of a gentleman in a trilby pointing at an abstract painting and saying What does this represent?, and the painting pointing back at him very angrily and saying What do you represent? In the context of the contemporary art world I would modify it to say Who pays for this? And then the painting (or the NFT) points back and says Who pays for you? It’s a milieu where you’re always working for some blob of capital that has come from taxing miners or selling slaves or taking the tops off of mountains and digging rubies out of them or whatever, but there is generally a comfortable ignorance of how the sausage is made, and you get to sit there and say to people that money is this horrible dirty thing. This has happened to me on a number of occasions: I can see that you are getting your money from a venture-capital-funded corporation, that’s terrible. I have this unproblematic position from which I can say that you should do this other thing that I have in mind. And that other thing turns out to be some sort of pantomime of value which only works if you have secure income and healthcare, which of course you have if you work for a big nonprofit or state institution. The fact that the money paying for it is the heat noise of historical capitalist exploitation seems to be far less problematic in that context because you can’t see it clearly, whereas Bitcoin is pure messenger, in the sense that it gets shot every time because you can see the money. My response to that isn’t to point out how terrible what we are seeing is, it’s to point out that being aware of it in this way is a useful thing. Here we have a transparent and plastic medium for looking at the exchange of value, where it comes from and where it goes to.

This lack of plausible deniability, the fact that not only do you know how the sausage is made, but you’ve got a sausage machine here whirring away on your own desktop—it changes things. Of course I know that there is no ethical production under capitalism. But why is this the production we’re objecting to so vocally? I appreciate that part of it is because people feel that crypto genuinely has no value, that it genuinely has no social utility that could not be achieved by more just and less socially and economically and ecologically harmful means. But as I often say to people, and as they often get very annoyed at me for saying, if it could be achieved in another way it would already have been, and if had been, then the same people who you don’t like buying up blockchain projects would be buying up projects on that because, you know, we’re like little financial amoeba around these financial gravity wells.

Blockchain technology is a tiny crack, a tiny shifting of the ass cheeks of capital, it’s a moment of potential for a temporary autonomous zone. And I’ve been amazed by how long that moment has lasted. I’m not here preventing revolution, I’m here trying to get a minor redistribution of wealth within this moment where capital has not yet slammed shut the door on any alternative or any support for anything other than adopting a hyperextractivist attitude in every single social relation. And frankly, I regard that as more useful than most of what the people who get terribly upset with me for doing that are trying to do.

MBK  So should we also add some salt to the tacit antihumanist suggestion in some of these works that, if we left the market to it, it could probably do a better job than humans at most things?

RM  I don’t actually believe in markets. I’m a ‘real markets have never been tried’ person, because the ‘markets’ we have are structured by the state: you need fiat money, you need the state legal system, you need the violence of the state to protect the commodity form and break strikes. You need all of that for markets of the kind that people game-theorise and insist upon naturalising. There may be a possible post-revolutionary anarcho-marketist ‘markets-but-not-capitalism’ wonderland, but I don’t have the heart to see it. ‘Bad Shibe’ is as close as I can get to seeing it, but it’s a scary world: it has child labour and there are black helicopters doing who knows what, and there’s a bug in the code that’s gonna cause the blockchain to jam in about two days time (if I ever get around to writing the sequel)….

So I’m not a market anarchist, I just play one on the internet. And in those moments you mention, what I’m doing is not entirely a bit, but it is a critical move.
The reason I make it in the form of an apparently first-order statement is that no-one is going to listen or give me the benefit of the doubt if I distance myself from it by saying Here are my complex emotional and history-of-ideas feelings about markets, now let’s talk about them.

And when it comes to blockchain art, things are not as straightforward as artists vs technocapitalists. I was once on a panel where I was doing that whole bit: we can just price art, and technology will save us…and Kevin McCoy pointed out that we were both from the art world and we were the people who created the ideas that became NFTs, so NFTs were not this spontaneously generated market phenomenon. Of course the standard was made by some very smart computer science and blockchain people, and I’m not trying to erase them, but all of the early NFT-type projects had at least someone from the art world or art academia involved in them.

So perhaps I play that role far too well. I did have someone come up to me once and say, ‘Hey, I’ve figured out how to make Arewethereyet from “Bad Shibe”!’ And I had to say, ‘Can you not? It’s a dystopian satire of credit score gamification that I really would not like to see be a real thing in the world…’.

Ultimately, what I want more than anything is for someone to say No, you are wrong, here is a better alternative. I have a genuine anger for imaginary solutions that are not critical or even prefigurative, that are nothing more than affordable hobbies for those who use them to resolve the merely psychological contradictions of their own existence as agents of the social reproduction of the ideology of capitalism. That anger and heartbreak and sadness goes into making this stuff and just hoping that someone who is kinder and smarter and better than me has an answer that is not merely confected, that is not merely a necessary cog in the clockwork machinery that neutralises revolutionary potential by redirecting it into dreamwork.

MBK  So operationalising Schelling Point convergence in order to optimise aesthetics and art criticism (‘The ABC of Accelerationist Blockchain Critique’) isn’t an entirely serious proposition?

RM  More of a modest proposal, I’d say, although it’s a functional one and one that would change things in interesting ways if it actually happened. The epitome of this is ‘Maximum Transaction Density’, which is as Janus-faced as everything else I write, but if as a reader you get to the end of it and treat it as simply a first-order call to turn ourselves into blockchain clickworkers or whatever, then it has failed miserably. It is precisely the most intense attempt I have made to horrify the imagined reader into either accepting that, yes, this will achieve our ends, horrific though it is, and we should pursue this, or protesting in horror, No, that’s not right because…. ‘Maximum Transaction Density’ is about the horror of getting what you want by means that you don’t want. If anyone has a better idea for how to do what they want than what’s in there, I’m all ears.

Just to try and wrap up my apology for my good leftist soul: some of this is simply Edward-de-Bono-style creative thinking. At times you have to make a sideways leap or lateral move to avoid being stuck in a rut. Working through these ideas, some of it is provocation, some of it is aspiration, some of it is education and…it’s not demanding that others do the work for me, it’s doing the work that I can do and hoping that others will be able to respond in a way that I haven’t already heard and been disappointed by.

Brian Reffin Smith, Usborne Guide to Computers (Usborne Publishing Ltd., 1981).

MBK  Your career in art eventually collided with computing, cyberculture, and cypherpunk, but how did it begin?

RM  Both of my parents worked in arts education at various levels throughout their lives. When I was little I would go to my father’s basement office at Central Saint Martins and smell photo developer and be very impressed by all the big machines. So I had that background. And then we were members of a militant offshoot of the scouting movement, although by that point it was just a bunch of families doing cheap camping holidays somewhere in England each summer. The kids who were one high-school generation up from me there, the cool kids, were all into art and were going off to art school, so that was a major influence.

There were a couple of books in the art room at high school that were important for me, books the new art teacher had brought in (thank you Miss Hamer!) There was the Warhol retrospective catalogue from 1987, which I sat and read front to back many times; and another which was the catalogue from an, at least in part, conceptual retrospective. The two works I can remember from it are one of the Conceptual Art projects where someone introduces two strangers to each other to have a conversation, and an early example of lifelogging, of the quantified self—an artist who kept track of their activities over a week or a month or something.

This was around 1989, I was reading this at age sixteen to eighteen, so obviously I liked H.R. Giger, I liked graphic design, album covers, and all that kind of thing, very much in line with what kids of that generation were into on the visual culture front. But from the get-go I wasn’t into trying to copy heroic Boris Vallejo-style art like some of my best friends were. Apart from anything else, I knew I couldn’t draw that well. I also lived through the home computing boom in the UK, and one of the books I got from book club was the Usborne Guide to Computers, which featured Harold Cohen and his AARON drawing robot, and Ken Knowlton’s BEFLIX programming language. So from the age of about ten or twelve I had the idea that, even if I myself couldn’t do it, there were people out there making art with computers. And when I began an art foundation course in 1991, I made friends with the technicians, which meant that I could squeeze past them in their converted corridor office to use one of the two Macintoshes in the building. I can still remember seeing Photoshop boot for the first time. It was something very different: going from a very this-is-technical self-taught command-line programmer world to the very positively commodified design of the Macintosh.

Rhea Myers, ‘Balloon Dog’, 2011, from Shareable Readymades. 3D digital model.

During that period I did a lot of screen printing and dye-transfer work. But I had this desire to make a sort of remix- or sampling-based art where I was taking things and chopping them up digitally and rearranging them. And by the time I got to art school proper, I was deeply in love with the sample-based musics that were going mainstream at that moment, and I was sure that was something I wanted to embrace. I had Andy Warhol as a reference, and I became an instant and lifelong Jeff Koons fan (sorry haters), between the pink Taschen book, which I still have on my bookcase, and the little red handbook which has that great quote: ‘If you put art in the hands of a monarch, it will reflect his ego and eventually become decorative. If you put it in the hands of the masses, it will reflect mass ego and eventually become decorative. If you put art in the hands of Jeff Koons, it will reflect my ego and eventually become decorative.’ And Koons’s comment about Warhol being about distribution, and his work being about the penetration of consciousness.

Later in the decade I found things like No Logo, and could conceptualise that sort of very Nike-style branding and Adbusters-inflected art, which you can follow through much later into Shareable Readymades (2011–2013), where I tried to take iconic art-historical objects and return them to the cultural commons by publishing them as 3d-printable digital models.

MBK  When did Conceptual Art begin to play a significant role in what you were doing?

RM  I’d encountered some Conceptual Art in that catalogue when I was at school, and on my foundation course we went on a field trip to the Serpentine for the exhibition Objects for the Ideal Home: The Legacy of Pop Art (1991), which included work by Koons and by Julian Opie, who was also a massive influence. I found out about the Lisson Gallery via Opie, and it was at Lisson that I first encountered Art & Language and had my mind blown by their writing and art.

On my fine art degree course at Canterbury College of Art I was at war with the Cultural Studies department because…they had not explained to us why reading Derrida was a Good Idea when I just wanted to cut pictures up and make weird things on my computer. But Art & Language was different, this was something that fascinated me and that I wanted to push through: it would take me a day to read two pages of Essays on Art & Language,3 which ultimately is a fairly straightforward bit of materialist art history by an Open University lecturer, but which to me at the time, relative to my interests up until then, was rocket science. I’d pick up odd copies of Art-Language and I wouldn’t feel so bad about writing raw PostScript programming code, you know, it was impenetrable!

Art & Language, Art-Language journal, February 1970.

From Art & Language and from Opie I took a couple of things: firstly, project-based work: there’s nothing wrong with project-based work, you can make work that completes a certain project and then move on. And secondly, the idea that it’s possible to make artworks that are critical and, as Art & Language would later call it, essayistic, without them merely illustrating a text—that really went in. And then there was Art & Language’s historical-materialist view of art, which is very unconvinced by the official story of Modernism and the claim that art is about sensations and that the artwork must be securely contained within the edge of the frame otherwise you risk polluting this wonderful clean world of formal composition.

The refusal of that sense of modernist autonomy tied into my enthusiasm for pop and computer art and graphic design work too—I was obsessed with The Designers Republic at that point, I gradually came to admire Tomato, and I loved Peter Saville’s work for Factory. This was very much the pop-culture-postmodern era when low culture was being celebrated, but rather than simply being swept along by the megaflow of consumerism, I was able to get some purchase on it with this art which assembled some of those materials in a way that made them somehow indigestible—in Art & Language’s case, indigestible to the well-educated nice spectator who just wants to go to the museum and be flattered before going back to their job; in my case, indigestible in the sense of something that doesn’t want to be simply reducible to the ambient clichés of the time, or to be immediately recuperable by capitalism, by The Way Things Are.

This is not as easy as it sounds, of course, and later I’d come to the realisation that art is R&D for capital: artists love making things that can’t be bought, but capital also loves things that can’t be bought, because they help it work out how to expand its property regime so that it can buy them. But even when I was doing my foundation degree show in 1995 I was thinking about price to some extent. I was showing prints of work that I’d designed in Corel Draw and Illustrator, vector- and text-based work, but printed out giant, as big as was possible in ’95. So I had these A0 digital photocopies—not laser prints but photocopies of smaller laser prints—up on the wall. And the price sheet was based on how much you didn’t want anyone else to have a copy. So if you wanted a little print that was fine, you know, it’s ten quid. But if you wanted a large print and for me to not make any more of them, then that would be £300.

MBK  So you were already asking questions about how the artwork interacted with price?

RM  Yes. Because this was digital art, and there was no existing model for its value.

MBK  Potentially infinitely reproducible, and therefore of no rarity value.

RM  Which was fine with me, but the problem that I had not thought about while making it was, how are we going to show it? As soon as it became possible to shove the work into a Git repository4 and tell a curator, Just grab the repository and do what you want with it, I was in hog heaven: I didn’t have to solve the problem, I just adopted a Creative Commons licence for all the work I could so that, by about 2003, nobody ever had to ask me for permission ever again (although they always do).

But at that point, my solution to the problem of producing work that fitted very awkwardly into either printmaking or existing digital imagery—and I did regard the computer as a printmaking medium at that time—was initially to let the market sort it out and, later, to let technology sort it out.

MBK  Were you already using exclusively digital media at that time?

RM  Yes. I had worked out that I had no 3D perception whatsoever. I cannot do sculpture. I made one painting, and I did life drawing, but I am not any kind of spontaneous expressionist. So I came to media that allowed me to move things around and recombine them, and vector graphics is perfect for that because you can draw a circle, you can squash it, you can rotate it, you can offset it, you can cut it out with another one, you can glue it to another one, it’s a very plastic medium, more like working with clay than with rock.

I also gained an understanding of colour that became so deeply enculturated so quickly that one of my tutors asked me if I was colourblind. I wasn’t, I was just using really horrible yellow, green, and blue photocopier paper to make reference to the world of office life, just like I was taking colour palettes from graphic design colour palette books and Pantone flipbooks and from sweet wrappers and cereal boxes—from everywhere except art.

MBK  You were using colour and shape as sign systems?

RM  Yes. And I was already doing series at that point: the first defensible series that I did was called Mixes. It involved taking canonical paintings and combining them using morphing software. What I emphasised at the time was the interplay between the compositions of the images and between the gazes of artists with different sexualities. There’s a Bacon cricketer and a Kitaj naked woman smoking, one with a tree by Gilbert and George and a Francis Bacon George piece, and another one which is Gilbert or George in a suit blended with a Warhol Cowboy Elvis. The combination of them in the morphing software looks like a Pierrot, and it’s this wonderful dive into male gender presentation—although I wouldn’t have had that language available to me at the time.

 

Rhea Myers, Mixes, 1993. Digital images.

Then I did a lot of text calligraphy 3d work, laboriously dragging computer type over freehand drawings and then photographs, getting into the computer room at 8am and leaving at 8pm because in those days, when you dragged that many bits of text around in perspective in Corel Draw, you could make at least one cup of tea waiting for it to render every time you moved it.

This was an early instance of one of the strategies that I stole from Art & Language, who stole it from Ad Reinhardt, which is disobedience-in-obedience, or work to rule. Taking a demand that someone has made, obeying it to the letter, and horribly disappointing the person who made the demand because that’s not what they meant. It’s like the meme format Hurt me! No, not like that! Clement Greenberg said that you should expunge all outside content from art; so why not just paint a black square? No, not like that! Or, for me, the Cultural Studies department had said the world was a text, so why not make images of a world that is entirely made out of text, and of course the response was, No, not like that! It’s a wonderful strategy for critical engagement because, firstly, you get to troll people, but secondly, you get to be creative and you get to do hermeneutics or interpretation or parody, which is far less boring and more generative than simply saying, No, you’re wrong, here’s a better solution.

 

Rhea Myers, PostScript Virus, 1993. Computer code (image shows before/after).

During this period I also almost invented the Word macro virus. I didn’t, because we didn’t have the Word macro manuals, but I wanted to make a virus that would replace the word ‘postmodernism’ with something random. I did make a virus written in Adobe PostScript, a language designed to describe page layouts for print (PostScript Virus [1993]). The virus would have infected PostScript printers and made all of the lovely graphic design sent to the printer just a bit more wobbly. I had the payload and an attempt at the replicator…but I thought, I probably don’t want to do this, I don’t know if it’s actually technically possible or not but if it is I don’t want to find out…. It worked by redefining how the PostScript interpreter itself works. And the fact that code can interpret things and can do things visually was interesting to me. At the time people were obsessed with the idea of software as text, but for me, the design of the Macintosh, that visual encounter with software, with a standardized GUI, was very compelling.

Rhea Myers, ‘Arching’, 1994, from Blobs. Digital vector image.

Eventually, when I got fed up with spending twelve hours a day in the computer room, I fell in love, had my first serious relationship, and the joy from that went into the more graphically wibbly-wobbly works that make up Blobs (1994). Again, these were all done using vector graphics. Each of them required a very keen visual eye, but none of it required great hand-eye coordination. What I was looking for in vector graphics was a medium, meaning something with both affordances that direct you, and limitations that you have to work around, and which comes with a theory and a history. That’s what’s happening in Blobs: the history I got from graphic design, from The Designers Republic, from the cereal packets and candy wrappers and so on. And there’s a lot of play with the form going on, to the extent that these are just about sensuality and joy, but they’re about that sensuality and joy in a way that’s technically mediated and is very contemporary to that era. There’s nothing spiritual to it, and there’s nothing political except to the extent that just doing this is political.

MBK  You mentioned Julian Opie, whose work is often interested in how much you can graphically pare down aesthetic means while retaining some kind of seductive or sensual allure.

RM  Yes, it’s curious because on the internet I play someone who hates dialectics, but all the work I like is dialectical, and my own work—don’t tell anyone—is also generally deeply dialectical. In Blobs you have a cold commercial technology being made to use warm, sexy (in my conception of it at the time) art. The Blobs are bodies, they’re ecstatic maps of making out, and they are free objects—each has its own shadow. There is no God. The light of the fact. But they exist within an indexical environment of certain technical problems within the production and history of art and carry these very impersonal references.

Opie is fascinating because the painted steel sculptures he started with are a sort of ironisation of turn-of-the-80s Frank Stella. Where Stella painted abstract sculptures, Opie used the same technique with representational imagery in a retro clip-art style. I simply adore Opie’s concrete Scalextric sets (Imagine You Are Driving [1993]), toy-like in their scale and reduction of complexity and in their educational, imaginative reflective purpose. They give you something to look at. They’re not a text stapled to the wall or a blank canvas, they are something that has presence, virtuality. His air conditioners from the late 80s, I believe, although I have no evidence for this, come from a comment David Hockney made about minimal art, which was that you might as well look at the air conditioning unit.

Julian Opie, K., 1987. Aluminium, stainless steel, foam, PVC, wood, 96.5 x 219 x 28 cm (38 x 86 1/4 x 11 in), OPIE870008. © Julian Opie; courtesy Lisson Gallery. Photograph: Gareth Winters.

MBK  Didn’t he steal that from Columbo? In the episode ‘Playback’ (1975), Columbo’s in a gallery in LA sniffing around. In the line of duty, he’s trying to enter into the modern art ethos, and mistakenly starts ‘appreciating’ the air conditioning unit. ‘Um, now, this here…I see it doesn’t have a title…’!

RM  Those Opie works put the visual to work in the way that—not to say that art should, but that art tends to, whether it’s persuading you to love Christ, to join the revolution, or to really enjoy getting on the train out of Paris for the weekend. The essentially propagandising function of art is a result of its visual seductiveness. Adorno says that to depict violence is to aestheticise it, which is entirely correct, but doesn’t go far enough, because to aestheticise something can be morally obscene, and the deployment of moral obscenity merely symbolically within art can be a very powerful move. Sometimes the only way you can get to sympathy is through disgust. I’m all about these lateral moves. But yeah, what is the point of visual art you don’t want to look at?

MBK  So although you were already engaged with Conceptualism in its most stringent form, you didn’t have the hostility or suspicion of the visual that can imply.

RM  No, and I still think design has a better claim to the craft of imagemaking qua imagemaking than contemporary art does. If you want a well-painted, well-drawn, well-proportioned image, go to design or comics or illustration or movies—anywhere other than the gallery, where the zombie formalisms are skulking in the hope that they can gather enough presence to be considered significant despite not signifying.

There’s a lovely Art & Language quote they’ve been trying to walk back for decades, which goes something like, ‘Some of the paintings seemed better than others, it seemed to us that this was somehow a product of the making of them’. This is about a series of their own paintings, and they tried to clarify that, of course, they were not saying that any skill or imagination went into them…no, too late!

In grappling with this kind of thing, I decided that you can’t do either reason or emotion alone. I’d seen a documentary featuring someone who had a brain injury which left them incapable of emotion, and as a result they couldn’t make a decision because they would never get bored of reasoning about it. Where should we go for dinner? Well, we could go to the fish restaurant but I like the wine at the Italian place but then we could…. They had no way of culling the search tree, because they had a faculty of pure logical search unconstrained by affect. So I realised that it’s ineffective to pretend not to rely on visual impact and the affect it creates. Often it’s ‘logical’ to use affect and indeed to be illogical, because sometimes you need to jump to a different line of flight from any you can think of directly. I wanted to make posters, things for people to have in their mental space. I didn’t have the kind of residual valuing of high culture that you find in the historical-materialist project-based discursive-but-not-illustrative work of Art & Language and Opie. I found something very interesting in the more productive and playful and, to be honest, queer art of Warhol and Koons.

MBK  You then moved on to the Centre for Electronic Arts at Middlesex University. The courses offered there were quite pioneering.

RM  Oh, absolutely. An institution I have described as throwing humanities students at computer programming for a year, and having the survivors put on a show. I got there in ’95 and there was the feeling of twenty years of history to the place already. There was a dead old Silicon Graphics box in the hall, there was a PICASO software manual next to a Macintosh attached to a video dumping device. We had lectures from people who’d founded the Computer Arts Society, people in the then-current new media art scene, and people who actually knew how to teach people like me maths and programming. So it was an absolutely amazing marriage between the technical knowledge that you needed to make a computer beep the way you wanted it to, and a sense of history that led you to feel that this was something that could be informed by and respond to precedent. And you were also situated within contemporary culture because you had people explaining the early social effects of the internet, or how virtual reality related to feminism, or whatever. So yeah, it was Camelot.

MBK  Did you have access to whatever constituted cutting-edge hardware and software at the time?

RM  No, we didn’t have access to the best computers, we had some little pre-PowerMacs. But if you were very nice to the Music Department they’d let you use their NeXT boxes, and if you were very nice to the Interactive Multimedia Department they’d let you use one of their ageing Silicon Graphics boxes or their PCs. I got into an ongoing battle with some Interactive Media students, getting there earlier and earlier in the morning to use the PCs.

MBK  This would have been during the period of the explosion of cyberculture too, and a growing general awareness of computing technology and the internet. How aware were you of that, and of the differences between the mainstream US Wired version of cyberculture and the more idiosyncratic and abrasive UK take on cyberculture by groups like Mute Magazine, Ccru, Orphan Drift, and so on?

RM  During this period, between 1995 and 2000, to be honest I encountered cyberculture and didn’t understand it. I encountered net art and didn’t understand it. I encountered the web and didn’t understand it (you’ll notice a theme here). I’d got over my printmaking obsession, but I thought interactive multimedia was the future. PostScript Virus was only the first in a long history of me not taking the bit as far as it could go…but it was the closest I got to cyberculture at the time. For me, cyberpunk was the eighties, I thought that was all done with, passé. Cyberpunk? You might as well be reading westerns, what are you doing?! My partner at the time was the creative director at a virtual reality startup in Shoreditch, you literally could not be more mid-nineties. So I got to see VRML and virtual communities emerge, and I also went to a show [Ccru, Syzygy, Beaconsfield, 2000] which had some strange drawings on the wall, featuring this sort of bracket notation for numbers. I looked at it and I was like Just use hex! So I very much didn’t know what the next big thing was at the time. If I had a time machine it would be a toss-up between whether I go back to teenage me and go, Hey, there’s a reason why all of your RPG characters are female, or go back to twentysomething me and go, Walk on the earth, engage with what is actually going on, take a risk!

MBK  How advanced were you as a programmer? Was your knowledge of coding drawn purely from having to learn in order to make designs and prints?

RM  I had no formal programming education until I got to Middlesex. I’d learned BASIC from the Usborne books, I’d learned Forth and some Pascal purely out of interest. I was obsessed with LOGO but couldn’t get a copy of it to use. I knew that LISP was a thing, probably thanks to Usborne books too, but couldn’t make head nor tail of it. I learned a bit of C, I had a C compiler for my Mac. But the language that I went all in on was PostScript, because it was the language that graphic design was made in. As a language it’s most like Forth, it’s an absolutely fascinating language, and the environment in which it operates is so amazing, this platonic mathematical realm of lines and curves that maps into very real ink being squeezed between metal plates onto paper. I later got a job working at a prepress company programming Adobe Illustrator plug-ins based on the knowledge that I had gained of the PostScript rendering model.

MBK  PostScript is a language that defines the available range of vector and typographical objects, the relations between them, and the transforms that can be applied to them, all in the most economical form. So it lays out a kind of graphic ontology, right?

RM  Yes, it’s curious because it’s not like having a pattern book for textiles or a Vasari study of Roman architecture; it’s not really a set of models, it’s not quite a tool box, it’s not quite a palette, it’s this combination of knowledge and tool and affordance that’s sort of the technical equivalent of a community of practice. If UNIX is the religious literature of computer science, then PostScript is its illuminated manuscripts.

MBK  It seems like, even for this period, you went a lot deeper into programming than you strictly needed to in order to do digital printmaking.

RM  At one point in art school I was given the task of doing what in the 2000s was called a ‘mashup’. I was given photocopies of a Futurist cyclist and a Jackson Pollock drip painting and told to put those together. I looked at the small grey reproduction of the cyclist and I just couldn’t work it out. I had been given a material task that I could not perform owing to my lack of experience, knowledge, and resources. So I went to the library and there was a book of preparatory sketches by the artist: a drawing of someone on a bicycle, a drawing of someone on the bicycle with lines of force, because it’s Futurism, then that was abstracted a bit, then that abstraction reduced to a circle and a couple of triangles…and I then understood the forms I was working with. And in the same way, I learned programming because I wanted to understand. I knew that I wanted to make computer art, whatever that was, so I needed to know the structures underpinning it. That interest in access to knowledge and source material for very practical reasons fed directly into my interest in copyleft and free culture and Creative Commons.

MBK  Because you had direct experience of the fact that it mattered whether people had access not just to the end product, but to the knowledge of how things are made? And you were able to see how that need translated from art to programming—in both cases, to really understand and be productive you need access to the ‘source code’?

RM  Yes, in my case I would not have competently been able to do the task without access to those resources. The funny thing is that, after doing a Marinetti in the style of Jackson Pollock, I found out about Art & Language’s Portrait of V.I. Lenin with Cap, in the Style of Jackson Pollock (1980).

MBK  Coincidentally, this is exactly where I was going to go next, because in the essay that accompanies that painting, Art & Language pose their question about ‘ofness’, a question many of your works can be seen as responding to.5 What is a painting of? In one sense it’s a visual representation of something, but in another, genetic sense, it’s of all of the conditions—material, sociopolitical, etc.—that have made its particular visual mode and material construction possible. That question may require less forcing when working with computer images than with painting, because it was almost inevitable with early computing technology that you would produce images that weren’t of anything representationally, or only in a very rudimentary way. And so the question of what they’re of genetically becomes interesting if you’re reflecting on them in a way informed to some extent by art theory.

RM  You can render balls bouncing around the screen—and I did—but what does that touch on outside of itself? The genetic of is of great interest to me, and I love the history of computing, no matter how boring. When I was doing my degree I read the book about the making of the Macintosh, Insanely Great,6 and I’ve read so many others, including Showstopper!, the story of the making of Windows NT.7 One of the most depressing books ever, but it fed into Surgical Strike (1996).

Rhea Myers, Surgical Strike, 1996. Digital images.

Surgical Strike is another mashup, ripped very directly from Art & Language. It reinflects William Latham-style swirly blobby organic computer graphics with stealth bombers and facts from the history of the production of computing (influenced by Manuel De Landa’s War in the Age of Intelligent Machines, if I remember correctly) and then puts the source code for it all in front. The objects are procedurally generated by a language which consists of military terminology rather than computer science concepts.

But yes, this ofness, which a work can engage with in order to draw in references from outside the canvas, gives art an irreplaceable power to carry out operations that can include depiction, critique, endorsement, whatever you want—but basically, it gives art the ability to not stop at the frame. This seemed key to me. It might have been that I’d seen how much people hated computer art for its lack of historical necessity, or it might have been too many group crits, but I needed to ground my practice in something beyond the frame, something offscreen, however much that led to me diving deep into the technology itself.

MBK  So with PostScript, for example, you could say, yes, a given document has circles, Bézier curves, bits of type, but part of what it’s of is the entire structure of the software that is used to make it, the affordances of the language in which it’s written, and the design decisions behind those affordances, and so on…which is the approach you apply later to blockchain technologies.

RM  Sure, although originally the interest in design software came from The Designers Republic, from Emigré, from the fact that this contemporary imagery was so vivid to me.

MBK  I can see how none of this meshed straightforwardly with the cyberculture of the day. So what drew you into that world in the end?

RM  I left art school and realised I didn’t have the social skills to make a go of it as an artist in the nineties art world. So what got me into the subculture was hanging out in Shoreditch, which was sort of ground zero for cyberculture in London at that time. I encountered lots of stuff at the ICA, I encountered Cyberia. Later I got onto the Rhizome mailing list, I got onto Furtherfield’s Netbehaviour list. And then over time I became more and more involved with Furtherfield, appreciated what they were doing, realised that I’d missed out on a very interesting historical moment, and did my best to understand it and to appreciate what I’d missed.

Olia Lialina, My Boyfriend Came Back from the War, 1996, Browser-based internet work.

But the real missing link here is Olia Lialina’s work My Boyfriend Came Back from the War (1996), which was a browser-based piece of art. I looked at it and thought, Oh, this is interesting. Twenty minutes later I was like, Okay this is really interesting. It was an experience with a very particular feel, a very particular aesthetic, you couldn’t have had that encounter and been drawn in by it in the same way in any other medium. If you were interacting with interactive multimedia in a gallery you’d be primed for something, if you had a CD-ROM and shoved it into your Mac, again, you’ve made a certain investment in what you expect to encounter. Being on the internet, following a link and just being there, this is a different way of encountering art. So that opened things up for me conceptually.

MBK  A largely visual but potentially multimodal medium, the browser, was increasingly becoming indispensable in every part of everyday life—a life lived through media that, as technology develops, becomes increasingly immediate.

RM  Precisely, it is mediated, mediatised, by a generic medium—computing information technology. As we have found, you can shove anything down a fat enough pipe. And even in the Netscape era it was obvious that what we had in the web was a singular medium in which text, sound, video, writing, conversation, and money were all brought together. Money didn’t quite fit yet because the government was determined that it wouldn’t. But that was coming.

And at the time you could work as a graphic designer in Shoreditch making websites for three weeks and make enough money to spend a week making art, and that was what people did. People were working to organise public access to the internet as a social project. You had internet labs helping people to get online. The dream of mass networking was starting to spring up and it just felt like this moment of possibility where people knew that they could both change the world and afford to do so, maybe even get rich by doing so. I described Furtherfield in the 2000s as a cultural embassy from a better world. Having that living model of people who are running a real artist’s space that was well ahead of the game by any measure, it was really inspiring.

MBK  Was it at this point that your interest shifted from interactive multimedia to an awareness that you could work with networked technology as a medium?

RM  I made precisely one piece of net art in the nineties, for a show by SoDA, a brilliant Middlesex University-derived tech art company. They were doing a show, so I made some YBA art generators as Java applets. There was a Hirst spot generator—and you’ll notice that I’ve continued making spot generators since then—there was a hospital door generator because Gary Hume was big then, and a couple of other things. They were very much in the hacker culture/Usenet culture tradition of making buzzword generator parodies. In doing that for visual art I was in effect saying, These spots aren’t worth anything, make as many as you like, a technique I’ve revisited a number of times. But we couldn’t actually get them showing on the woefully underpowered Macs that we had for the show, so, entirely by accident, I was one of the first people to exhibit a URL in a gallery! That was the point the installation team got to, and I was like yeah, sure, go for it: another time they also printed out a listing of the code and exhibited that. So I was trying to do buzzword-generator-style net art but I ended up as a conceptualist by default, in my absence!

I spent a few years on some unrealised projects that involved me trying to avoid being the kind of performance art shaman that I thought I didn’t want to be. I did get a tri-field electromagnetic field meter and go to a gallery searching for the aura of art—I thought I’d found it but a statistical regression indicates that I was just kidding myself. In the end I couldn’t genuinely be enthusiastic about doing this sort of thing. No, it’s got to be visual, I’m not being paid for this so I should at least get some visual joy from it.

After Surgical Strike there were a couple of years at the end of the nineties when I pretty much stopped making art. My creative output consisted in porting the Gwydion Dylan compiler to classic MacOS as a historical intervention in the history of computing after Apple cancelled their Dylan project. It got to the point where I was at Apple’s developer conference in California for Douglas Adams’s The Digital Village game company, and I was fielding emails from the company that did the one remaining commercial Dylan compiler, trying to open-source their user interface toolkit. So I went pretty deep into that community. I do tend to—I don’t know what the politically correct anthropological term for ‘going native’ is—I do tend to dance with wolves.

MBK  But presumably it would have been already evident to you at that point that, if you were looking to have an appreciable impact on the way in which people went about their lives and saw the world, intervening in software would be a more reliable means than making art?

RM  Well, there was no hope that everyone was going to start using Dylan. But yes, I had already realised that, and that was where PostScript Virus came from, I wanted to intervene in everyone’s everyday experience of aesthetics critically.

It’s tricky though, because there’s such a gap between what software developers see and what everyone else sees. One of the good things about Richard Stallman’s ideology is his absolutely resolute refusal to differentiate between users of computers who are producers and those who are consumers. That was hopelessly idealistic even when he was doing it, and it’s become no less so since, but yes, we experience so much through computers that changing what computers do is a surefire way of getting things out into the world.

MBK  You’ve talked about how you always felt it was important to understand, to know the recipes: as an artist, if software of some kind is the medium, that requires an engagement with and preferably a mastery of that plastic medium. The vast majority of people engage with the screen without understanding what’s going on beneath the surface, though. Would those that know be justified in holding that consumerist passivity in contempt?

RM  Nerd supremacism slips into honest-to-goodness fascism far too easily. I’m someone who knows how to program a computer, and I’m deeply relaxed about you not needing to know how to do that. Honestly, if you want to engage critically with the technology, what you need is just some degree of technical and cultural literacy, whether it’s from training or street smarts. I don’t care where it comes from, just as long as you know what you’re talking about. (I may have had different opinions about this in the past….)

The sense of superiority can go the other way too. All of the concerned calls to ‘teach nerds ethics’—why not teach venture capitalists ethics, teach managers ethics?! Why are you, my status-anxious humanities bro, so obsessed with the idea that if only software developers understood that killing people is bad, software would be different? It’s almost like you’re aiming for the wrong part of the body technologic, for reasons that are at least as interesting sociologically and psychologically as the aims you claim to be trying to achieve by doing so….

And in fact, even though I’ve accidentally become a very good smart contract programmer, I didn’t mean to do that. It was an accidental side effect—but I always do this, I always learn an obscure language for history of ideas or cultural history of computing reasons and then it turns out to be the next big thing. I learned Objective C in the nineties because I was fascinated by NeXT, Steve Jobs’s big failed project after Apple. Then we got to 2001 and he tells us that they programmed MacOS in Objective C, and then the iPhone comes out, and suddenly everyone wants Objective C programmers. With programming Ethereum, I thought, this will be interesting to do a couple of projects in, and then suddenly everyone wants Ethereum programmers. There were a couple of other examples in-between. I’ve had the privilege, obviously, to be able to do that, but as someone who is not going to outcompete the world’s best C++ programmer, I’m very lucky that that’s not my ambition. I’m drawn to languages more for how amusing they are than for any claims of efficiency they might make.

As with absolutely everything I do, I don’t suddenly have genius original ideas out of nowhere, it’s always that I remember something from earlier in my life, or that I didn’t understand something the last time I saw it, or that I want to do this thing so I’ll try and learn how to do it and maybe I’ll be able to use it later. It’s all fantastically contingent.

MBK  You mentioned wanting your works to touch on or draw in something from outside of themselves. A part of what I see in your works is a continuation of the question of the artwork’s autonomy that was central to site-specific and institutional critique: the enterprise of making artworks that refuse to be autonomous self-contained objects transportable from one gallery to another, and instead manifest the conditions and environment of their production and exhibition—an idea allegorised physically in Hans Haacke’s Condensation Cubes (1963–1968).

RM  In the late sixties cybernetics was being received in a popular way by the same public who would later become interested in environmental concerns, precisely because they were primed to understand feedback systems. I’m not trying to say that RAND were actually hippies, I’m saying that after cybernetics escaped the lab, it was received by political movements in a way that we cannot imagine today. But once opposition to the Vietnam war became the key focus for many of them, cybernetics became unimaginable to them as well, which is why information-based and cybernetic art disappears like a vampire in sunlight about a year before I was born. But Haacke is very much in this milieu, and the thing about Haacke’s institutional critique is that it is genuinely uncomfortable for the institution. You do not want a study of the slumlords of New York in your gallery as the nice polite property-owning class file in. There has to be that degree of discomfort to achieve successful institutional critique, but it can’t be so much of a discomfort that it won’t get shown. Haacke had the historical advantage of institutions not yet having worked this out. The end of that sort of institutional critique was when museums realised that they didn’t have to put up with this, that artists can actually be told, yeah you’re a genius but we don’t have to show this work. You can always aestheticise any ethical move of resistance: ‘They’re selling hippie wigs in Woolworths, man.’

MBK  Just as Conceptual Art was wrongfooted by its documents being aestheticised as exhibitable works.

RM  Yes, I forget who said it, but if you point your finger at the moon, the dog just looks at your finger. There is a lot of this in the reception of Conceptual Art. And with institutional critique, once institutions worked out that they could have the aesthetic of critique with an ethic of affirmation—because institutional critique affirms these sites as powerful—Yeah, tell me more about how awful and powerful I am, it’s terrible, stop, no stop, no go on…—that historical moment gives way to a candy shell of critique with no actual payload.

But Conceptual Art does also critique the ideology of art criticism at the time of the emergence of information technology. Upon arriving in Vancouver in 2013 I discovered N.E. Thing Co., some Vancouverites who had a teletype in the late 1960s and would spam companies with artworks via the telex network. They sent out the first networked call for a networked art show over the teletype network around 1970. This engagement with what now we don’t see as information technology but which absolutely was at the time—the typewriter, the teletype, the photostat, the photocopier, microfiches, hot metal type printing, whatever—sought to problematise both that information technology and the idea of a purely visual unproblematic aesthetic art of pure sensation which you can consume without remainder or problem upon walking into the white cube.

N.E. Thing Co., Art is All Over, 1971. Collage on paper. Collection of the Morris and Helen Belkin Art Gallery, University of British Columbia, gift of Iain Baxter& and Ingrid Baxter, 1995. Photo: Michael R. Barrick.

Of course, institutions love site-specific institutional critique now. The horror of that kind of recuperation is something I priced in from the start. But if we think back to that landmark Haacke show [Shapolsky et al., 1971], I’m absolutely ripping off that technique of saying to the art world, You may actually want to think about where your money comes from or about your aesthetic of democracy and the problems underlying it, while also saying to the people producing blockchain technology, What if this works? What if this works and it has consequences, should we maybe look at those in an arena which doesn’t risk starving or freezing people or preventing them from getting their meds? Can we make use of the realm of ideas, of art, play there a while, and learn what we should not do before it has an actual impact? Treating it as a plastic medium for an art that is meant to be interrogative and uncomfortable both for the art world and for the blockchain technology world, that’s a strategy taken very directly from Conceptual Art.

Hans Haacke, Shapolsky et al. Manhattan Real Estate Holdings, a Real-Time Social System, as of May 1, 1971, 1971 (detail). 9 photostats, 142 gelatin silver prints, and 142 photocopies, dimensions variable. © Hans Haacke / DACS, digital image courtesy Whitney Museum of American Art / Licensed by Scala.

So yeah, it’s focusing on an emerging technology which provides an attentional figure to the ideological and political and economic grounds that people are then able to see it against.

With Conceptualism, you had the first generation of artists who had modern art-school, degree-style training. It was the Cold War, it was consumer society, Greenberg had run out of steam but no one was really replacing him. Smearing some colours on a canvas just didn’t seem up to it. The work of art could no longer do the work of art. So either you stop doing the work of art but continue making art, or you stop making art and continue doing the work of art. Art & Language and a few others chose to just keep going without a map, but with the intuition that it was politically or socially important to keep going. This led to either intentionally or unintentionally market-resistant forms of art. Art & Language talk with great amusement about galleries that would say they wanted to show their work, and would then end up sticking a copy of Art-Language up in a frame. An understandable mistake, but no. And then for their own internal pressures, needing to organise the conversations into the indexing projects, and then later on in the eighties needing to feed their families and having to come up with a sustained project of painting. But that initial ‘We will not be constrained by the history of art because we wish to continue the history of art’ is a fascinating move, and it is very much a move of freedom however one looks at it. This exit from the rump of the post-Greenbergian art world was the creation of an imaginative world in which they could keep alive the possibility of resistance. That’s different from the dreamwork that I criticise, which is that of people simply resolving the psychological tensions of working against what they claim to work for every hour of every day in their day job.

Certificate of Inauthenticity (2020) was originally a Sol LeWitt certificate of authenticity and some Tipp-Ex. LeWitt’s certificates took unsaleable wall drawings and turned them into very saleable contracts and signed certificates. I took this aesthetic and conceptual reference and used it to capture what I felt was going on with the anxiety of authenticity in the actually existing blockchain art world at that time. That piece and Tokens Equals Text I’m fantastically happy with in terms of the way they draw in their references and compose them in order to be usefully attention-directing and critical, not in the sense of self-aggrandising gestures of dismissal but in the sense of, we need to understand x, extend y, and head off z. The ability to do that, to the extent that I can, comes from working through that model of exit, of not being constrained by the expectation of exhibitability or saleability or any of the other things which I would be much richer had I been concerned with from day one.

MBK  Ultimately, then, are you happy with being described as a conceptual artist?

RM  Kind of, yes. As Art & Language say, ‘Whatever the destiny of Conceptual Art was, it wasn’t Conceptual Art’. I feel quite awkward when people say that I’m a ‘blockchain conceptual artist’. I needed this sort of visual and technical and art-historical and cultural resource of signification and association in order to communicate something which, if I’d have come up with a novel form of representation for it, I’d have had three strikes against the resulting art: a technology that nobody really needs to understand, an ideology that nobody needs to understand, and a critique or consideration of these in a representational media that nobody understands. At that point I’m just screaming into the void. So what I do does look like extremely late postmodernism or extremely late conceptualism or, in the worst case, extremely late pop, but it’s more to do with a sort of cultural smog of the internet-era shift from successive movements to parallel genres, where everyone has the history of culture available at their fingertips, and therefore I can get a leg up by using people’s knowledge to put out signals that will make them look at something. It isn’t first-order Conceptual Art, just as Art & Language’s work already wasn’t. It does the Conceptual Art thing as part of its operation because that is the most effective way of engaging with what I wanted to engage with.

In early crypto art, seeing the retreat from the art market and the attempt to create an alternative economy and life world, I recognised that from Conceptualism. And my other reference was the net art era, where artists had got into this media of communication exchange and exploitation first: the net artist got there before massive corporate exploitation and made it so that, from the get-go, you couldn’t just go on the internet and unproblematically consume ‘content’ without an alternative. They had a shared interest in the use of communications technologies. Glueing those two things together and recognising the idealism from the cyberculture moment in London, I was ready to, and had a desire to, demonstrate to the art world that there was something of critical and possibly organisational interest to the blockchain, and to try to explain to the blockchain people that there was something of organisational and possibly technological interest in the art world. So I’ve been trying to rub these two groups together to produce weird mutant offspring, to generate comprehension. What I’m doing is this double-A-side of critique of the institution of art and critique of the institution of technology and of the ideology of both.

MBK  As it develops and intervenes in or substitutes itself for social relations, technology disassembles, analyses, and in effect critiques the social fabric, in the sense of disabusing it of claims to transcendence and reproducing its operations immanently as machine parts. In doing so, at the same time it also unbundles concepts, and your art and writing often hinges on demonstrating how blockchain technologies and crypto separate elements of social relationships that we thought belonged together by definition: property, ownership, unique reference to something and disclosure of its content, authenticity and authentication, social trust and the social construction of value, provenance and third-party authorities. All of these are things that you use the work to drive a wedge in between, or to show how blockchain technology is in fact already doing so.

RM  This is what I refer to as ’delamination’. You can’t say that these things definitely go together now. Blockchain technology does accelerate the process of decoding, disrupting how these things work and how we assume they cluster together. And you can emphasise different bits of the cluster to get different takes on the technology, which make strange the phenomena we choose to try and capture or represent.

The interesting thing for the blockchain ‘event’ (pace Badiou), the publication of the Bitcoin White Paper, the creation of the Bitcoin software, is that it is very much an assemblage—it’s a copying and pasting together of existing technologies, which it places together in a unique new way to solve the problem in the cypherpunk political project. A lot of what we talk about when we talk about Bitcoin is surplus value of that code: the White Paper doesn’t say blockchain, it doesn’t say cryptocurrency. The White Paper talks about making a ‘timestamp server’, that is its technical contribution.

But as I argue in ’Blockchain Poetics’, what the White Paper specifies is a combination of very finely tuned different capabilities. If you emphasise one or the other of these capabilities more or less, you get something different: for instance, you just get distributed ledger technology because you don’t care about the ability to enforce the rarity of the currency. Or, if you emphasise the solution to the double-spending problem, which is the problem of the fake in art, if you lean into that, then you get NFTs. If you read the White Paper, you can see how each paragraph that Satoshi writes on the way to this timestamp server has so much surplus value, it weaves in so much possibility, so many affordances.

MBK  Like an engine that you can tune in different ways to get it to perform differently?

RM  Yes, and my only argument to the Left really is, for God’s sake, understand that engine, otherwise you will get played.

Nick Land talks about it in terms of subtraction. Bitcoin subtracts the need for a trusted third party. Money as previously understood is something issued by a trusted party that we can then exchange, to the degree that they allow us to. If we subtract that trusted third party then in theory we shouldn’t have money. The fact that Satoshi, to a greater or lesser degree, successfully does so, performs the operation of critique.8

MBK  In the sense that it demonstrates that the immanent operation of money is not necessarily dependent on a transcendent instance, on something that stands above it and is claimed to be the condition for its functioning.

RM  Land’s relating of the technology of Bitcoin to such philosophical problems, and to his own political projects, and the fact that you can do that, shows what an interesting object Bitcoin is. It is a screen for the projection of hopes and fears, it does fit into the history of ideas, it does have a design aesthetic, and the problems that it tackles have their analogues in the problems that the market introduces into art, problems which ultimately are simply a projection of the patriarchal anxiety of primogeniture: the double-spending problem is simply Is this my child? Just as, in the art market, the fake is simply: Have I continued my line or have I been cuckolded? These are the anxieties attached to patriarchal society, a society which tries to find answers to them through vast amounts of money and/or power and/or violence, in order to reassure the male reproductive subject that actually, yes, he is secure: this security system for humanity conceived of as Man will look after you and make sure that what you are entitled to has not been stolen from you, that your offspring have not been right-clicked.

MBK  But isn’t the timestamp server a way of securing provenance?

RM  Absolutely, it operationalises post hoc ergo propter hoc, it produces a universe in which one thing follows another and you know that is true. You cannot spend something twice, which means you cannot spend it out of order, which means that if you are receiving currency then, over time, your certainty as to the security of your possession of it tends to grow. Now, to be clear, if the Vogons arrived tomorrow and had 278 trillion hashes of power, they could very quickly rework the order of transactions and exclude blocks. But they still couldn’t spend anyone else’s coins—unless they have a solution to the discrete log problem, something which we currently have our fingers crossed doesn’t exist.

MBK  How about property, what happens to the concept of property here?

RM  Land makes the argument that property didn’t truly exist before public key encryption: public key encryption is the thing that makes it possible for there to even be private ownership or control proper, with control of resources conceived as ownership in the absence of a state guarantor that can revoke that right.
