MBK  That’s the move you make at the end of ‘Recanting Quasiproperty’, saying that whereas in earlier texts you had protested against the idea that Bitcoin is property, in fact what we used to call property was a kind of contaminated form of property, which Bitcoin distils into its pure form. But this does bring us back to the question of how this absolutisation of property could be some kind of a leftist project.

RM  There are two things here: firstly, if you want to have any kind of critical purchase on society you need to understand what you are trying to critique. And the other thing is that it’s here, so let’s at least understand what can be done with it. And how we can ironise it or extend it or judo-throw it into something we can make use of.

MBK  ‘We don’t yet know what property can do’?

RM  One of the arguments I make is that property regimes are generative. It’s not the case that certain entities come into being and we then create a property regime to describe them. We create a property regime and then this generates, in a very real way, the object to which it applies. It may be that there are a few initial exemplary or liminal objects that it’s intended to cover for some special interest or other. But once it becomes possible to describe things that were not previously describable as property, people will track that singularity and it will start to generate these objects—like a market Stalinist executive demanding that you measure particular metrics….

MBK  I think that’s what Deleuze and Guattari, reading Marx through the psychotic Judge Schreber, call ‘miraculation’:9 in every type of society there’s a recording surface upon which social acts and the organisation of bodies and the earth are inscribed (for us, money), and eventually it seems as if everything is emanating from that recording surface, and its markings become the ‘quasi-cause’ rather than the effect of everything that happens in the social world—it naturalises itself.

RM  Yeah. Depending on how you rotate the time crystal, this is autopoesis. The hyperstition of property creating itself. Here you have a blockchain timestamp server producing this Deleuzian empty repetition of time—every block is the same, block after block, every ten minutes. You have festivals, you have halvings of block rewards for example, and hard forks. But at the block level there are no seasons, there are no birthdays, there are no jubilees. This sort of very capitalist linear clocktime feeds very nicely into Anna Greenspan’s idea of capitalism’s transcendental time machine,10 because this quantisation, this meting out of time, once again, is generative.

MBK  It is linear and empty time in one sense, but it’s also not, because every block ‘contains’ every other block that’s gone before it, so there’s a kind of continual intensification.

RM  Yes, the being of blockchain is intensified with each block, each block makes it more of a thing, it would take more work to undo it‚ to rewrite its history or to destroy it or to reallocate the value within it, everything becomes more difficult to rework over time. This is the advantage of proof of work: it would take an appreciable chunk of the earth’s computing power to rewrite even a fraction of Bitcoin’s blockchain and you still wouldn’t be able to spend other people’s coins. You could prevent them ever having existed but you couldn’t send them to someone else and pretend that they sent them. So the existence of the blockchain intensifies, and certainly the amount of value within each block can be said to be more or less intense if we treat monetary value here as a pure intensive quantity. We are however in a smooth space: there’s nothing within the Merkle tree of the block which says, this block has this hash, because that’s impossible. There is an inside and an outside, an onchain and an offchain.

Certainly, in one sense all the information that you need to understand the blockchain is onchain, but then to interact with it you need the private keys, which are offchain and must not be onchain otherwise you can break the system. Thinking about on- and offchain in terms of natural/supernatural or phenomenon/noumenon is fascinating—it’s trivial, you know, it’s a card trick in philosophical terms, but it’s kind of evocative and it’s one of those things which, if you press on it, helps to unpack assumptions. You have this inside world of transactions with an ontology enforced mathematically, and then offchain you have this wild domain that nothing onchain can know of, except as cryptographic signatures transmitted from it.

And again this can be a useful irritant, part of disobedience-in-obedience. We must have a realm of pure mathematical understanding and reason, and this can then bring justice to our messy society. Hey, here’s a blockchain! No, not like that! Okay, but then in what way? Just…not like that!

MBK  Two elements that blockchain brings together in a somewhat paradoxical manner are fungible currency and the concept of a ledger in which information is retained forever—they seem to run counter to one another, because the key thing about fungible money is that, once you’ve done whatever you do and received payment for it, that money is just as good as anybody else’s, no one knows where it came from and it doesn’t carry any information beyond its exchange value. Pecunia non olet. In a sense, money, or cash at least, is precisely a machine to wash information away, so how does that fit with a ledger that is transparent, public, and perpetual?

RM  Right—to quote Barbara Kruger, ‘Money is an information-destroying medium’, which is the point of it. In the third part of Liu Cixin’s Dark Forest trilogy, where the solar system is being collapsed into the second dimension—which freaked me out for days after reading it—that dimensional reduction, the image of Jupiter leaching out into the second dimension like an ice cream on a pavement on a hot day, that’s money.

Firstly, Bitcoin is not anonymous money, so it doesn’t quite solve the cypherpunk requirement. It is pseudonymous money: you can tell which address has carried out which transaction. Satoshi said to use one address for each transaction, do not reuse addresses. There are good cryptographic key safety reasons for doing so. But also, doing so allows us to hold on to the early internet pseudonymity of not having a tied-down singular identity. So if we were using bitcoin as Satoshi told us to, then you would have this sort of pseudonymous thicket of transactions which you can absolutely see, but which would not be tied to singular addresses, and you would just need to make sure that you do not leak that this address is actually you.

The public ledger is a means to an end of enforcing transaction order; the point of Bitcoin is to produce the transaction server, not the ledger as such. Satoshi said that for efficiency of space reasons, once you have spent all of a transaction’s outputs you don’t need it anymore, you don’t need to validate past that point, and you can dispose of that information. So keeping this information and making it available in block explorers is absolutely something that Bitcoin as a technology doesn’t prevent, but it’s not an intended outcome of it.

There are two reactions to this at the level of technology. The first is something like Web3 and NFT provenance, where you say, that’s fine, forget about these multiple pseudonymous identities, normies aren’t going to be able to manage those, let’s just have a singular identity, we can register that with state ID services to give people safety, and then people can send money and report on their taxes, and they can know who they are buying their NFT from and who’s minted it, and so on. So that leans into the identity part of it and the public ledger part of it as a social good, and it’s where the provenance argument finds its strongest home. But we could equally go the other way and say what Satoshi did was brilliant, but this is pseudonymous not anonymous money and we wanted anonymous money. So we can take this timestamp server technology and make the addresses and the ledger—which come out of Bitcoin but are not deliberately there as things in themselves—we can make these actually secret using zero-knowledge proof technology. Then you get secret transaction-capable chains such as Zcash or Monero, and on these you do not have to have public provenance, although it’s perfectly possible to create zero-knowledge provenance systems tokens—but for secret transactions you cannot tell where a transaction has come from or how much value is involved or where it has gone to by looking at the publicly broadcast information or by recovering the history of the blockchain.

So Satoshi knew about the problem with pseudonymity and suggested ways to cope with it, and didn’t regard the public ledger as a value of Bitcoin beyond the way it serviced the timestamp server. And this is a great example: as with everything in Bitcoin, there’s enough going on here that we can tweak each of these variables in different ways and get different outcomes.

MBK  And in your work each of these tuneable parameters maps onto some dimension of the history of art’s interaction with money?

RM  Art, ideas, and politics. For me it’s a very rich index of concepts. It resonates, it opens out onto something else, and in a sense that’s bound to be the case because it’s unbundling the concept of property and the concept of property is intrinsic to the way in which art has been understood and the way in which the art world has worked.

I’m a big fan of the argument that society was created to produce art rather than that art was a product of society. And art has not always been property. Cave art is art despite obviously predating the concept of art, and being owned only in the sense that it was within the territory of a group of hominids. As soon as you get to the first wood panel painting with egg tempera, and then to canvas painting proper, you have a commodity form of art. Before that, the Sistine Chapel ceiling is certainly the product of commerce and exchange, but transferring ownership of it would be tricky. Whereas some coloured creams on a bit of hemp, now that is something that can be put in a crate and shipped.

MBK  Which is the beginning of the autonomy of the art object which, in the twentieth century, emerges as a problem at the same time as something called ‘art’ turns up whose being is notionally separate from its existence as an object in the social and commercial world.

RM  As something that exists in the commodity form, yes. And the commodity form interests me because the answer to the question of what we do about the commodity form differentiates left from right anarchism, anarcho-communists from anarcho-capitalists. The latter believe that the commodity form is vital to personal wealth and freedom and that it can be maintained in the absence of the violence of the state, while anarcho-communists believe that the defence of the commodity form, the policing of it, is a major driver of and is only possible with the violence or an equivalent to the violence of the state. So if you abolish the state you abolish the commodity form. I’m simplifying of course, but both of these are wrong in interesting ways. The left anarchist needs state-level violence to prevent the commodity form reemerging; the anarcho-capitalist needs state-level violence to prevent the commodity form ceasing to be. And so you’re either reproducing state violence as something else—which is a critique of the blockchain as a sort of property regime—or you’re assuming that people aren’t going to restart capitalism in their basement. And that’s really a terrible bet because as soon as someone can afford to pay two people to beat you up, they have the ability to reinforce the existence of commodities.

Art is always an important reflection of the property regime of its time. When people own and profit from commodities, art becomes a commodity. The content of art also ties into the content of those property regimes. Perspective is Renaissance trade maths, Impressionism is French state-sponsored colour theory plus railways…. As property becomes digital and as money becomes digital, as the state tries to get us to stop using cash and just submit our transactions to its prying eyes to make sure we’re not doing anything naughty, cryptocurrency may look like just more of the same. But in fact it’s the dark forest thing of going into electronic currency before the state can force you in there as a means of escape.

So there’s that aspect to it, but I feel very strongly that art also has this affordance of symbolic resolution or closure. If you know that the apocalypse was meant to happen last Wednesday and it didn’t, you can get an artist to paint it on the wall so it’s there. If you’re an amazingly rich merchant whose wealth comes from spice and fruit from distant lands, you can have a painting of that on the wall, Here’s what I’ve got. And this is why revolutionary art is such a thing—There is going to be a revolution, the world does work in this way. What we know but cannot see, or is not present now, can go into art and it becomes a sort of supplement to our reality; our brains aren’t very good at differentiating between dreams and reality or between pictures and things, and so it sort of resolves these tensions or papers over these gaps in ideology.

We see this with the contemporary art market, which should be a single word, artmarket: art appears as a pure global commodity, it’s supposedly something that is valued perfectly at auction by the market, its value and its price are identical, it is moved through logistical networks just like everything is, possibly without the owner seeing it (contemporary art as literally encrypted capital—you exchange your money for art and the art goes into a freeport holding, literally a crypt). And it is the product of an individual worker being recognised, correctly rewarded, and sucked into this system of exchange. Okay, but nothing works like this, not even artworks. It’s a pantomime, a sort of mummers’ play of how the global neoliberal capitalist economy is supposed to work. The people who need to believe that it works know that it never works like this, but the best way for them to show themselves that it really does, that Santa Claus really is real, is contemporary art and its unregulated market value.

MBK  So if property regimes are generative, producing the conditions for a certain order of object, and if art in a given society is an index of the property regime in force, then Bitcoin also drives wedges between the concepts that have structured the way that the value of art has been understood?

RM  In my early writings and art, I don’t think I started out with that, I started with very simple little things which did one thing that art did, or that the art world wanted or did. They were all very much following this strategy of disobedience-in-obedience. You know, Artists need a catalogue raisonné. Here you go, here’s one in the blockchain. No, not like that! I didn’t move on to the decoding/recoding side of it until NFTs became a thing, at which point I could contrast what I had been imagining as a blockchain art world in 2014 with what the actually existing blockchain art world of 2017 had become. And it did not go in the direction I had imagined, but it was a really interesting direction. The reason I didn’t anticipate that direction was precisely because I was being anti-commercial for political reasons.

The point at which the delamination becomes entirely explicit in my work is when I get to Certificate of Inauthenticity (2020), which is obviously a negative assertion: This is not an authentic artwork by me. As Michael Jackson said, The kid is not my son. But I only really went for the delamination idea when genius Twitter users started pointing out that you can just right-click on and save the JPEG associated with an NFT, and that this means you don’t own the NFT. It’s absolutely wrong, in a really interesting way. Let’s just imagine for a moment that we destroyed the artwork as property, by placing it into the copyright public domain—and we can do that with CC0 at this point—then you have delaminated possession from ownership, patronage, and reputation from exploitation; you have delaminated use and exchange value. Anyone can use the image, nobody can sell it. You can sell the thing which represents its social cachet or its reputational proxy.

MBK  And then there’s the delamination of authenticity and authentication.

RM  Authenticity is tied to the problem of the fake. A fake is a cuckoo in the nest, it’s a double-spending of something that should only exist once. And it’s absolutely key to the commodity property regime, because if we take the story of the history of art, what we understand as the history of art at face value, you either look at a nice aesthetic work and it is good or you look at a nice aesthetic work and you don’t like it. Notice the difference. But if a fake has that effect on me, then we’ve introduced something from outside the canvas—and then the happy world of either aesthetics if it’s the 1950s, or signification if we’re in the postmodern and contemporary era, starts to look like a just-so story. The fact that whether something is fake or authentic can change its value from nothing to millions…it tends to focus the attention.

I think the Warhol authentication committee, for example, have stopped stamping works because you can’t authenticate a Warhol. He got his friends to make them, he gave them out as party favours, he would happily write ‘This is not an authentic Warhol’ on the back if you asked him to while he was signing your copy of Interview. It’s a category error in the case of Warhols. And people have these double-denied stamps on the back of these screen prints that Warhol gave them. As I already mentioned, this investment, this sort of fatherly ownership of offspring that can suddenly not be who you think they are, this anxiety of ownership, this anxiety of authenticity, maps from the double-spending problem in blockchain to the problem of the fake in art to the problem of patriarchal lineage in anthropology to the problem of wage labour in capitalist society—because you are doing this thing and you’re not getting the benefit of it, the benefit is going to someone else. You’re not getting what you think you should be getting.

The fake is a modern problem which belongs to a certain property regime. If you were a priest commissioning a panel for your church, someone turned up, they painted it, you were perfectly happy with it (unless they were a heretic, in which case you’re in big trouble). It’s only once you get the market operation of art that you know, and need to know, where the art has come from: you have paid for the thing, and if you find out that you didn’t get what you paid for then you are perturbed; if you find out that you are not selling what you thought you were selling, that’s even worse; and the promise of the surplus value affordance of the public ledger is that you know where the art came from, you know who’s had it, you know how much it was sold for, you know where it is.

Now, anyone within crypto discourse can immediately tell you all the problems with that—like the fact that you can register the Mona Lisa as an artwork that you have made on the blockchain. That’s simply illustrating what crypto theorists could have told you long ago had you been listening. It’s not ‘owning the crypto bros’, it’s being late to the party. It’s the problem of getting offchain truth onchain that undoes the dream of a more transparent fake-free market for art as an absolute.

There are two potential ways to approach this: firstly you can go for the oracle problem, the problem of getting offchain truth onchain in various ways that allow you to establish, with however much certainty you desire, that the person who made the artwork is the person who made the artwork and the person who bought it is the person who bought it, and that it’s not anywhere else. The NFT then becomes like a useful publication medium and a useful medium of representation and exchange but it becomes sort of a means to an end for these lofty ideals for blockchain art. And there’s nothing wrong with that.

But the other way you can go is to lean into not knowing who your father is, and just say: look, the idea that you look at something and like it or not is absolutely correct. We’re going for pure cypherpunk anonymity. Purely onchain work. If you like this bunch of pixels and want to pay me £10 in Bitcoin for it, that’s great; if you want to give me £1m that’s great; you don’t know who I am, you don’t know who made it, it’s entitled Turd at Sunrise or whatever, you find that amusing. You’re paying for the work not the meatspace provenance, so if you overpay then that’s on you. In theory at least.

MBK  These are the effects on art of turning the dials on Satoshi’s assemblage, tuning the parameters to produce different situations. The interesting thing is that a certain contingent of people get angry about whatever model you suggest. They get angry about people buying NFTs just because they saw something they like, but they also get angry about the idea that NFTs can be real carriers of value. One way of making NFTs interesting is to ensure that they activate the fears that produce that anger.

RM  People do get upset when artists just mint something. If it’s bad art then that’s perfectly valid. I’m going to grit my teeth here and say that what Damien Hirst attempted recently was good: he did an NFT drop, you could own the first Damien Hirst NFTs; he then said that, after a certain amount of time, you can burn the token and he will send you an actual little drawing or print or whatever. That’s a use of the technology to get people to work through the game theory of value. Which is far more interesting to me than anything that was hefted off of the wreck of the Unbelievable, it’s the most interesting thing he’s done since he positioned a shark carefully, because it leans into the game-theoretic and value-questioning underpinnings of technology in a way that needs someone with that reputation to give it weight, to make it a serious proposition.

You do get people who just go to content honeypots like DeviantArt, grab a load of pictures and use free minting on rare art platforms to see who will buy it. That is bad, but it’s an attack on both the NFT community and the illustration art community, it’s not the NFT community attacking the illustration art community.

And if we look at those levers and dials again, we can get rid of free or just-in-time minting but that reduces accessibility and inclusion, so it has a social justice effect. Or we can maintain free minting, which includes more people who otherwise would not be able to afford to start with this stuff, but reduces the cost of double-spending—it precisely works against Satoshi’s original use of the hashcash algorithm, which was designed to stop email spam, to make it too expensive to be worthwhile. So once again, this is the fine tuning of the parameters to achieve different goals which people regard as just but which, if you change them without due care and attention and hope to have a nice subtle effect, may disappoint you or overexcite things in the wrong way.

MBK  In relation to the actually existing world of NFT art, it seems as if there are two possible levels of appreciation, which are slightly at odds with one another, and both of which you bring into play in your work. There’s an appreciation of individual works that have a critical purchase on the medium, but then, in ‘Welcome to the Dessert of the Real’, you argue that the aesthetics of the majority of what I’m going to uncharitably call landfill NFTs can be seen as the product of the affordances of the collision between human perceptual response patterns and the technological platform. In that case, from a population-scale point of view, the NFT phenomenon as a whole tells us something critical about the platform and about art; it becomes its own critique, demanding to be read and interpreted.

RM  Yes. My kink is obviously critical art. That is not for everyone, and I came to that position from liking pop art, graphic design, airbrushed painting, whatever. So I am desperate not to be a snob, or a ‘gatekeeper’, in current crypto art terms, and not to miss what is interesting and new and valuable in actually existing NFTs by sitting around pontificating about the ideals of mid-twentieth-century critical theory, now some seventy years old. I have had some very long conversations with very competent art historians about what could be interesting about 10k PFP projects, for example, and for me there are three things: firstly they’re all very anti-aesthetic; they all have anti-aesthetic aesthetics. Secondly, there’s a sort of all-overness to the collection and this is enabled by the way that you see them on their sites. And finally, they get used as ritual masks. These are interesting aesthetic and anthropological and art-historical things which my distaste for apes looking to one side must not prevent me from being able to examine. I’m not going to be a snob about this, I’m going to look at those things. It ties back to creativity theory: if you retain a certain naivety, you will look at things that people who have to be terribly secure in their sophistication will not pay attention to, and because of that they will not be able to jump between the tracks of ideas that they need to in order to get to the very critique they claim to be interested in.

There were all the NFTs in which lots of people were just stealing motion graphic demos off YouTube, which is bad, but at the same time, a lot of very talented illustration artists and 3d modelling artists and motion graphics artists suddenly had a source of revenue because they could represent their work as NFTs. What did the audience for these go for? It’s difficult to categorise it but in ‘Dessert of the Real’ I try to. I’d tie it to the idea of something ownable, something that, like a bower bird seeking a mate, signals that it has what you want. And what the buyers want is digitally expanded ownability. There’s a trivial sense in which any NFT is ownable, but it needs to look like it, like an expensive watch that has lots of jewels on it because the jewels tell you it’s an expensive watch, despite the fact that the actual expensiveness is in the very finely tuned mechanism. By symbolising or communicating or depicting quality, whether gold or heft and solidity or skill or unusualness, this art does this. And I think that if you ran some sort of cultural analytics software you would find that the art that people have gone to has this kind of overt ownability to it.

MBK  It’s a mimetic lure that tacitly responds to the problems of value that the platform creates?

RM  Absolutely, yes. And not just the problems of value that it creates, the problem of how to hook yourself up to those signals, of understanding or being able to see that value.

MBK  And we’re back to property regimes being generative.

RM  The other thing that interested me was that they all tended to be short animated loops, which for me irresistibly reflects blockchain time, but I think in reality it gives the buyer more to own, because it adds duration and therefore extra substance to the things you’re looking at. It renders it, not kitsch necessarily, but certainly unproblematic, by smoothly looping it. So you can consume it unproblematically, but the thing it does do in a critical way is to signify this value. It’s a peak shift stimulus, it’s a aesthetic value sex toy or fishing lure, it takes it all to 11.

MBK  You could probably talk about that in quasi-Darwinian terms, because I assume the first rash of NFTs were more varied, before makers homed in on how to put the ‘right’ signals out there.

RM  There’s definitely a fitness function at work.

MBK  In the approach to aesthetics you present in many of the essays in this book, and which is embodied in some of your work, you lean right into this: Why don’t we just produce a market for aesthetic elements or qualities, and see what wins? Like a techno-anarcho-capitalist Koons: not only allow people to like what they like and celebrate it, but use technology and the market to reveal what they really like rather than believe their stated preferences.

RM  If we accept the idea that markets are epistemological environments, that they are truth procedures but in which the truth is that of price, then yeah, the truth has to be extracted from a population. Something which has never been sold is difficult to price, something which is singular is difficult to price, something which is sold infrequently is difficult to price. Price is meaning, it’s one-dimensional meaning, it’s meaning collapsed into one dimension in a dark forest strike. But it’s all the more substantial for it.

MBK  And is that compatible with critique?

RM  For critique, there cannot be, there should not be, and there is not any secure external position from which we can point at the object of critique. If we believe we occupy such a position, that way lies very bad things. It’s very much that we have to build the plane while we’re flying it, or we are jumping between icebergs as they head towards the waterfall or whatever. There is no fixed point from which we can move the world by virtue of adopting an aesthetic or even an ethic of critique. Critique is not a solved problem. And the worst pseudocritic is the person who has an answer for everything, and it’s the last idea they ever need to know. What’s this? I don’t know but it’s fascism.

MBK  But when you talk about criticality as a kink, you’re manifesting a kind of modesty that critical art is generally not known for, because it’s generally understood to be inherent to critique that its conclusions stand for everyone, that my agency as a critic tacitly involves a universalisation—everybody has to participate in the game of critique I’m playing. We are moving into something different here—the critique is not happening at the level of the epistemological subject judging the world. The critique is something that’s out there taking place, and which the subject needs to decipher in order to understand which concepts are valid and which have, objectively, been invalidated by the new behaviours of the virtual objects outside.

RM  Which is very much where my work syncs up with the argument found in a neorationalist philosopher like Reza Negarestani, that intelligence is not merely in the head, but is a faculty of society.11 Critique is a faculty of society. If we are good Marxists then we have to say that things contain their own critique; recognising it is useful work that we can do, but to try and earn credit or social capital from that is to misunderstand one’s role in the unfolding of history. At the same time, the game of ‘giving and taking of reasons’ so dear to neorationalism is not a thing. There is no greater reason than a Tsar Bomba. Beyond a certain point, Because I say so always wins out. So the imagined role of the Greenbergian critic is an illusion. The greatest art critics of the twentieth century were the CIA and Valerie Solanas, not Greenberg…I’m back in character here and I’d be absolutely horrified if anyone started the Myers school of theory based on that! But what do we believe criticism does? Firstly, I just have no time for permanently negative criticism. Give me some criticism which says how something can be made better in a particular direction. It takes work, it’s inefficient, it does not accelerate, it slows you down, and yet it’s where the value is. And at the same time, the social text is not written in ink; sitting there saying You’re doing it wrong is the opposite of critique, and distributed machines are better at doing that, actually. This is something that happens at the level of populations, not at the behest of some godlike figure of the critic.

Think of the lawyer cross-examining someone at a criminal trial: Answer the question, yes or no, it’s binary, it’s zeros and ones. What kind of epistemology are we in here, where it’s yes or no to Have you stopped embezzling funds yet? This may not be the best way of reaching a judgement or, if it is, judgement may not be the best thing to reach for. It may not matter so much for art criticism, but if we are destroying information whilst attempting to ‘do justice’ then there is going to be a big problem.

So this is why I try to have epistemological modesty and recognise that we are all guilty. I stopped talking about ideology and I started saying ‘investments and prejudices’, which is obviously trolling, but the serious point is that your ideology is the product of your spending time and various kinds of capital to achieve a particular position, and your prosecution of it is speculation on that investment. The degree to which this is wrong is interesting, the degree to which it is right is why actual criticism is very hard. Investments in the emotional and financial sense and speculation in the philosophical and financial sense. And with prejudices, the idea is that judgment is discrimination, it is literally discriminating. A lot of artificial intelligence criticism gets hung up on the idea of a discriminator or statistical bias because terms of art in different fields of endeavour mean different things but have the same name. If you have a discriminator, it just tells you whether a value is a or b. Elsewhere, if you are discriminating you are irrationally and or unjustly performing an incorrect action based on prejudice. Artificial intelligence burns capital to produce discrimination in both senses—but the idea that we are somehow not immanent to this, that we are somehow transcendent to everything that we think is wrong with humanity, for me it’s just untenable, it’s the worst sort of thinking your shit doesn’t stink. I’m sorry, you do have objectives within society, however much you dress them up in flowery language. We all have our breaking points as well, and avoiding them is probably a good idea for ethics.

But if you want to produce an actual critique, do something: talking is doing something but it’s not the only thing you can do, hence the art and writing and code and stories in this book. Because if you limit yourself in the means that you are willing to employ to achieve your ends, if you say I’m only going to hop on one leg until I achieve what I want, you might not actually get there. This is the problem of local maxima in hill-climbing artificial intelligence search algorithms: you follow a line obsessively, get to the top, and think I’m as high up as I can possibly be—but there’s an enormous mountain next to you which, had you wandered a bit more randomly, you might have found. I’m grounded in these metaphors from corporate-inflected creativity theory and from artificial intelligence, because both of them fascinate me as epistemologies. None of it taken at face value or first-order, but used as resources.

MBK  A set of methodologies or meta-methodologies?

RM  Yes, it’s like chaos magic: don’t get hung up on whether something appears terribly serious or has a pure lineage. If it works, use it. It’s being a hacker not a software engineer.

MBK  Speaking of magic, at various points you invoke it in relation to the blockchain, particularly in relation to hashes.

RM  That’s to do with the problem of names. A hash, a cryptographic hashing algorithm—and as far as I know the term ‘hash’ refers to a recipe which mixes everything up so you don’t really know what went into it but it tastes good with some Tabasco—is designed to produce a stable and deterministic result: run it again and again on the same data and you will get the same output: stable, but unguessable until you actually do it. If you look at the data, you have no idea what’s going to come out at the other end. And vice versa. Now, if you think of the philosophical question of names—Is there any necessity to a dog being called ‘dog‘ rather than ‘sheen’?—once again, the cryptographic wedge drives itself in, and says you can have names that are entirely determined by what the thing is, but that have no meaning, and have no traceable relation back to the thing that they perfectly identify.

How is that possible? The magic of cryptography. There is no recoverable semantic link to the item that the cryptographic hash nonetheless perfectly identifies with a uniqueness that comes down to big numbers. Most modern hashes produce a 256-bit identifier. You could hash every particle in the visible universe and not run out of space using that. It’s really unique. Whenever there was a cryptographic hash and it had to go into a database, I used to worry about avoiding collisions. The computer science argument is, if you’re worried about that, then you should be absolutely terrified about the sun exploding tomorrow because that’s more likely.

These are the dark-forest cryptographic spaces in which you can hide something, but still know the true name of it, and can refer to it without necessarily revealing what it is. And this is the dialectic of perfect identity/perfect secrecy that you get with public-key cryptography, applied not to the content of communication, but to the content and identity of data.

This relates to magic because cryptography and magic go together at liminal moments in history. In the early modern era cryptography had to separate itself out from witchcraft. A lot of the early cryptography manuals in Europe served a double purpose as magical summoning guides—serious talking-to-angels-summoning-demons-creating-magic-squares kind of thing. Now, it may be the case that this was simply to conceal the cryptography, or it may be the case that the government agents of the time were concealing their cryptographic knowledge by pretending to be wizards. This would be fairly high risk given that you’d be burnt for being a wizard, but lower risk than the King or Queen being horribly displeased with you. Take John Dee, bumming around Europe trying to turn lead into gold for various monarchs and sending back magical writings to England—it makes a lot more sense as the activity of a secret agent than that of someone who was really easily taken in by anyone with a black mirror.

So at liminal moments the two are confused, and then they separate as things settle down. We see this again in the Victorian era: Edgar Allan Poe was big both in cryptography and in spiritualism. After Poe died, for a while one of his most popular poems was one that was written posthumously and channelled by a spiritualist medium. There’s the idea that the book of nature is encrypted and that to access it we have to break the encryption on it—a veil of ignorance that separates us from the spirit world and the afterlife.

Today, cryptographers are still mediating figures between the known and the unknown. If you take a sociological view, they do have this priest-class role in terms of their responsibility for communing with the unknown to underwrite the social order. This is why they appear to be such morally fraught figures; this is why the question of access to cryptography is so morally fraught for people who want trusted third parties in our communication that can stop us doing Bad Things as conceived of by Good and Nice People who of course never overreach, misunderstand, or have false positives.

So magic and cryptography look like one another, they both have these weird symbols and systems, apparent nonsense comes out of the other end of both, and yet it is claimed that communication is happening. They are both regulated by the state up to the modern era in various oppressive ways, and they are both concerned with access to an outside or an underneath.

To be more demonic about it, the dark forest of cryptography, this cryptographic hyperspace through which we send messages to be read by the recipient but no one else, embodies the same human desire as magic, the same masculine design on secrecy and power, but in a very efficient way.

Over time, the legal and social rhetoric around the threat and promise of cryptography is remarkably stable. Since the early modern era the same arguments have been given each time, with the same players and the same outcomes, and this rhetoric always comes back when you have these liminal moments where cryptography and magic, however conceived, are tangled together again. This rhetoric goes back to the early modern era, when the state was concerned with being able to decrypt communications between individuals and individuals were concerned with being tried by the state and the secret communications that they had produced being decoded by the state. The questions of privacy, of secrecy, of disclosure, and of methods of cryptography as popular entertainment and as a means of securing state commercial and personal communications, all of these go back to the late sixteenth century and recur in the Victorian era and then again in the nineties with the cypherpunk era. Each time the rhetoric is the same: the rhetoric of big numbers, of saving people with a timely secret communication, of harmful secrets. The cypherpunks certainly made the correct and winning latest move in this ongoing struggle, but we’re seeing it again now, just twenty, thirty years later with the state insisting that they should be able to read all of your communications: Trust us. No. We’ve been doing this for hundreds of years and if you had any historical literacy whatsoever you’d know that you’re being assholes: don’t do it, just for once learn from history.

With public key cryptography, we’re again dealing with a kind of delamination. There is something curious about it since it gives you both absolute secrecy and absolute identity, because the two don’t go together in the popular imagination. They’re not a mathematical product of cypherpunk ideology, they were deduced first by GCHQ, but the British can keep secrets so they didn’t tell anyone—and about two minutes later by some American researchers. The technology was liberated in the early nineties. This access to technology made it possible to imagine a society where you didn’t need the state to guarantee your private communications or your identification to others—you don’t need to show your papers, you don’t need a mailing address on the voters’ registration list. And, taking that and running with it, money is communication: money, as the American courts found if you’re a corporation trying to corrupt the political process, is speech. This is correct and interesting in ways that individuals have yet to fully benefit from. Cryptographic private speech—not private language in the Wittgensteinian sense, more like twinspeak but between an infinite set of twinned correspondents—allows you to imagine that you can be secure and private and identified against all areas of state overreach and interference.

Once again, Satoshi’s assemblage of different technologies just so happens to enable the completion of this part of the cypherpunk project. But it has this massive surplus value that we are still working through today. And for me, this technique, the combination of technology in these ways that speak to these anxieties and fit into this history of ideas and history of rhetoric, it seems like a useful irritant.

Then there’s obviously the problem of where religion goes when we’re all terribly smart post-enlightenment materialist thinkers. If you accept the theory that Satoshi was Hal Finney, this is someone who was terminally ill and is now cryogenically frozen in a transhumanist afterlife with the promise of resurrection. And Bitcoin happens to be a good way of paying for that because it’s a futureproof currency…. So the American spiritual landscape may feed into Bitcoin, or at least Bitcoin comes from that milieu—a milieu that is not just cypherpunk but where spiritual engagement takes the form of transhumanism.

MBK  In ‘My Little Penny, Bitcoin is Magick’ you set the idea of sympathetic magic against kabbalism and gematria, which don’t require belief or faith, but just running the numbers—a kind of signals intelligence.

RM  Yes, because there are some who have talked about blockchain as ritual, and they’ve very dismissively tried to reduce it to ‘mere ritual’ from a sociological or anthropological and, at worst, a merely psychologised point of view. The ritual aspect of it was as obvious to me as to anyone else, but for me the idea of its being a ritual had a lot more weight, and can be used to underwrite more explicit rituals.

MBK  Implied in the use of gematria is the fact that, as soon as you get a symbolic system of a certain density, then connections can be detected in it which are undeniable and yet don’t functionally contribute to the intended operations of that system. Short-circuits, synchronicities, correspondences, and so on. The effect of the rich semantics of the outside world mining the surplus value of the code within a more impoverished functional realm.

RM  I talk about nature being encrypted, and about gematria as a side channel attack on God’s private signing key—this is very flowery, but at the very least at the level of metaphor, this is what gematria is: gematria is the idea that the encrypted book of nature has a weakness in its cipher that we can, in cryptographic terms, attack, or in hacker terms, exploit, to get it to leak information from the dark forest of creation. So I zoom into and out of metaphoric mapping and literal operation, being deadly serious about the history of ideas and deeply ironic about the actual application of them.

In using the hashes, one idea that was important was Deleuze’s idea of the ‘dark precursor’, which is any coincidence in the structure of knowledge or culture that you can hang something onto. And again, this is coincidence intensification, which is magic, and critique as disenchantment with the self-description of something.

MBK  Some of your work seems to operate by showing that, when brought into contact with the density of cultural and historical associations available to us—which, in fact, it always is in contact with—Bitcoin as a ‘machine for truth’ (to adopt the most boring-sounding description of it) can be used to produce something oracular and impossible to resolve. Is Art (2014, 2016/2017) is the most acute possible case of parlaying the blockchain model of truth into something that we regard as being a truth worth thinking about, a profound but irresolvable truth.

RM  It is absolutely correct that Bitcoin is a truth machine, but in keeping with the theme of bathos, it is a truth that would make a philosopher weep. There is no profound truth to ‘this coin has been created by the system, this coin has been spent by this person, we have moved this value from address a to address b’. All of that may be entirely true, and it is a truth made real by a substantial fraction of the planet’s computing machinery, which makes it a truth that the writer of a dictionary or a law or a sermon or a book of philosophy can only dream of having at their disposal. A truth underwritten by that much computing power really is a truth that really is generally acknowledged. But it’s not the sort of truth you can guide your life by, ‘the block reward went to address 1La8w72…’.

In Is Art, you have people choosing to spend Ether as, you know, gas—so there’s an opportunity cost for this and there’s a pricing effect to this—just to toggle the state of this artwork. And this truth will then be made real, intensified, or underwritten, with each block of the chain making it harder and harder and harder to materially undo. This is a model of almost precisely how truth doesn’t work. There’s a passage in The Hitchhiker’s Guide to the Galaxy where Arthur Dent is asking the Heart of Gold starship drinks machine for a cup of tea, and it keeps on analysing his brain to find out what will be absolutely perfect for him and producing a small cup of something that is almost but not entirely unlike tea. Blockchain truth tastes something like that.

Disobedience-in-obedience again: I enjoy producing these monstrous and thereby critical misunderstandings or overly literal understandings of people’s takes on what truth is and how it relates to socio-economic reproduction. That’s a
lot of weight to put on one bit of text and a few lines of code, but it’s there if you want it. If you don’t, it’s a fun bit of the history of art, it’s a fun little early Ethereum hack. But for me it problematises this model of truth whereby truth is something we arrive at either by agreement or through power and which then just sort of spreads by some mysterious osmotic process through society. In Hitchhiker’s, again, the ultimate truth of the answer to the question of life, the universe, and everything is 42. That is quite profound and understandable compared to the average truth on the average blockchain—and yet those truths are unassailable.

MBK  To tie this question of truth back to magic, perceptibility, and attention: there’s something to be said here about the unquenchable human need for aesthetic stimulation and how it betrays the supposed technological quest for efficiency and reason, whether that’s conceived of as progressive and virtuous or fascistic and oppressive. Vanity addresses, for example: Why isn’t the type of truth produced by the blockchain enough? Why isn’t it enough for you to just be a whale, why do you also want to have the equivalent of a personalised number plate for your Bitcoin address?

RM  The structure of those early Base58 bitcoin addresses is different from modern Bech32 ones, which are more effective in many ways but less interesting aesthetically. You do weird things to the hash of a public key and a checksum and a version identifier which gives you a guaranteed first letter. So for early bitcoin that was 1, and for Dogecoin it was D. And that was followed by a series of letters, and from that you can unpack the hash of the public key, which means that you can check that something has been signed by the right person without them having to reveal their public key. You know you’ve got the right one because of the checksum, and you know that you’ve got the right chain because of that letter prefix. The old ones have sort of various computer science problems and problems as to how you process them, which is why there are newer address formats. But as a piece of both information and user experience and aesthetic design they’re wonderful, they’re instantly recognisable.

Ultimately, this question of truth, I think truth is one of those ideas that’s like love. There are many different phenomena that we call love and they are absolutely real but they are also massively confused through the imprecision of language. With truth we have facts, we have deeply held wishes, we have resonant statistical phenomena, we have, you know, the outcome of applying computer code—these things are all ‘truth’. The ability to produce truths in Bitcoin is very clearly and directly a product of the possession of capital. Miners can censor the inclusion of transactions, although they cannot change the meaning of transactions sent by other people. And if they do so they are economically irrational, they are economically disincentivised to do this.

MBK  They play the role of the judges of truth?

RM  The miners are the producers and the underwriters and the judges of truth, but they have no capacity to decide what is true or not in the sense of the truth procedure: they can only apply it, they can’t even say I would like to let you off from this criminal charge but the law won’t allow me to, they just say Yes, this computer program will run, that’s fine, I am calculating this, look at this hash with a long string of series at the start, yeah, give me some money. I’m deliberately playing fast and loose with the word ‘truth’ here, I am pretending I’m using a philosopher’s conception of truth, when I mean merely the fact of a logical one rather than a logical zero. Ultimately, the truth of the transaction having taken place in this dark forest of pure intensive value tells us very little about what has happened on the social plane. If a philosopher could produce as much truth as a Bitcoin miner they would be delighted to death; but if a philosopher could only produce the type of truth that miners produce they would die of disappointment. So as far as the production of truth goes, it’s this ironisation: Hurt me! It’s possible to have absolute truth but with no meaning! No, not like that!

MBK  Undermining Plato’s cave….

RM  This is why I prefer doge, doge miners are called diggers—not in the Christian sect sense but in the dog-looking-for-a-bone sense. With the cave, again we’re back to phenomenon inside and noumenon outside, the prime material plane and this realm of absolute fact, of absolute zero-meaning truth of pure financial value, of price, mere shadows of things. Out in the world it’s doing something, it’s paying people’s rent, paying for people’s meds, it’s very often buying cryptography-related swag. And the truth of the transaction having happened is related to the truth of that offchain event, just not in any recoverable way.

MBK  In ‘Crypto Sigils’ you tie the notion of magical invocation and access to the outside to hashing algorithms.

RM  Again, it’s all about names. Hashing gives you a true name for some information that you can use to summon it, but which you can use to refer to it without revealing its content—you can share it with others without them knowing what it is. As I say in ‘Crypto Sigils’, this is like a residual sigil as an information-destroying representation for an idea or an intent. ‘Crypto Sigils’ describes an entirely serious bit of chaos magic, because it’s saying, take this popular technique from contemporary culture and apply it to the actualisation of your intent, to your coincidence intensification, which is what magic is and also what money is. But the reason it’s an interesting exercise is that it helps to understand how these things work, and it’s attempting to understand how they work in a way that isn’t going to melt down a nuclear reactor or lead to anyone being out of work or anything. It’s just this pocket universe where you can go into that realm, mix everything up, come back, and it’s concretised in different ways.

In Blockchain Aesthetics (2014–2015, 2019) I rendered transaction hashes as modern and postmodern aesthetic bromides. As I originally realised with the YBA generators I made, you can use those kinds of things because people will recognise them as signalling art. So we’ve got the crunchy candy shell, the reassuring protein shell of recognisable and recoverable aesthetics. And then we have the experience of the complexity of this system which, at the time I made it, was this unseen network which was understood by the people making it, but I felt there wasn’t an appreciation of it as a thing in the world that was really doing something.

Hashes are produced all the time, everyone pushing github is producing a hash, everyone using Bitcoin is producing a hash, Bitcoin miners produce trillions of hashes a day. But this means that hashing is something which has being, which has reality, through repetition, through the intensification of its application. And so rather than saying that the application of a hash is creative, I make the move of saying that the application of a hash is culturally resonant. It’s the same as using a pack of cards in the pack-of-cards era, or using a newspaper for bibliomancy in the mass-media era. Here we are in the cryptographic era, and what is the way that everything is touched? By hashing. And that gives me what I regard as a critically or genetically or projectally defensible thing that I am doing that is not simply cargo-culting the technology. I had to convince myself that it was legitimate to use this purely mechanical procedure as a link in the chain of production of meaning.

If you can get a hash which, like a vanity address, has curiously ordered properties that grab human attention—for example, if you represent your hash as any kind of numbers, if it has a lot of zeros at the start—that’s unlikely. Something unlikely is perceptually interesting, something perceptually interesting can be worked with creatively or reflectively and, because you have something unlikely, you have already started from the position of coincidence intensification. And this comes directly back to Bitcoin, because its proof of work algorithm is precisely modifying the inputs to a cryptographic hash algorithm until the output starts with a long and unlikely series of zeroes. Bitcoin mining is literally coincidence intensification, and as I say coincidence intensification is one definition of magic, particularly within chaos magic: polling chaos repeatedly until it produces something that’s perceptible.

So on one hand these gestures map very clearly onto the technology of Bitcoin, but on the other hand they take the work that Bitcoin does, in various senses of the word including the physics sense, and extract surplus value from what is already a coincidence intensification machine.

I’m not suggesting that I can take an external point of view on any of this, I’m involved and there are limits to rational investments in it and logical reasoning about it. We don’t have a clear view or a perfect theory for this because we don’t know how it turns out.

MBK  Before we leave the question of magic, the other spook you invite in is the possibility of artwork-DAOs that have a kind of agency and are able to operate in the art world on their own behalf without human intervention. Which conjures up associations with golems, tulpas, and egregores, but also operates an amusing inversion of the modernist question of the artwork’s ‘autonomy’.

RM  That’s very tricky, because the quasi-agentic model of art is in some ways key to the idea of art’s autonomy. The idea that artworks can somehow speak or enter into a dialogue with the viewer is very powerful, but it threatens to mislead our understanding of society. If we make this horribly literal via bot art—and I was working on bot art before blockchain—a DAO can absolutely be a quasi-agentic collection of rules, of embodied intent, and people lose their shit about those, even though they have no problem with charitable trusts or legal personhood for rivers. This representation and embodiment of intent by a particular model of code and a particular set of incentives is merely something that the blockchain makes cheaper for anyone to be able to do.

There are various different models for how DAOs could produce artworks which literalise or operationalise the quasi-agentic model of art. You then come back to the idea of embedded purpose that you get in chaos magic with servitors, and, back in computer software, with daemons, which run in the background and do things without you always asking them to. Again we’re going back and forth through the rhizome of the history of ideas from computer science to demonology to chaos magic to anarchist co-ops and the market anarchist idea that if you give people property rights over the sky then they’ll be economically incentivised to not burn it up. That all comes together and, again, doing this in art gives us a dry run for doing it for real, but with lower stakes.

LeWitt said: the idea becomes a machine that makes the art. DAOs are ideas made into machines, and they can produce something. Let’s take each of those steps, juice the surplus value from it, take it very literally, and see what we
can do.

Why people who are interested in human organisation in all forms are not fascinated by a public plastic medium for making explicit organisational principles, I’m not sure. Sure, we can try and evolve bigger bladders so that we can win any given cooperative meeting, but, you know, the telegram was invented some time ago, the average conception of organisation in contemporary organisational circles is less sophisticated than in Ancient Greece, not philosophically but literally, technologically, in terms of the technology they use.

I have to admit that there’s some bad conscience to my continued individualistic art production, because I should really have made a DAO or some sort of platform or standard where I could bring in the wisdom and madness of crowds to make art that could not be made any other way and that would therefore have a historical necessity to it. I haven’t done that, partly owing to lack of social skills, partly owing to a very heavily internalised conception of the modernist artist, and partly because this is how I have fun! Having meetings is not how I have fun. But if there’s a failing to my own performance relative to any reading of what I’m doing, it’s that I’m still doing it as an individual cryptographic identity rather than at least socialising myself through a DAO.

MBK  Maybe something for the future…! Having looked back in this conversation to where you started with all of this, how do you feel about the point it has brought you to—in art and life—today? I get the impression that you toiled for a long time in fairly cheerful obscurity, and that your sudden notoriety in recent years has come as more of a surprise to you than to anyone else. But you also seem to be engaged in a constant reinterpretation of what it is that you were doing in those earlier works. Has the art done what you needed it to do?

RM Where I started was the feeling of not belonging in the world and not understanding how the world worked, so there’s this emotional or psychological impetus in the work which I recognise now very clearly as dysphoria and dissociation—the feeling that the world is somehow behind glass and that everyone else has a rule book that you don’t have, and trying to explain the world to myself. With my art I’m trying to collapse the distance between myself and the world through understanding. I was aiming to change the world through my art, which is unlikely, or to change myself through my art and rehabilitate myself as a social subject. But I didn’t have the knowledge that I needed to fix my epistemology in order to really attempt that task because the part of my brain that should have worked out I was transgender when I was eighteen at the latest was being overridden by the part of my brain that was spending more and more of the day making very sure that this was not something I would ever consider as the remotest possibility. But by the time I was in my forties it was like having a stage magician following you around and whenever you’re like Hang on, this doesn’t make sense, they pop up with a card trick, Pick a card! It was just distraction and denial and disavowal and dissociation, not as a conscious project but as a background process in the operating system of my brain, taking up more and more of the runtime.

But this meant that, on the way through a succession of incorrect understandings of the world, I did learn how to learn about different aspects of the world, how to criticise myself and these aspects and their relations, and I managed to create this sort of spider web of associations and critiques. I came to enjoy the exercise of my critical and creative faculties upon something which other people couldn’t tell me was wrong and which I couldn’t tell me was wrong, and which often had an irresistible quality to it, something like the Shareable Readymades or Tokens Equals Text. These are ideas that assembled themselves in my mind from what I was doing at the time, and I learned over time to get out of the way of that: to just make it, and then afterwards find out how it relates to everything I’m doing at the moment. So, much like Conan being chained to a wheel for his entire adolescence, I built up my critical faculties by not knowing what the hell was going on.

MBK  Do those realisations mean that you now have a different view of the work, of its interrogation of secrecy and privacy and identity? And does the fact that the urgency of avoidance has abated make you less inclined to make more work, even though your work is now getting more attention than ever before?

RM  In a sense, my egg cracking was the fulfilment of the projects I was doing when I was twenty.

MBK  We’re entering into a retrochronic temporality here?

RM  Yes, you can see why the work of the Ccru continues to resonate for terminally online trans women: we are our own hyperstitions, terminators sent back from the future to assemble ourselves from the resources we have now: our bodies and what we understand about culture, having not really understood it for however long.

But my constant theme is disobedience-in-obedience. What I had asked was How does the world work? and the answer Here is why you don’t understand how the world works does not answer my original question! It has the effect that I wanted, which is to remove the barrier between myself and the world, to collapse me into the prime material plane, to make me mortal. But it only raises more questions, so I am absolutely no less motivated to make the work. And I’m deeply flattered to have an audience that has invested psychologically and financially in the work that I have made, so I have a strong sense of obligation to them not to fuck it up.

Transition does not solve all your problems, it may not even make you less anxious, it may not make you less depressed: what it does do is it makes it possible for you to face the reality of those things. And this can be awful because actually dissociation can be very useful when you are dealing, or in fact not dealing, with them. Being checked out can help you survive things much better; if you’re no longer checked out it doesn’t make things easier to cope with, but it may give you the knowledge and impetus to deal with them. When Morpheus gives Neo the red pill he says, Remember, all I’m offering you is the truth. Being unplugged is not paradise. I always remember the line from Neuromancer where Case is injected with neurotoxins and he’s lying in a hotel room for three days feeling his ability to connect to cyberspace basically dribble out of his ears, and then he ‘crashes into the meat’. Hackers have disdain for ‘the meat’, and you’ll notice that lots of trans people are software engineers, and if that’s not the effect of a revenge hex by one of the women who was cast out of the software development in the sixties when it became professionalised, perhaps it’s because dissociation is a superpower: if you’re checked out of your body and want a world that makes sense when the world around you doesn’t, and if you do not care about how you look, feel, or smell, then you’re prime material for software development. But in whatever field it gets expressed, this displacement, this attempt to master a body of knowledge rather than knowledge of your body, it’s a coping technique which is completely understandable and deserves love and a big hug.

MBK  But reality finally seeps in and the work then becomes about something other than what you thought it was about? Is that what you mean when you mention an anxiety around people who have invested in your work?

RM  As with any body horror story where someone begins a transformation, the thing that has to start the process of change will be changed by the process of change. This is the general problem of self-knowledge, which is diagonalisation. Once you know that you are not who you should be, that doesn’t mean that the thing that you become will want to be the thing that you think you want to be…if the book tries to rewrite itself then you end up in the realm of recursion.

A meaningful existence is something that was inconceivable to me—I was proud of being a parent, I enjoyed being in love and making art, but each decade I lived I was amazed by it, and I could not imagine a future beyond. Crashing into my body just in time to watch the end of the world is the sort of cruel irony that would make you feel a Marvel universe movie is a bit underwritten.

I’m not an expert on ideas of gender, I’m not even an expert on me, I can certainly talk about my own experience, but one of the things I had to do was to let go of trying to explain it. This is an overwhelming reality, I will worry about the impossibility of any of it later but this is happening, so we’re gonna ride it out because trying to deny it was threatening to destroy me. Once I became aware of it, it became aware of me—in the best tradition of horror movies. And that’s where ‘Necronomicon’ begins.

MBK  There’s a great deal to explore here in terms of encryption, signatures, time, identities and their backpropagation…. This process, also, involves not a transcendent given but a generative regime: gender has to be pragmatically enacted in order to find out what kind of virtuality is produced, and the generative effects of that virtuality have to be navigated.

RM  Rerolling your character sheet halfway through the campaign. Why not? Aly Chace points out that hyperstition is just Butlerian performativity….

MBK  How do you see the contrast between how you imagined blockchain art in 2014 and its actually existing reality in 2022?

RM  There’s both the danger of romanticising paths not taken and that of romanticising the early experiments. But everyone needs to look at Plantoid, everyone needs to look at Terra0, everyone needs to look at these early projects that answer so many questions about how we can do these things in a way that doesn’t simply build platforms that will be captured by venture capital and destroy the environment and won’t support artists. They are amazing applications of the real affordances of this technology to the real problems of artistic production.

In my case, I was very anticapitalist, I thought, we need to bring socialism into this, not just transactional economics. That meant that I was completely wrongfooted by NFTs because I was leaning away from commodification and financialisation. The model I had was the one I work through in the representations of art in ‘Artworld Ethereum’—these different functions and entities in the art world operationalised as code that you can then hack on to modify the performance of those functions. By the time I wrote ‘Ethereum Standardized Contract API’, which included resale rights, and handled registering and buying and selling and listing works onchain on Ethereum, that brought up the legal, financial, and moral responsibility of actually making that into a business, which is what I would have had to deal with in order to make it into a real thing, and I couldn’t cope with that.

But in that early work there are so many models of things that people simply haven’t done since, and there are so many representations of artworks that people simply haven’t used. By the time you get to ‘Standardized API’, that’s a complete model for representing artworks not as tokens—I didn’t know artworks were going to be represented as tokens—but as smart contracts that can offer themselves for exhibition, take exhibition fees, evaluate opportunities for exhibition, and interact with curators in order to show the (presumably electronic) artwork that they manage. There’s nothing like that out there. There are wonderful curating and collector DAOs—disclosure, I am collected and curated by various DAOs—but there is absolutely a more social model of the art world to be had; there are still these interesting models, different representations of things that are artworks and different possibilities of organising the commissioning, production, exhibition, sale, and critique of art in ways that simply haven’t been done.

There are the actually existing rare art platforms, NFT platforms, and there’s the potential of DAOs, which are intensifying their way toward very interesting production. And for me there’s the historical example of technologically accelerationist art, modelled as autonomous or quasi-genetic entities on the blockchain.

So there’s interesting work to do here that isn’t simply treating my early work as an 8-bit aesthetic, or using it as a signifier of lost futures or as retrotech camp. I would love people to rip off my early work, I’d love people to combine it with modern NFT standards. But if we want this sort of social organisation against the capitalist reification of value and extraction of value then the best way out may be DAOs, with their alignment with economic incentives. This is precisely the horror story of ‘Maximum Transaction Density’: the best way of doing this involves everything being bought and sold, timestamped with the mark of the beast….

MBK  The only way to the best is through the worst?

RM  It’s the worst from the point of view of an anticapitalist, antifinancialist psychology, but it’s not the worst from the point of view of material existence. You don’t need everything to collapse socioeconomically in order to get to maximum transaction density, quite the opposite. You do need to let go of your ego and your investments and prejudices in exculpatory fantasies. We’re into cosmic horror with economics as the only limit, outside of the human subject. And the social subject is going But I just want to draw furry art all day and for people just to give me food for no reason. How about that, but via a DAO? No, not like that!

MBK  Given that your work addresses two different constituencies which are generally suspicious of or indifferent to one another, and you take great pleasure in prodding and provoking both of them, who are your audience?

RM  Art & Language talked early on about producing work without an audience: they had an imagined future audience—probably after the revolution—but there was not an actually existing audience for their work. And with my stuff, if you wanted to get a full understanding of it you would need to know meme culture, computer programming, some French philosophy, arguments from bulletin boards, and so on—but to get the impression of it, which will certainly do a lot of its work, you need to know a lot less, you just need to be open to the idea that there may be something there.

As for the book, some readers will pick this up and go What is this? I wanted doge memes and there’s a technical standard here that no one has ever used, there’s something claiming to be art which is basically a computer program with one bit of information in the variable, and then there’s a story that does have doge memes in it but I can’t read it because the narrator is unreliable… So if I pretend not to be me (which I spent many years doing) and step back from this, it looks sprawling and chaotic. But I think it’s actually very consistent—not that I planned it all out from the start. The previous phase of my work was broadly around the idea of freedom, free culture, free software and the limits on that. And then it became very much about secrets and making them very secure. But the persistent, repeating question here is: Are you sure? Are you sure this is a horrible right-wing technology that has no value for your socialist project? Are you sure the art world is all blue-haired hipsters who want to ban you from owning firearms and silver? Are you sure that perfect security is possible? A constant voice of doubt, but not the kind of doubt that sits there and points and feels very secure in having found a place outside the world from which to act superior. I’m diving into the mud.
