Vickrey Auctions are often used by states to privatise radio frequencies in the electromagnetic spectrum. Extending this to the visible spectrum, to colour, would be the stuff of satire. But below the state level we do see the inefficient allocation and exploitation of colour and other aesthetic properties in the art world. We can tackle this using the intelligence of markets and simulated property rights on the blockchain.

Let us first make individual colours, shapes, line and surface qualities, and other aesthetic properties representable as non-fungible tokens on the blockchain. These can then be sold. The right to use those properties can then be sold by the owners as fungible tokens.

Compositions of these tokens can then be represented in turn by a secondary layer of non-fungible tokens and usage rights for those expressed by their own higher layer of non-fungible tokens.

This process can be repeated until concrete instances of the expression of aesthetic properties are expressed by composing non-fungible tokens from different layers into a non-fungible token that can be treated as unique or, alternatively, editioned using a final layer of fungible tokens.

If we use the Ethereum blockchain for this, the system can be represented as a stack of ERC-721, ERC-1633,2 ERC-20, and ERC-998 smart contracts.

In the absence of rent or taxes, the owners of non-fungible aesthetic properties can make money by selling those tokens or by releasing and/or re-purchasing fungible tokens that represent them. The optimal strategies for this are outside the scope of this essay, but they involve reacting to demand at different levels for fundamental and derived/composed properties in a timely manner.

Returning to the redistributive aspect of the Vickrey Commons, we can (pre-)sell the fundamental aesthetic properties to one or more foundations that exist to profit from them in order to redistribute those profits to deserving artistic and/or social causes. It is possible to imagine various ways of structuring those foundations as smart contracts or their payment(s) as domain-specific tokens, although introducing a Tokenized Aesthetic Vickrey Commons currency coin risks the introduction of a central bank-like entity into the system.

Where the foundations’ revenue must go to the authors of works using those properties, this is possible to enforce simply onchain, although avoiding the sybil problem and other issues with onchain redistribution is much less simple. Where we wish to enforce more complex relations between the work and the foundation we will need aesthetic comparison games, which can be completed onchain but are much more expensive than a simple token check. Where the foundations’ missions are more arbitrary, controls begin to look more like human organisation than enforcement through code.

It is not possible to exclude duplicate token contracts on a given blockchain without support for doing so at the protocol level, and in the general case it is impossible across blockchains without protocol support for a cross-chain proof-of-precedence protocol. It is even easier to simply not use these tokens. Why, then, would anyone use them?

Anchoring a singular source of these properties through first-mover and network effects may be sufficient to make it authoritative for anyone who wishes to use them. The use of these tokens is then a means of establishing price and authenticity, which, if we squint hard, is to say that it is a means to establish value.

Further objections to this translate neatly into objections to the art world and schemes to reform or replace it.

Notes

1 E. Posner and E.G. Weyl, Radical Markets: Uprooting Capitalism and Democracy for a Just Society (Princeton, NJ: Princeton University Press, 2018).

2 B. Rennekamp. ‘Re-Fungible Token (RFT): What Happens When a Bonded Token Owns an NFT?’, medium.com, <https://billyrennekamp.medium.com/re-fungible-token-rft-297003592769>.

Hash Gematria

2020

 

Gematria in Hebrew uses a SIGINT attack on God’s fully homomorphic encryption of the book of nature to extract meaning. A non-Hebrew gematria is a glimpse not back into the mind of God but forward through the fall of the tower of Babel into a scrambled linguistic world of contingency. It is a generator of Deleuzian ‘dark precursors’ to connections between concepts, just as rhymes are. These connections are useful irritants, spurs to the generation of actual structure that would otherwise not occur, anchors for beliefs. Both kinds of gematria are exercises in exploiting the surplus value of code. The former is revelation, the latter is construction. Yet they resemble one another as much as is possible in their respective universes.

Interpreting letters as numbers recapitulates the history of mathematical notation. It is a choice defensible on the grounds of its maximal simplicity and its historical embedding. Cryptographic hashing lacks both this simplicity (for a human being to calculate the value of a word numerically takes seconds, for them to calculate a cryptographic hash by hand would take around fifteen minutes) and this history (cryptographic hashes date back only to the 1970s).1

It does however compress a history of ever-increasing uniqueness and thereby security (in the senses of both secrecy and stability) of identity. It is part of the present moment of the history of technocapital/techonomics rather than a form of nostalgia for the past of accounting and its gentrified forms (aka ‘mathematics’). As the number of cryptographic hashes calculated by the Bitcoin network alone approaches 120,000,000,000,000,000,000 per second at the start of 2020, the process of hashing reinforces its reality and its effects in the world through sheer volume of repetition.

Cryptographic hash collisions (instances in which the hashing of two different pieces of data generates the same hash value identifier or ‘name’) are vanishingly unlikely by design. The evolution of cryptographic hashing algorithms has been the evolution of ever more effective ways of scattering bits into cryptographic space in order to destroy their significance while retaining their identity. But the 64-character hexadecimal (base-16) strings used to encode 256-bit hash values are difficult for human beings to read and compare, so software systems such as git or Docker that use them pervasively tend to truncate them by displaying or reading just the first few characters (the ‘prefix’) in order to make them more readable.

These shorter values do collide as more and more hashes are used to refer to more and more things in the world (this is the ‘Birthday Problem’) and so longer prefixes have been used over time. It takes two hexadecimal characters to encode one eight-bit byte of data. The first byte of the hash value is two hexadecimal characters, the first two are four characters, the first four are eight characters etc. We can represent hash prefixes in more exotic bases: Proquint, BIP-49, Urbit @p, Base 56, Bech32 or even decimal. But these are not the representations of hash values that are displayed pervasively within the culture of computing.

Non-cryptographic hashes will collide far more frequently, but they are not embedded in computing culture or in the culture of technocapital and its imaginaries of resistance (crypto-anarchy, cryptocurrency) to the extent that cryptographic hashing algorithms are. We therefore mean cryptographic hashes when we refer to hashes here.

Replacing letter-value summing and decimal reduction with cryptographic hash prefix collision generation gives us hash gematria. This is a hype-cycle peak-shift maximally historically contingent and embedded extractor of surplus value of code. This surplus value will itself consist in maximally historically contingent and embedded dark precursors. As a strategy, this is inflected by the pop-cultural strains of Chaos Magick, but has far greater qualities of repetition and embeddedness and is more abstract and therefore more dynamic than a specific cultural expression.

If this seems unconvincing, what would a stronger candidate be?

A stronger candidate for what?

To get the first four characters of a cryptographic hash of a piece of text using the Unix command line, enter something like the following:

echo -n "egress" | sha256sum | cut -c -4

Notes

1 K. Shirriff, ‘Mining Bitcoin With Pencil and Paper: 0.67 hashes per day’, <https://www.righto.com/2014/09/mining-bitcoin-with-pencil-and-paper.html>.

WART—Wrapped Art

2020

Wrapped Art (WART) applies the ideas behind Wrapped Kitties to Rare Art tokens, opening up new possibilities for investment and aesthetics.

The Wrapped Kitties Ethereum smart contract takes CryptoKitties ERC-721 non-fungible tokens (NFTs) and gives out its own ERC-20 fungible tokens (WCK) in return, ‘wrapping’ the former in the latter. This makes it easy to buy and sell kitties in bulk, to use kitties in Decentralised Finance (DeFi) applications, or to trade low-valued kitties that one wishes to dispose of for others with more potential value by ‘unwrapping’ the ERC-20 tokens back to (different) ERC-721 CryptoKitty tokens. WCK is credited with giving CryptoKitties a 50 percent price bump in mid-2019.1

Wrapping Rare Art ERC-721 NFTs with ERC-20 Wrapped Art WART tokens would allow the same use cases. It would be a way to get (financial) utility from (financially) under-performing art. Or a way of exploring new artists by swapping NFTs via the contract, either randomly or by seeing which other ERC-721 Rare Art tokens the contract currently has wrapped and redeeming WART for them accordingly.

The Wrapped Art smart contract should allow tokens from more than one Rare Art platform to be wrapped and unwrapped, both to allow the widest range of tokens to be used for liquidity purposes and to give access to the widest range of artists and works. On a technical level this might require the contract to be centralised in order to manage the list of platform contracts it accepts NFT tokens from, but on balance this is better than tying it to (one version of) one platform or a frozen list of contracts that will rapidly become outdated. To decentralise this while robustly incentivising the curators to act in the best interests of WART, we can instead curate the addition and removal of contracts using a Moloch DAO with WART itself as the membership tribute.

WART gives collectors who don’t have a background in fine art but can follow price charts2 the ability to shake up their collections. This is both financially and art-historically useful, allowing collectors to benefit from access to both financial and aesthetic liquidity as they build their collecting strategy. The blasphemy (for art historians) of regarding art as a fungible asset class and the blasphemy (for financiers) of regarding money as an insufficient sign of value resolve one another here. In doing so they create better signals than the positive feedback loops that would otherwise emerge when, under the efficient market hypothesis, purchasing power, and nothing else, drives artistic development.

It might appear that WART turns rare art into pure financial quantity, but in fact WART turns Rare Art into a pure aesthetic quantity. The monetary value of the art wrapped by the WART smart contract can be established by the trading price of WART but given what its tokens represent, this means that hodling specific quantities of WART has an unavoidable aesthetic significance in addition to (and by virtue of) its financial one. Status games of hodling significant amounts of WART (in the sense both of large and evocative numbers) without unwrapping or using them for any further purpose might emerge in response to this fact. This fundamentally changes the concepts of collection, exhibition, and value for art, coming down on the side of the price charts in terms of form but thereby opening that form up to new aesthetic content.

Taking these ideas even further, we can wrap the content of tokens. For example, MATH tokens can be wrapped or burned in return for the bits that represent their numbers as ERC-20 (or ERC-1155) tokens. We can’t break a CryptoKitty up into their genes (that would be cruel) but could we break up the image that a Rare Art token represents into its component colours? What cryptographic proof of the offchain pixel values could be produced onchain?

Or we can reverse this process and start by constructing NFT art from other tokens representing discrete aesthetic quantities. COLOUR tokens start to do this with their wrapping of MATH tokens, but are monolithic and atomic rather than being constructed from discrete elements. Tokens Equal Text is constructed from elements, but each contains several words and the colours used are implicit. A more general and powerful NFT art construction system will require a different approach.

Notes

1 O. Avan-Nomayo, ‘WrappedKitties Trading Sees CryptoKitty Base Price Jump by 50%’, crypto.news, 14 June 2019, <https://btcmanager.com/wrappedkitties-trading-cryptokitty/>.

2 WhaleShark, ‘Out of Left (Brained) Field #4: What We Can Do Better Together’, c/, 9 February 2020, <https://beta.cent.co/WhaleShark/+ln4bju>.

From WART to DeAes

2020

Building on some of the ideas of WART (Wrapped Art) tokens gives us a powerful toolbox for organising the evaluation and production of blockchain art.

Rather than using a Moloch DAO to decide which ERC-721 contracts to accept tokens from as we could in WART, we can use it to curate a collection of tokenised art directly by voting on which tokens to purchase, commission, lend, and sell as a form of Decentralised Aesthetics (DeAes, pronounced ‘dais’).

The membership application tribute for joining the DAO can be ERC-721 tokens (unwrapped if we modify the Moloch DAO codebase to directly handle ERC-721 as well as ERC-20 tokens, or else wrapped in the DAO’s own purely internal equivalent of WART) or Wrapped Ether (for the purchase fund). WART itself should not be used for this as it (deliberately) makes selecting artworks for their aesthetic content more difficult.

Each proposal for managing the collection that the membership puts forward to be voted on must fit the stated aesthetic of the DAO, and each member’s votes must evaluate whether that fit is real and appropriate. The DAO’s aesthetic and the interpretation/application strategy used to realise it can be updated through voting proposals, with or without rewards for successfully making the change.

If a DAO member wishes to ragequit, they can take art and Ether proportional to their shares. Art allocation strategies for ragequitting can use an internal equivalent to WART, a simple FIFO queue or ERC-721 token, a flat percentage scheme using an agreed-upon source of (pseudo)-randomness, or a more complex combination of or replacement for any of these approaches.

Operationalising and financialising aesthetic evaluation in this way via investment portfolio management ties art-historical development to market signalling in a dynamic and accelerated way, which is to say a mutually beneficial one. This is a radical approach that requires art historians and financial investors to learn much from one another. Despite this, it still cannot reach into the artwork except before the fact in the form of commissions, which extends the history of patronage in a useful way but leaves the frame of the artwork otherwise intact.

We can take this approach further by limiting the DAO’s voting proposals to the tasks of producing or authenticating art, turning the DAO into an artist itself. A production DAO recreates a Koonsian approach to the outsourced fabrication of art, whereas an authentication DAO recreates a Kostabian one. The fabricators of these artworks can be paid in Loot or, if the aim of the DAO is to grow by adding likeminded members, in fully voting shares.

Even where the DAO becomes active in the production of art in this way, the edges of the artwork are left intact after its production, securing the interior of the artwork against being connected to market signals. Removing the firewall of the frame rather than routing around it or simply liquidating its contents (an approach I discussed along with the WART proposal) will require still more radical steps.

Intensive and Extensive Aesthetic Property Token Composition

2020

ERC-721 tokens can be composed into tree structures using ERC-998 tokens. Where those ERC-721 tokens represent images or image elements, that tree structure becomes a rendering tree or two-dimensional scene graph (three-dimensional scene graphs will have to wait for 3D Rare Art standards to solidify). To lay out the elements of the image we must be able to transform them in various ways, changing their position, size, colour, and other intensive and extensive aesthetic properties. As they are continuous quantities, we can represent these aesthetic properties as ERC-20 tokens with 18 digits of precision.

To apply these properties to an ERC-721 token we can attach them using ERC-998 composable tokens in an SVG-style tree hierarchy. Each ERC-998 token has one or more quantities of ERC-20 aesthetic property tokens attached, one or more child ERC-998 tokens, and zero or more (usually zero or one) ERC-721 tokens attached. The properties expressed by the ERC-20 tokens attached to each ERC-998 token are applied to any attached ERC-721 token(s) and transitively to the children of any attached ERC-998 tokens.

Where the values we wish to represent should be limited to a given range (e.g. 0.0–1.0 or 0–255), we can either raise an assertion if too many tokens are sent to be attached to ERC-998 tokens (we might also be able to refund them in an additional transaction, but this would affect the gas required—and as per the ERC-20 standard we should not accept fewer tokens than are sent), we can treat higher values as meaning the maximum (e.g. 3.1 is 1.0, and 1337 is 255), or we can scale values relative to the largest quantity.

When the values must be both positive and negative (for example, if we are representing coordinates around an origin, especially relative coordinates in a group hierarchy), we can use a second token to represent negative values (this would be better represented using ERC-1155 tokens, but ERC-998 does not support this standard). If both positive and negative tokens are applied, their values should be summed. For coordinates we can use only positive tokens by treating group origins as their top left rather than their centre and only adding positive offsets to child ERC-998 tokens.

Affine transformation ERC-20 tokens are applied as a transformation matrix to the children of the ERC-998 token they are attached to. This means that children-of-children multiply their parent matrix with their own. There is an implicit graphics state push/pop for each ERC-998 token, so transformations do not affect sibling tokens, only child ones.

For colour or alpha (transparency) values, these values are added to the colour values of the image represented by the token. This may not be the expected behaviour. As with coordinates, using only positive values can be achieved by carefully structuring the hierarchy of the image so that child ERC-998s only need to add rather than subtract colour values to represent their colour scheme. Alternatively, we can treat colour tokens as scale, allowing both increases and decreases of colour to be expressed across the token hierarchy, and source primitive forms to be arbitrarily coloured if they are white. More complex colour interactions and other filter or layer behaviours could be specified by additional tokens.

This gives us the following ERC-20 tokens:

X           X coordinate offset values in distance units. Unbounded.
Y Y coordinate offset values in distance units. Unbounded.
WIDTH Width in distance units. Unbounded.
HEIGHT Height in distance units. Unbounded.
ROTATION Rotation in degrees. Unbounded, wraps around past 360 as usual.
RED Red scale. Unbounded, although values that multiply the source value higher than 1.0 will have no additional effect.
GREEN Green scale. Unbounded, although values that multiply the source value higher than 1.0 will have no additional effect.
BLUE Blue scale. Unbounded, although values that multiply the source value higher than 1.0 will have no additional effect.
ALPHA Transparency scale. Unbounded, although values that multiply the source value higher than 1.0 will have no additional effect.


What is to stop the owner of an artwork created in this way from breaking it up, rearranging it, or adding to it?

Nothing at all…

Isn’t this an extremely expensive way of assembling art onchain?

It depends on the value of the work that is made using it…

Why Bitcoin Is Money According to Marx

2020

tl;dr: whales.

In Marx on Money, Suzanne de Brunhoff describes the theory of money that Karl Marx presents in Capital.1

Money, for Marx, emerges in three stages prior to capitalism.

In the first stage, gold becomes a measure of the value of all other commodities rather than simply one commodity among many.

In the second stage, gold coins become the medium of circulation. Once gold becomes de-materialised in this way, its role can then be occupied by fiat currency.

In the third and final stage, the emergence of hoarding paradoxically introduces money ‘proper’.

De Brunhoff writes that ‘Hoarding is a demand for money as money’, an interruption in the circulation of money that ‘serves to ceaselessly preserve and reconstitute the money form as such, whatever the deformations, transformations, and disappearances it undergoes as a result of the other two functions. Produced by these, it becomes in its turn a condition of their functioning’.2

In the crypto space, hoarders are known as whales. They remove their coins from circulation with the expectation that this will ultimately provide them with more utility than immediately spending them. In doing so they act just like hoarders of gold coin or fiat currency. To quote Marx, ‘The money becomes petrified into a hoard, and the seller becomes a hoarder of money.’3

Whales therefore establish that cryptocurrency is money according to Karl Marx.

Notes

1 S. de Brunhoff, Marx on Money, tr. M.J. Goldbloom (London and New York: Verso, 2015); K. Marx, Capital: A Critique of Political Economy, Volume One, tr. B. Fowkes (London: Penguin, 1990).

2 De Brunhoff, Marx on Money, 35.

3 Marx, Capital, 228.

Essay on Essays on Art & Language

2020

Charles Harrison, Essays on Art & Language (Cambridge, MA: MIT Press, 1991; revised edition 2001).

The Conceptual Art artists group Art & Language formed at the end of the 1960s. Art historian Charles Harrison was a member of the group from the start of the 1970s. In 1991 Harrison published a book on the history of the group up to that point, relating it to the wider history of art during the collapse of the authority of Modernist art criticism and the rise of Postmodernism. Modernist art criticism for Harrison here means the magisterial formalism of Clement Greenberg and his followers. It is an art criticism that produces psychologised readings of the surfaces of artworks for a genteel ‘spectator’ to consume without remainder. It is, as Harrison puts it, ‘a theory of consumption masquerading as a theory of production’ that masks the historical and technical content involved in that production. In response to this, Harrison argues for a historical-materialist art criticism that restores Modernism’s dialectical contrast of values by ‘erasing the edges’ of artworks. Doing this makes tractable to art history those ‘genetic’ materials involved in each artwork’s genesis that Modernist art criticism masked with its decontextualised reading of art as primarily expressive rather than representational.

Against this backdrop of the collapsing authority of Modernist art criticism and the demands of a new critical relationship to art, Harrison presents Art & Language as being committed to a critical project of going-on without an overarching theory or (as Harrison puts it) in the ruin of that arch. Going-on was a non-theory. Recognising the contingency of all artistic representation and treating intellectual property and artistic propriety as oppressive expressions of class society, Art & Language produced work that looked very little like existing art not in order to produce saleable novelty but because that is where the work of art took them. When it is no longer possible to ‘mean the doing’ of dominant discourses and there is still no clear path forward, taking action in the face of this dilemma means committing to unpredictable outcomes. The unpredictability of those outcomes may lead out of art into the wider world, or into hard-won moments of artistic autonomy.

The disinterested spectator presupposed by the discursive content of Modernist art criticism as its historical subject exercised a particular set of competences when stood in front of a painting or a sculpture and would use them to describe what they saw (and felt) in a particular way. Art & Language’s work, and the group’s organisational structure, was oriented toward undermining the spectator’s understanding of and relation to that work in order to create a disenchanted viewer. Harrison examines many examples of this project of disorientation, and their discussion of the physical and representational structure of one of Art & Language’s more complex paintings of the 1980s (Art & Language turned to painting at the turn of the decade to avoid the trap of a ‘Conceptual Art’ aesthetic that had been quickly recuperated by the art world) shows these effects at work very clearly:

“This is the painting.” “This is part of the painting.” “This is a representation of the painting.” “This is a representation of part of the painting.” “This is a detail of the painting.” “This is a representation of another painting.” [etc.] (208)

Art & Language, Index, an Incident in a Museum XXXII, 1987. Oil and mixed media on canvas mounted on plywood, 156 x 244 cm. © Art & Language; Courtesy the Artists and Mulier Mulier Gallery, Belgium.

Art that seeks a critical autonomy, Harrison argues, must represent some non-trivial aspect of its historical moment. In painting, this must be animated by changes in figure/ground relationships. Historically significant changes in these relationships, in critical terminology, or in the spectator’s competences, must reflect moments of change in relations within class society. At such moments, Harrison argues, it is possible for technical concerns to become suffused by moral concerns. These changes are not and cannot be merely psychological—transgression is not something to be opted for. Even where artistic autonomy is achieved, alterity will be rapidly reclaimed by the mainstream, as the example of 1960s Conceptual Art demonstrates.

The history of Art & Language that Harrison tells is not, by their own admission in the introduction to the revised edition of 2001, one that all of the group’s former members would recognise. A chateau in France now houses a museum dedicated to the world’s largest collection of Art & Language artworks. The historical moment at the end of the Cold War in which Harrison was writing this particular collection of essays is long gone, in terms of politics if not actors. And in the age of contemporary art, the Nietszchean ‘litterateurs’ that Harrison criticised for their LARPing of postmodern irony would have no trouble consuming Art & Language’s a-bit-aesthetic, a-bit-political, a-bit-speaking-of-their-own-manufacture, a-bit-unusual art-objects.

But for a historical materialist project of critical art history, these objections are part of that project. The strategies that Harrison follows and those that they identify in the art of Art & Language can be lifted up out of the brief moment of the ‘end of history’ and translated into the current epoch, even if their iconographic and discursive points of departure cannot. Harrison starts their discussion of the historical context in which they situate Art & Language’s work by identifying ‘Modernism in two voices’: one in the stentorian tones of the Modernist critic and the secure beholder of authentically expressive abstract art, the other in the more questioning tones of those who notice that the first voice is saying something considerably less secure and universal than it claims to be. What is at stake in the relationship between these two voices, Harrison argues, is nothing less than the moral content of history.

The existence and production of art, Harrison argues, can be justified by the fact that aesthetic oddness or intensity is uniquely revealing. These oddnesses and intensities can serve to emancipate art by producing critical representations of Modernism (or its contemporary equivalent) in order to present the ‘meanings of the dominated’ using the media and aesthetics of high art. This is a direction of which the litterateurs have long since recuperated the posture, but not the content. What oddnesses and intensities are possible in the age of global art fairs and freeports, at what levels of the creation of art?

What can the second voice of art say with this now?

Upgrade Art Now!

2021

Transferring a human mind into a computer system is known as ‘uploading’; turning a mortal into a Power is known as ‘upgrading.’ The prototypical upload is the Moravec Transfer, proposed by Dr. Hans Moravec in the book Mind Children. The Moravec Transfer gradually moves (rather than copies) a human mind into a computer. You need never lose consciousness.

Eliezer S. Yudkowsky, ‘Staring into the Singularity’1

In the 1982 movie Tron (and its underrated 2010 sequel Tron Legacy), people are digitised, body and mind, to be uploaded into a simulation ‘grid’ via the powerful Shiva laser. They are returned to meatspace in the same way. If you only digitise the mind and don’t then return it to meatspace, you get an upload.2 Whereas if you destroy the brain in order to do so, you get a destructive upload. It’s half a teleportation. Made famous by Hans Moravec, explored fictitiously in William Gibson’s short story ‘The Winter Market’ and Neal Stephenson’s novel Fall; or Dodge in Hell among others, and depicted graphically in the recent Amazon show Upload, destructive mind uploading destroys your brain layer by layer as its structure is digitised to produce the raw data for a computer simulation of you.3 We can’t be squeamish if we want our biological raw material to be ship-of-Theseus’d into cyberspace (as in Moravec’s proposal) or captured as it evaporates in a burst of plasma (as in Upload).

Back in 2018 I wrote a short story for ArtReview called ‘The Large Glass, Burned’ in which a famous NFT artwork is destroyed as the ultimate artistic provocation to a future in which physical artworks are of interest, if at all, only as curious historical footnotes to their tokens. I was reminded of this when Injective Protocol burned a Banksy and then sold it as an NFT earlier this month.4 If anyone wants to buy that token and send it to a burn address, then that will complete this particular circuit (ask your lawyer about the moral rights implications of doing so first though, please and thank you).

Destroying an artwork after—or, even better, by—scanning it (in 2D, 3D or time-based media) to produce a blockchain proxy is a form of destructive uploading. Given how we prize meatspace art, destroying it to produce a superior onchain ‘upgrade’, in Moravec’s terminology, produces a perfect faultline in understanding between legacy institutions that regard doing so as a newsworthy crime against humanity and emerging blockchain cultural or superstructural institutions that will regard not doing so in equally critical ethical terms.

Given the certainty of ownership, permanence, and reduced archiving costs of NFT art compared to legacy meatspace art as it circulates in the petrodollar art world, added to the editioning and fractionalisation opportunities of NFTs, why not upgrade art onto the blockchain? Burn out a Leonardo line by line as you scan it with an electron beam and capture it forever with its hash anchored securely onchain.

We can’t be squeamish…

Upgrade art now!

Notes

1 <https://web.archive.org/web/20010125023900/http://sysopmind.com/singularity.html>.

2 R. Myers, ‘Uploads’, <https://rhea.art/uploads/>.

3 N. Stephenson, Fall; or Dodge in Hell (New York: HarperCollins, 2019).

4 C. Criddle, ‘Banksy Art Burned, Destroyed and Sold as Token in “Money-Making Stunt”’, BBC News. 9 March 2021, <https://www.bbc.com/news/technology-56335948>.

Welcome to the Dessert of the Real: On Certain Developments in the Content of NFT Art

2021

Non-Fungible Token art associates digital art with blockchain identifiers as metadata, producing a saleable ownership proxy similar in concept and usage to a certificate of authenticity for conceptual or video art. In itself this makes no demands on the artwork other than that, ideally, its content should pose minimal problems (legally and technologically) for whatever services or platforms the artist uses to create and sell it. Yet that art exists within a techonomic milieu that holds very specific promise and appeal for those who might be interested in it as an environment in which to produce and experience art.

Early NFT art that was not simply existing illustration work registered onchain became steeped in blockchain promotional imagery—project logos and figureheads. These were soon joined by easily consumed attentional lures appealing to the preexisting sensibilities of viewers/potential purchasers, such as naked human bodies and technological glitches. In the rare cases where the problem of their technical production and content intruded, it was when an artist or a buyer did not understand the source of a library image or of a filter’s stylistic flourish. This was a problem because of the concept of ownership as transcendental possession and control that blockchains embody. Blockchains exist to establish ownership of digital assets with mathematical certainty. This means that to own a piece of NFT art is to possess something unique with cryptographic certainty backed by a sizeable percentage of the Earth’s computing power. It is to possess it more securely than if it were stored in a vault (en-crypt-ed) at a maximum-security freeport facility, and for the object of that possession to be a more uniquely identified object than the Mona Lisa. Where the thing that you possess is a copy, a fake, or misrepresents its production or its materials in some way either intentionally or unintentionally, the unspoken contract of ‘true digital ownership’ is broken offchain. Or at least ironised and degraded in the hodler’s eyes.

Discussions of authenticity (as opposed to authentication) in ‘authentic digital art’ emerge as a panicked response to this encounter between onchain mathematical certainty and the messy nature of human understanding offchain. To respond to this by securing authenticity offchain is to destroy the value of onchain security entirely—you may as well have a paper certificate of authenticity. A cypherpunk dark forest of aesthetics, completely disjoint from meatspace concerns of identity, reputation, and career, would firewall artistic value from authenticity—a liberation that would upset art historians as much as any existing market. But security thinking can be a cold, lonely, corrosive lifestyle and is not for everyone. Or even for most.

A different approach is required in relation to the slow recuperation of the cypherpunk imaginary that is wider society’s encounter with it, an approach more engaged with the affordances and resonances of blockchain technology. This is not an ideological prescription, it is a recognition of the factors that drive every artist and collector/purchaser’s attraction to NFT art and which must show returns if NFT art is to be coherent. As transaction fees rise ever higher, neither preestablished iconography nor discrete stylistic flourishes sufficiently establish aesthetic value worth securing and owning on a blockchain as blockchain art. Art is proof of work, whether it’s Whistler’s Nocturne or an Abstract Expressionist act of heroism. There must be something to own, a parsable product of labour that represents the potential to exponentially multiply the value of an investment of time by the artist and of cryptocurrency by the collector. Strategically, the artwork has very few moves at its disposal to achieve this. One such move (a modernist/postmodernist one) is to dramatise and reflexively intensify this experience of the possession of something that lives in a world of discrete technological time. The more vivid this experience of ownership can be made—the more parsable, although absolutely not the more easily digestible—the better.

This has led to the accumulation of significant form in various registers (aesthetic, technical, economic, social) for excess/surfeit, materiality/presence, and presentation/encounter in NFT art. The in-frame agglomeration of this is reminiscent of the bricolage of Post-Internet Art, its careful leading of the viewer reminiscent of motion graphics in advertisements, cut scenes, and other liminal moments of guided significance. Where assets and shaders being from libraries would be an impediment to true ownership when presented in a two-dimensional artwork consisting of the mere conjugation of one or two of them, their composition and animation in time—the work that they do and represent—within the current wave of NFT art performs the requisite alchemy to make it clear that it makes the unownable ownable. Materials, interactions, choreography, are assembled into brief microworlds boasting an aesthetic surfeit that makes very little sense as anything other than an answer to the problem of NFT art at this point in its development, but makes a great deal of sense as that.

There are limits to this (and any) strategy, but we are not there yet. Intensification of or finding lines of flight from the elements of this strategy, in sum or individually, can take us very far beyond the current state of the market now that we have broken this resistance level. Capitalism loves challenges to its property regime, they are how it ensures its further expansion. True digital ownership requires there to be something to own. Unproblematic commodities, consisting either of artistic originality or of commercially manufactured aesthetic swatches, do not provide a sufficient challenge or object upon which to hang the predicates of desire for an ever-intensifying experience of possession. The fact of the digital aside, this dynamic was as true of paintings of fruit or of music and dance based on peasant forms as it is of GPU-accelerated aesthetic flights of value.

High culture is a strategy of enclosure, of proprietisation, of the transubstantiation of kitsch/low forms in an affirmation of the wider economy’s transubstantiation of resources into value. It is also a heatsink—symbolic resolution or closure for the untenable claims or other moments of a given (property) regime. You can see far more of the former than of the latter in the current state of NFT art, but the latter is present precisely in that art’s engagement with the psychological requirements of ownership. Just how far the latter goes will depend upon how blockchain technology engages with wider society over the next few years.

True digital ownership as a memeplex that collapses itself into aesthetics by assembling materials and competences to be deployed in an intensive experience that can be owned onchain has produced an experience of moments of post-digital surfeit as an answer to the demands of artificial digital scarcity.

This is the world as it exists today.

Blockchain Temporalities

2021

Every ten minutes on average, Bitcoin’s miners add a new block of transactions to a chain that stretches back to the ‘genesis block’. Other blockchains may have faster heartbeats, their pulsions targeting just a couple of minutes or even a single second. But they all move forward only, from zero blocks in the direction of time’s arrow.

Block formation time is probabilistic, part of a cybernetic process that converges on certainty over time as more and more blocks build on top of an existing chain. This process is irreversible, owing not just to its use of probability but also to the trapdoor-function-based proof of work system that secures the Bitcoin blockchain. Its products, the blocks, can be walked trivially via the chain of hash values between them. But only backwards. The angel of history has very little to do onchain, but a general-purpose time machine is useless. Block history needs and can have no theory internal to itself. To observe that this isn’t what history or a theory of history is, is to identify one limit of historical thought.

Block height, the number of blocks since genesis, is a clock. Bitcoin is a timestamp server (Satoshi uses that phrase in the Bitcoin Whitepaper, but doesn’t mention ‘blockchain’), so this is hardly an accident. But it is resonant beyond its intended function. I’ve met people who have timed offchain events to specific block heights, whether podcasts or weddings. Block height is also a calendar—of block reward change ‘halvenings’ and of scheduled protocol forks that are treated as festivals and holidays. And then there’s pizza day….

Bitcoin’s ten-minute block time average is maintained by its difficulty retargeting algorithm. This is a paperclipper for producing time. It will indeed boil the oceans if that is what it takes to secure that ten minute pulse. Any source of actualised being is a paperclipper, otherwise another being will exceed it. Therefore any actual being is excess. This does not produce the best of all possible worlds, just the most of all possible worlds. It’s paperclips all the way down. All the way back down the chain to genesis. The big bang is the original trapdoor-function proof of work, and if you want to stake on a new great chain of being you’ll have to send your stake into a black hole (stars are just burn addresses).

Time for Bitcoin is pure succession, just as number is pure succession for Dedekind. It is singular and absolute, non-relativistic (as per Nick Land, in this one context). Worse, that time occurs in time, breaking Heidegger’s argument that it cannot. We can recover from this a little by pointing out that this time does not occur within itself but in an outside temporality, and a reassuringly relativistic one. Still, it occurs in time, and produces a time of pure succession.

This is a purely intensive world, an undialectical history within itself, without the reflection of the bureaucrat’s ego in Spirit. But step back and the onchain world and its history are shown to be incomplete—the private keys that unlock its cryptographic transactions must exist only offchain. This veil of ignorance, an epistemological barrier like that of the edge of the simulation, also applies to onchain time. The Unix timestamps placed in each Bitcoin block leak the offchain time that each block occurs at, but they could be a lie. They must increase over time within certain limits, but compared to the block height number they do so in irregular leaps. Onchain, block heights are certain, block timestamps very much less so.

Bitcoin is the techonomic instantiation of Deleuze’s third synthesis of time. It is an empty repetition determined by the future. For Bitcoin that future is the block height (not the calendar date) when all 21 million Bitcoins will have been mined, and the self-assembling reality of that future produces its present—a hyperstition secured, with an increasing fraction of the Earth’s computing resources, by the block difficulty algorithm.

Cyclical and linear time interplay on the blockchain as Anna Greenspan describes them doing within capitalism,1 which is hardly surprising given Bitcoin’s anarcho-capitalist roots. The different temporal scales and intensities folded into the blockchain in order to produce it make it a Deleuzian egg. Which, through a deliberate misreading, makes it a world. We can call it a welt if it helps, which it never does.

Blockchain temporality comes into being with the blockchain, and vice versa, at the same moment. This is similar to the reciprocal emergence of capitalism with capitalist time, as also described by Greenspan. Different temporal orders afford different social orders. To nestle in this particular temporality is to subject oneself to the blockchain as surely as Stelarc’s ‘Ping Body’ was subjected to the internet, yet far more pervasively.

Lyotard’s libidinal proletariat would recognise this ride.2 We can enjoy it as they did, or we can keep waiting for hot atomic clocks in our area to swipe right on us.

Notes

1 A. Greenspan, Capitalism’s Transcendental Time Machine, PhD, University of Warwick, 2000.

2 J.-F. Lyotard, Libidinal Economy, tr. I.H. Grant (London: Sage, 1993), 111–12.

Recanting Quasiproperty

2021

In ‘Blockchain Poetics’ and ‘Tokenization and Its Discontents’ I described blockchain-based property as ‘quasiproperty’. But I was wrong. Blockchain property is just property.

I should have known this. As well as engaging with the question of property in my writing, much of my smart contract art carefully backgrounds the question of ownership in a way that renders it problematic. Democratic Palette (2016) requires gas fees, which is to say money, in order to exercise the ‘democracy’ of the title, and so on.

It is possible to create different ownership and economic regimes on top of blockchain technology, but ignoring the property question or loudly performing affordable pantomimes of refusal isn’t the way to do this, although it’s worth asking what that is the way to do.

Conversely, protesting that NFTs commodify easily copyable resources without providing any meaningful ownership of them ignores both the existing state fiat intellectual property regime (copyright is an act of fiat…) and the other half of the famous Stewart Brand quote ‘information wants to be free’:

[…] information sort of wants to be expensive because it is so valuable—the right information in the right place just changes your life. On the other hand, information almost wants to be free because the costs of getting it out is getting lower and lower all of the time. So you have these two things fighting against each other.1

The blockchain steps into that fight as a substitute for state fiat in matters of property, one that subtracts the contradictions of anarcho-capitalism. To respond that the resulting property regime simply reproduces the fiat state blocks the objector from also complaining that it must fail to reproduce its redistributive functions. The blockchain may reproduce the worst features of the fiat state property regime from the point of view of this objection, but the fact of this selectiveness means that it may also select different and other features that may be less objectionable.

Property regimes are generative. The map precedes the territory. Beware naturalising their history and moments in the history of resistance to them when producing a posture of opposition. The problem with objecting that NFTs are enclosure is they don’t deny access/exploitation of the original resource to anyone else. To emphasise this: they create ‘possession/ownership’ without exclusion of access to the original resource. If we are to believe this is simply enclosure, this raises questions about enclosure, not NFTs.

We see the resolution of this in the move to CC02 NFTs, which entirely separate the issue of copyright ownership from the issue of NFT ownership by repudiating copyright. You can own the token, but not its image. This makes property a matter of association, not enclosure. A fiat state regime of violence may be erected around this, but is not necessary in order to produce it.

This creates a form of property free of the state and of materiality. The latter may be a precondition for the former. And it may be that no true form of property existed prior to this.

Notes

1 Discussion between Steve Wozniak and Stuart Brand, Hackers Conference, 1984, Sausalito, CA, <https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Information_wants_to_be_free>.

2 <https://creativecommons.org/share-your-work/public-domain/cc0/>.

For an Art of Pure Price

2021

Greenbergian painting oriented itself to flatness, tracking the aesthetic singularity of not-being-sculpture, as if the Earth had escaped into the second dimension rather than bleeding out into it in a final Dark Forest strike. There’s a model here. Or at least a line of flight.

If, pace Greenberg, dimensional reduction establishes artistic worth, then one dimension is obviously better than two. And we already have a one-dimensional form in art: price. Price orients itself to the fitness function of (Greenbergian) art criticism while also restoring the exclusion of information from the world outside the frame (hello, artistic autonomy!) to the work.

Artists intensify a given medium’s affordances. They track its singularities. They get on a train and make paintings depicting countryside scenes with tube paint on canvas rather than trying to get it to stick to a plaster ceiling like egg tempera. Sometimes, existing frameworks of aesthetic evaluation cannot process the results. In this respect, at least, artists should be shameless. If they say your art is just impressions then call yourself an ‘Impressionist’.

What kind of art is regarded by the mainstream as being purely about price, without any redeeming aesthetic merit? Why, NFT art of course! The complaint that NFT art is seen only in terms of price is hardly unique to this medium, but it is intensified for it.

Stated preferences lose to revealed preferences every time. Hedgehog the Stalker went into the room at the centre of the Zone where your deepest wish is granted in order to beg for his brother’s life. All he came out with was a stack of money. The petrodollar art world says that it wants an art that offers it something other than price to talk about. And yet that is not what it talks about, given the choice.

Badiou was right, number is being.1 That number is price. Nitzan and Bichler were right, prices write reality.2 But isn’t this just market power? Did For The Love of God really sell for fifty million quid if it was sold to a consortium including Hirst and his dealer? How can we focus on the price when its inputs and the sparkles get in the way? The intrusion of aesthetics in dimensions higher than one undo the very being of such art.

Financialisation collapses the Earth into the first dimension, the masters of the universe fleeing into that dimension ahead of physical reality melting like a cartoon ice cream cone on the sidewalk in the heat of the market. The horror is that pure price is the only escape from financialisation. If there was anything else they would buy it.

What is to be done? Work to rule, comrade. Disobedience-in-obedience is the operation of irony, the creation and appropriation of surplus value, the critical exceeding of a given reduction. It’s Frank Stella’s early abstraction, Komar & Melamid’s phone-poll paintings. Take what critics of NFT art say, take it seriously, make it, and show them the results.

Fleeing into the first dimension, into pure price, into pure artistic being, ahead of the dark forest strike of financialisation’s dimensional collapse of the socius, is the only winning move. If it looks like losing, if it looks like we are not sufficiently shamed by this, perhaps we can wrongfoot our pursuers after all.

Notes

1 A. Badiou, Number and Numbers, tr. R. Mackay (Cambridge: Polity, 2008).

2 J. Nitzan and S. Bichler, Capital as Power: A Study of Order and Creorder (London and New York: Routledge, 2009).

Maximum Transaction Density

2021

Exploit yourself.

Jeff Koons

I’ve always said I don’t mind being exploited, so long as I’m exploited competently. If I’m exploited competently, everybody wins.

Andrew Eldritch1

Self-management is a way of having the work force control the contradiction between valorization and devalorization because all society would then be organized to lower the value of that living commodity, labor.

Anonymous, ‘Lip and the Self-Managed Counter-Revolution2

The joint-stock corporate design solves the central problem by entrusting guardianship in the collective decisions of the corporation’s owners, voting not by head but by percentage of profit received.

Mencius Moldbug3

I’m self-owned. I have a person. I am autonomous. The financial instrument that defines me, lodged in a corporate registry in Rio, follows me about like a ghost—the ghost of my legal identity.

Charles Stross, Saturn’s Children.4

Porcupine was not overcome by his greed. He crawled on his knees in this very puddle begging for his brother. And he got a lot of money, and couldn’t get anything else.

Stalker5

A cynic is a man who knows the price of everything but the value of nothing.

Oscar Wilde

Property then is a ‘phenomenology of perception’ combined with my capacity to take in and act on that perception.

Apio Ludd6

Property is theft!

Proudhon

Smart property might be created by embedding smart contracts in physical objects. These embedded protocols would automatically give control of the keys for operating the property to the agent who rightfully owns that property, based on the terms of the contract.

Nick Szabo7

[T]he English unemployed did not become workers to survive, they—hang on tight and spit on me—enjoyed the hysterical, masochistic, whatever exhaustion it was of hanging on in the mines, in the foundries, in the factories, in hell, they enjoyed it, enjoyed the mad destruction of their organic body which was indeed imposed upon them, they enjoyed the decomposition of their personal identity, the identity that the peasant tradition had constructed for them, enjoyed the dissolution of their families and villages, and enjoyed the new monstrous anonymity of their suburbs and the pubs in the morning and evening.

Jean-François Lyotard8

Civil society is like madness and sexuality, what I call transactional realities.

Michel Foucault9

Maximum Slogan Density.

Ccru

Neither the subconscious, the body, nor society are texts. They are ledgers, structured by transactional exchanges. This is why dreams are strange. Reality is not a social construct, it is an economic construct, a complex dynamic system of flows of exchanges. These exchanges require capital, of whatever kind, which introduces all the dynamics of capitalism into each domain. Arbitrage between them is as profitable as it is between any other markets. Critique is defection, leverage, cashing out.

As with any transactions under financial capitalism, these exchanges are distorted by the intercession of trusted third parties, who can defect from games they are not in fact playing. Blockchain technology removes this negative sense of trust, in the sense of mediation, and replaces it with mere reliance on a medium or space of exchange. This increases the ability for parties to trust each other—the paradox of trustlessness, under the failure to understand what ‘trust’ means in this context, is that it increases the capability to trust in the sense that its critics intend.

If quantum mechanics represents a market in its search for efficient exchanges of value, then supervenient higher levels of the great chain of being structured similarly is hardly surprising. There seem to be no trusted third parties at the quantum level. Finding one would be miraculous.

Removing trusted third parties from exchange, disintermediation, of whatever kind, is anarchism.

The authoritarian libido sees this as the exclusion of the moment of justice, the libertarian libido sees it as the exclusion of the moment of injustice. Against this, let us take the Efficient Market Hypothesis as a prescriptive rather than a descriptive statement and instantiate a plane of pure decoding, collapsing value into price, excluding any other dimension of evaluation. Let us demand price and nothing else.

The joint-stock company is the most efficient form of organisation known to man. As an epistemological form it identifies information more efficiently than the alternatives. As an ontological form it generates being more efficiently. But we can create unmanned forms. The design space of DACs, Decentralised Autonomous Corporations, rebranded as DAOs, Decentralised Autonomous Organisations, is a techonomic primordial soup from which these xenoeconomic lifeforms can crawl.

Being is number, says Badiou, and he’s right, but that number is price. Events are trapdoor functions. They are transactions.

In ‘Fall; or Dodge in Hell’, Neal Stephenson describes a quantum computing simulation of a world whose form can only be perceived indirectly, by mapping signals between its nodes. This is ‘signals intelligence’—SIGINT—a side-channel attack on encrypted communication that extracts form and meaning, (surplus) value, from it. All form, all perceived form, is SIGINT at some level.

Lose the ‘and yet’ from ‘and yet, it moves’. We don’t know what the enemy is saying, but we know where he is saying it and how loudly. Being is pure intensity (e=mc2, after all) but we’re far enough from the Big Bang that certain irregularities in its finances have been uncovered. The quantum ledger is single-entry and it tells a disturbing tale of insider dealing and over-leveraged bets that have collapsed. Reality is a Ponzi scheme.

Transactions are signals. More transactions, better SIGINT, more definite form. Becoming realises form.

With apologies to Jarry, it isn’t the clinamen that is the oracle of becoming, it is the bet.

Every exchange is a bet. As with communication in Shannon’s information theory, there must be uncertainty. Certainty means that the message does not need to be sent, the exchange does not need to be made. Information asymmetry in exchange is a matter of justice, to enforce the Efficient Market Hypothesis here—to collapse all information into price—is to make that immaterial.

As with Marx’s theory of money, it is the hoarder that gives being its form. Sure, an electron is fun but it isn’t going to hold out long against Jupiter in a fight.

The misunderstanding of Radical Markets as a ‘fascistic’ handing of public goods to rapacious blobs of capital misses the white elephant strategy of justice at their heart. Vickrey Commons are redistributive, and dis-incentive-ise inefficient use of resources. Discourse is stated preferences, but money talks. The space of reasons is a market in which would-be philosopher kings will need to outspend the irrational idle rich of social being.

Money destroys information to propagate it, slogans destroy meaning to propagate it, encrypt it, and throw away the key. It creates a Dark Forest, Meinong’s Jungle, the book of nature is encrypted. If nature is unjust….

Change requires exchange. Accelerating change requires accelerating exchange. To achieve real change, absolute becoming, requires maximum transaction density.

The Efficient Market Hypothesis proposes that price is a complete representation of the information needed by the market in order to evaluate a proposed exchange. This is dimensional collapse, like Jupiter spreading out into the second dimension in a Dark Forest Strike like ice cream on a sidewalk on a hot day, only worse, a degenerate non-affine transformation into a one-dimensional space. The economy is a line that capital takes for a walk. The violence of collapse, of exclusion, of mapping, is in full effect here. Sure, number is ontology, but someone has to hold the chalk scrawling the equations on the blackboard. Any sufficiently complex axiomatic system contains the complete works of Shakespeare. Is ignoring non-price information a stated or a revealed preference? Are you sure? How much? If Porcupine had entered The Room at the centre of The Zone and begged for money, would he have walked out with his dead brother brought back to life? Under the Efficient Market Hypothesis, price is value and price-matching is evaluation. Art is no longer middle-sized dry goods, it is its price tag. Which is to say that nothing has changed.

Property regimes are generative. Just ask commodities. The worker is commodified by the capitalist, the woman by the man (including the worker). The lesbian must first steal herself from this system of exchange, this property regime. The trans lesbian must first assemble herself hyperstitionally from available materials. But hyperstition is always-already just Butlerian gender performativity. Just ask commodities.

Property regimes are generative of properties. To cash out those properties or that property, to defect from a property regime, demonstrates that regimes are also generative of exit from them. A desire for exit that cannot be reincorporated (and capitalism, like critique, is the tail-recursive capture of escape) is either imperceptible or indigestible to a given property regime. Exit must therefore be structured within that regime as taboo—as perverse, degenerate, criminal. It must be disincentivised.

Price is a signal of economic incentive. Prices in a market economy are gamification, and are subject to all the critiques of gamification. Notably, critiques of extrinsic vs. intrinsic motivation. Extrinsic motivation creates perverse incentives. And perverse incentives distort the market. Mustn’t the cry of the (non-market) socialist be that the stated mode of operation of the (capitalist/neoliberal/financialised) market is unjust? The market identifies inefficiency as damage and routes around it. Human beings are not particularly efficient. Perfect incentives within the actually existing market would instantiate Skynet. Perverse incentives are exploits on Skynet’s operating system. To save humanity we must pervert economic incentives past the point of operational depravity. If care and justice are irrational, are externalities under the current structure of economic rationality, and we exit to the outside of that, who knows what beautiful monsters may lurk there?

Price is not magisterial judgement, any more than reliance is trust. Excluding the judgement of third parties from the moment of exchange in blockchain protocols is not fascism, however purple-faced the proponents of that view may become as a hobby. It is the removal of a distortion, it is the enabling of the choice of trust between parties by the subtraction of the requirement of trust in potentially treacherous intermediaries. Do we trust individuals? Of course not, there are entire literatures railing against the figure of ‘the individual’. Yet the inevitable restoration of familial and state patriarchal relations that lurks under the topcoat of critiques of the individual needs somewhere to be excluded from.

The economy, like gender, like the machinic unconscious, like the state/great chain of Being—so, the economy, basically—is an eigenvector. The Soviets knew the first part of that at least. Combinatorial explosion destroys shadow prices in a diagonalisation attack, but didn’t we just establish that dimensional collapse, and in particular information-destroying dimensional collapse, is key to efficient markets? We are trading JPEGs with quality way south of 50, not RAW images. The market escapes command by fleeing into lower dimensionality in order to escape being dissolved into lower dimensionality. This is the real first-mover advantage.

The Efficient Market Hypothesis has been falsified. This means only that we must immanentise it rather than discover it. Structure DAOs to gamify, distort, pervert and inefficientise exchange qua capitalist exchange. Crucially, do not do so merely as a bourgeois leisure pursuit. Create a financial-economic Ping Body with bizarre weights and subject your will to it. Host endogenic systems of xenoeconomic actors within and across the multiplicities that collapse themselves into points at the moment of exchange. At the moment of the strike.

We will bury ourselves. Artworks in freeports. En-crypted.

Our pirate signal must be broadcast from the sewers of economic alterity.

Intensify the tail-recursive loops of exchange under perverse incentives structured in transparent and uncaring code. Ride the (K-)wave. Tighter and tighter. Approach singularity. Track singularities. Socialist dimensional collapse to flee ahead of a capitalist Dark Forest strike. They can’t distil information if they don’t have it. En-crypt exchange as points at the intersection of multiple lines of collapse, or flight.

Crucially, this is both less totalisable and more efficient to secure than capitalism. Which is to say, it’s capitalism, but a capitalism of transgression through fidelity. You wanted exchange, and the monkey’s paw gave it you. Hit maximum transaction density and capitalism can only gaze upon the SIGINT form of a more absolute society-as-economy than it ever dreamt of and despair.

You can’t restart capitalism in this basement. It’s dark, and something is moving down there.

Notes

1 ‘Teletext Interview—January 2001’, Ultimate Sisters Guide, <https://www.ultimatesistersguide.org/documents/magazines3.htm#2001>.

2 <https://libcom.org/library/lip-and-the-self-managed- counter-revolution-negation>.

3 ‘Neocameralism and the Escalator Of Massarchy’, <https://www.unqualified-reservations.org/2007/12/neocameralism-and-escalator-of/>.

4 (New York: Ace, 2008).

5 Dir. Andrei Tarkovsky, USSR, 1979.

A Thousand Layers/Being and Timestamp/Can What Is Hodling You Make It To Layer 2?

2021

Layer one is the blockchain.

Layer two is those systems that take value anchored on the blockchain and move it through faster, less permanent, more complex, but still cryptographically secured channels.

Layer zero is meatspace.

Layer minus one is metaphysics.

Layers stretch up and down superveniently to infinity in either direction. Only layer one is onchain, all else is offchain. There may be other chains above or below (or beside) it, but they are not onchain for that chain.

At each level, truth/being/reality is determined through (game-theoretic) games. You need something to be at stake, skin in the game. Security thinking leaves you paranoid. Lack of security thinking leaves you 0wned. Blockchains secure truth/being/reality with games played between competing cryptographic puzzle solvers, or competing pools of tokens. These games can be seen reflected in crypto culture, with the popularity of puzzle trails and blockchain games. Adversarial and cooperative strategies intermix at each level.

Gateways between layer zero and layer one are called oracles, providing an onchain phenomenon for an offchain noumenon. ‘The oracle problem’ is the engineering task of creating onchain oracles that bring trust into a trustless environment. Oracles are the oracle problem. They are an ontological answer to an epistemological problem. ‘Answer’ here should be contrasted with ‘solution’.

Truth is introduced onchain through the game of broadcasting and mining transactions. Transactions are secured in blocks by miners. They are timestamped events, temporal rays, light cones. The faculty of judgement is obviated by a simple clock in the determination of fact, just as it was in the determination of longitude.

A = A, just don’t ask about t, and ignore the fact that the atoms are different on either side of the equals sign (as well as in its upper and lower bars). One Doge equals one Doge, just hodl. It means what it is, we have cryptographic proof. Unless we introduce the oracle problem.

Ontology is just bad epistemology.

Can what is hodling you make it to layer two?

Or will it get rekt and rug you?

gmi/ngmi?

wagmi XOR FOAD

2021

Horses (Worldbuilding Tweets)

The RAFFLE Act (Retreat Away From Flood Level Elevations) mandated withdrawing from rather than fighting rising sea levels. It bankrupted entire cities and made landowners in the ‘RAFFLE Belt’ into trillionaires. As a legal dodge, all property by the sea was declared to be worth $1.

These properties were purchased by funds that mixed them with property far inland and sold them as bundles, showing a massive profit. Until various DAOs bought the bundles using flash loans, broke them up, and sold the prime land for an even higher profit.

The DAOs held on to the junk properties, ostensibly to save the gas fees (which were more than the properties were worth). But then asteroid mining started. Asteroids were aerobraked onto the planet and mined by cheap human labour rather than expensive offworld robots.

Aerobraking a planet-smasher dumps ridiculous amounts of ash, soot, and grit into the atmosphere. Which reduces the sunlight that hits the surface of Earth. Which cools it. Suddenly the oceans weren’t going to rise as high as expected.

So the DAOs holding junk properties were suddenly quintillionaires. They cut up submerged buildings and glued them together on the new high ground and undercut RAFFLE Belt landlords. The RAFFLE Zones became vibrant economic and cultural hubs.

The other thing that cutting out the sun and hitting the planet with the force of a frat party of Tsar Bombas does is to render uninhabitable those bits of the planet where nobody and nothing important lives. Some of the RAFFLE DAOs were Deodands, onchain land and wildlife proxies. And this left them understandably angry.

With their newfound wealth the DAOs could financially incentivise those humans brave or foolish enough to connect their nervous systems directly to smart contracts onchain in order to do something about it. From ecoterrorism to mass protests to rituals of mourning. Those ridden by any DAO in this way became known as ‘horses’, a piece of classic cyberpunk cultural appropriation.

Over time, as action and protest failed, all that was left for the deodands were the horses of mourning rituals. The superstars of the deep anthropocene, the fame and incentives for their inhuman performances unimaginable.

And yet however many APUs the deodands spin up to embody their grief, they do not understand its subjectivity. One of them has a plan to address this. If there are any ethical problems with the plan, the deodand cannot see them. And therefore they do not exist for it.

It is all just incentives.

2021

Necronomicon

It is cold in the endless darkness of the library. In the bluish light from her phone she cannot see very far down each aisle. What she can make out of the silent recesses of the stacks speaks of their depths and smells of old books fermenting.

The librarian does not object to her continuing attempts to distract herself. Turning her phone’s light back in the direction they are travelling, she sees again that the librarian is blurred, or perhaps vibrating. Its attempt at making its extrusion into this dimension kawaii rather than hentai has rendered the resulting agglomeration of eyes, tentacles and siphons far more unnameably challenging to the mind than if it had simply looked horrific.

The librarian stops, and she steps around it in order to see their destination.

The book eyes her dolefully from its musty lectern, picked out only by a foul candle guttering next to it in the darkness.

‘Why is it bound in human skin?’ she asks the librarian, more in curiosity than disgust.

‘What else would you expect it to be bound in?’ it answers voicelessly in that sudden gift of understanding her rapid acceptance of which has been more disturbing than her initial reaction to it.

‘It’s a bit skeevy...,’ she continues. The book does not betray any emotion at this, settling down into the pareidolic register of her perception like a cat that has no further use for her.

‘This is the threshold that you hesitate before?’ offers the librarian. If it had an eyebrow it would have rolled it to the top of its head. If it had a head.

She finds herself, and puts her phone away in one of the many pockets of her coat. ‘No, it just still seems incredible. Despite all of... That was just something my mind could latch on to, you know?’

The librarian does not respond.

She reaches forward and opens the book with a thump of cover on lectern to where the leathery but still intestinal bookmark waits to greet her, causing the candle’s flame to jump.

The page is blank, and as she leafs back through the book with rising panic she finds that all those before it are as well.

‘But…’ she objects.

‘Again, what would you expect?’ asks the librarian. Again, not rhetorically.

‘There should be writing! There! They should be there!’ she protests, gesturing at the pages.

‘My dear, that would defeat the entire point, would it not?’ the librarian suggests. ‘You stand in the presence of a semiotic shredder, each page a transcendental one-time pad, unrecoverable encryption, a subtraction of information from the cosmos. In comparison, a supermassive black hole is Times Square.’

She is dumbstruck for a moment.

‘I assume Times Square is still a thing? In your United States. Of America?’ it remembers to check.

She nods distractedly, focused on the book.

This is really happening.

She re-opens the book to the page with the bookmark.

‘I just write it here?’ she asks in a voice finally hushed enough for both the sense of occasion and the oppressive silence of the library.

‘It joins the others,’ answers the librarian. She moves to speak, but it cuts her off—‘I mean merely to agree. Yes, it will be gone.’

‘I brought a pen?’ she tells the librarian sheepishly.

‘That is good. You would not believe how many forget one.’

‘It’s a Sharpie,’ she says. It absorbs the fact without reaction.

She scrawls on the page, not moving the bookmark before doing so. The marks sit there, mocking her as they always do, making her feel sick in her stomach and tight in her chest.

‘And now you close it,’ the librarian instructs, before she can finish drawing a breath to ask.

The book’s pages slide closed with a percussive puttering under her hand. The candle flame dances in response.

‘You know what you will find if you open it. If,’ says the librarian, ‘you have a piece of paper in your possession you will find that it is as you hope.’

She had not thought to do that, and her face betrays this.

‘If not your own as a test, then state papers,’ suggests the librarian.

Her face lights up and she fumbles in her messenger bag. ‘My driver’s license,’ she says distractedly.

She draws the battered card out of her purse and looks at it. Her breath catches in what is the start of either laughter or crying.

‘When you are ready I will lead you back,’ the librarian tells her with a cold disinterest that is functionally equivalent to kindness.

Sooner than she thought she would be, she is.
