First Notebook Entries on Blockchain Art

Concepts, Precedents (23/04/2014)

Accelerationism. Perfecting & intensifying capital to slingshot out of it.

Nick Land’s cyberpositive machine eschatology. Feedback loops & posthuman attractors.

0rphan Drift deities. Descriptions of properties & axioms. Collages of matching media as portraits & sigils.

Art & Language’s collaborative non-art work as critical art-work and artwork. Their use of current topics, and production of indexes. The blockchain(s) as contemporary foci for that kind of activity.

Libertarianism as ultimate capitalism/free market economics. Cryptocurrencies/contracts as freedom from state money and law, a dehumanised analog of society in technology. This is a proper subject for acceleration.

JODI (etc.)-style critique of technology and art-appropriation/detournement of the surplus and failure of technology.

Carey Young’s use of contract law to create [legal] form and relation, to create art critical of social & legal trends.

Art & Language’s comment about critical relations in art work being about modulations of figure/ground relations.

Crypto-signing as identity. Cryptography as privacy. The blockchain as consensus. The politics this touches on and that touches it.

Working and learning together to overcome technical barriers and participate in new social & technical resources (mailing lists…).

Fetishisation of ‘the artist’s signature’ and of crypto signatures. Web of trust/art world.

The Cybernetic Artworld. As an actually productive microworld.

Identity/identification/search for/to identification as key to neorationalism, identity politics, and logistics/search engines.

Danica Phelps’ ledger art. Plains Indian ledger art. These are very different but useful for thinking of art in or on or of the blockchain ledger.

Time and event in the blockchain. On Kawara and Alain Badiou.

ROCI.

Numerology, Gematria. Land again.

Commissions (23/04/2014)

Make a blue artwork 8x8 pixels and submit it for n Ether.

Artwork must be a PBM.

Artwork must be 8x8.

Artwork must be 40-95% blue (#000010..#0000FF etc.)

The contract will check these criteria before paying.

The contract will check uniqueness before paying.

The contract will only accept one artwork from each address.

The contract will only accept n artworks total.

This is the simplest possible meaningful commission template. Size and colour could be dropped but their presence ensures family resemblance and is a good approximation of e.g. the requirements for a mural.

Format, size, colour are easy to check in a simple contract script. A more complex script could ask for a portrait and check for faces.

A kollabor8-style script could ask for a work based on the previous one then check for similarity to ensure it is a derivative. Again this would be much more complex.

A consequences/Mad Libs style commission could ask for n drawings of objects or n drawing strips continuing from the last few pixels of the one above then combine them into a larger whole/finished image.

A Sheep Market-style series commission could specify a subject (‘draw a sheep facing left’) and collect the results.

Images can be sent as transaction data. Write a tool to facilitate this? Take format, size, depth, even evaluation (pre-)conditions from contract comments or data.

Reviews/Critiques (23/04/2014)

Ask people to view n images and:

Star rate each.

Rank them in order.

Tag each with 3/5 words.

Select the ones they like (simple binary choice).

Rate how long it is interesting for (tingle…).

To avoid abuse, include n dummy images.

Seek consensus.

Use images from outside a series, chosen as great or current art. These provide baselines and contrast.

Obfuscate the contract, use multiple contracts rather than a single one that can be taken up n times.

Post contracts at different times of day to get different demographics/regions.

Post different images and different ‘do not rank this one’ dummies in each contract.

Provide a grammar for tagging, or require a particular ontology to be used? No, this adds too much complexity.

Be suspicious of all 1-star or 5-star or same-tag reviews? Use dummies that should be rated to filter this?

Ask people whether they think a review is serious/valid as a next stage? Or just rely on multiple reviews converging & remove outliers?

Shows (23/04/2014)

Either use a multi-commission process or:

Choose a theme and describe it verbally or by visual example.

Ask people to recommend images by image url.

Ask another round of people to evaluate those submissions. Use the review/critique system, with ‘does this image fit the show criteria’ as one of the questions.

Either:

Store the results in the contract’s data as the show.

Offer the show as a contract to whoever wants to pay to show it.

Start a new process to commision a web page or gallery slot for the show.

How to commission catalogue essays and/or a press release for the show?

Use a functional equivalent: a tag cloud or some other outcome.

Ask n people to write a paragraph, get consensus or ask a second wave to score or even glue together.

Ask people what they think should go in a show then ask people how to achieve that.

At each stage get a second wave to evaluate the seriousness/interest of the contributions.

Readymades/nomination/the artist’s intent.

Critiquing formalism and the commodification of art. These were contemporary concerns, but zombie formalism and the art market make them relevant today. And libertarian social reification of relations in economy-representing code map into this nicely.

Dematerialisation as either continued reduction or as a break with objects that can be reduced to their formal essence.

Code reifies concepts, which many artists used to escape commodification. Idea/expression brings in ‘intellectual property’ and enclosure.

Cybernetics, systems, other accompanying discourses.

Deskilling.

So use conceptualism’s strategies on the political indexes of the blockchain, and the blockchain on the political indexes of conceptualism’s critical strategies.

Contracts (24/04/2014)

Ethereum reifies Libertarianism’s fetishisation of state-law-free contracts into technology. Lessig’s ‘code is law’ as an instruction manual.

There is a history of critiquing the legal and economic ontology of art in art. This is part of a more general critique of art’s ontology, although that is also mediated by and through art’s institutional/legal/economic representations.

__________‘s artwork that is only an artwork at particular times. Have a contract that can turn the art status of itself on and off. Combine this with options, shorts, HFT.

A+L’s ‘Invisible Painting’. A contract that reveals its content only to purchasers. Or that can be queried for properties in return for payment. Or that contains the artwork unrecoverably encrypted. Or that has a key available separately.

A+L’s ‘Guaranteed Painting’. Create the original, show it to n people, get them to sign a guarantee of its content, then encrypt & attach & destroy the original. Or just encrypt & sign as a guarantee. Is there any way of selling-on the insurance?

A+L’s ‘100% Abstract’. Describe the construction of a work: the contract itself. This goes with Dan Graham’s ‘Poem Schema’. Self-identity works. See Matt Butler’s XML translation of Graham’s Schema.

On Kawara’s date paintings. Use a nonce instead? How to relate?

Kosuth’s ’One and Three Chairs’. Different representations of the same object. Which object, which representations, how presented? Three values, e.g 1,0001, 0,9999, 1,0 round()ed. Three contracts with the same subject. Three options/derivatives?

Weiner’s ‘Statements’. Are these usable? Use the fact of an evocative proposal that is not executed . So a closure/contract (subcontract) that has this character.

LeWitt’s drawing instructions & schemas. A contract (or contract engine) that accredits images (PBMs) submitted to it with payment as being instantiations if they validate.

Carey Young’s ‘donorcard’: requires two signatures to become art for the duration. How to set the duration?

Carey Young’s ‘Artistic License’. Use public keys.

An artwork registry contract. Submit hash & payment for the smart property or ID to be added to the list of objects recognised as art. Nomination.

Hirst’s ‘For The Love of God’ and other fractional artwork owning schemes. A coloured coin as an artwork that people can buy & sell fractional parts of.

Dave’s [David Bausola’s] account that briefly held a million. A contract that once held n Ether, and the contracts required to make that happen.

___________ ‘s large sheet mail art that I saw at the ICA {Institute of Contemporary Arts, London—2021}. Tokens like this that gentrify mail art and expose logistics and colonise the context while at the same time being irresistible to it.

‘A Tool To Deceive And Slaughter’’s self-selling. This is easy if timers are possible.

A contract that becomes more art the more people sign/pay it.

An art-or-not contract that people can vote on by signing/paying. Either as a thing-in-itself or tied to another object.

Relational art. Contracts that express relations between pairs or ordered tuples/lists of entities. Contractual arrangement as compositional arrangement.

NEThing’s faxes etc. as contracts.

Rauschenberg’s ‘Portrait of Iris Clert’. ‘I say so’ in Ethereum is signing/paying. So sign ‘this is…’.

Maurizio Bolognini’s ‘Programmed Machines’. Pay to have an image generated which either a) isn’t stored or b) is stored unrecoverably encrypted.

Allen McCollum’s ’The Shapes Project’. Pay to get the unique shape for your address. (Address = identity).

www… .jodi—hidden images in apparently broken markup.

Lisa Jeubratt ‘1:1’—mapping IP addresses. Visualise & graph transaction paths & address links, also empty addresses.

Vuc Cosik ‘ASCII Architecture’—ASCII art projection on buildings. Use addresses/transactions on banks etc. .

Charles Broskoski ‘Directions To Last Visitor’. Again GPS vs. other location. Submit an address and show the shortest transaction path to it?

Andy Deck ‘Glyphiti’. Collaborative image editing in a contract.

David Horvitz ‘241543903’—fridge head meme. Use transaction amount as a tag. What else to send?

John F. Simon Jr ‘Every Icon’. A contract to generate them, or a service to return them for payment. Also icons for accounts or transactions.

Jonas Lund ‘Collection Enlargement’. Spam aesthetics. What spam? Contract spam for unrealizable promises?

Visualisations (26/04/2014)

A Bitcoin address is an identifier of 27–23 alphanumeric characters. It is a 160-bit hash of a public key.

A transaction hash is 32 bytes (256 bits).

This can provide:

Address Transaction

4-bit values 40 64

8-bit-values 20 32

15-bit values 10.6 17.07

16-bit values 10 16

There are no 3-value divisors that go precisely into either number of bits.

Single values can be used as depths for height or bump maps, or as indexes into palettes.

Pairs can be used as a, b in Lan colour space, as 2d coordinates on a plane or surface which can then be incrementally raised (by an amount equal to the index of the pair), or as {unfinished in original—2021}

Visualise addresses (identity/portrait), transactions (event/still life), block hashes (landscape).

Animate successive blocks, or successive transactions or account addresses in a chain of transactions.

Graph into planetary systems, constellations.

Coloured Coins (27/04/2014)

e.g. coins representing gold bars. The issuer gives you a transaction hash, called the genesis transaction, and tells you to enter this into a coloured coin client. They then tell you that every 0.0004BTC from the genesis BTC means you have 4 gold bars.

colouredcoins vs. mastercoin vs. OpenTransactions. The latter two have problems.

Coloured coins are a category of smart property. A finite but divisible or multiple resource.

Art editions or units?

Shares.

In a transaction, if input is colour coded, then output is colour coded.

To issue a new colour, release the coins of that colour by creating a ‘genesis transaction’.

Non-reissuable colours—the inputs are irrelevant. The issuer has no power once the transaction is issued, so all that matters is the transaction(’s outputs…).

Let artists submit URLs and addresses to each round of allocations.

Let members vote to add/remove members.

Only allow people to vote if they add funds in a round? OR/ Have funding and voting members/groups, who may or may not overlap.

Run like Rhizome’s art prize, or find a better model.

Have Furtherfield involved as a voting party. Do it under their banner or find a new brand to use?

Vote validity & tiebreaker rules.

Definitely allow outside sponsorship/funding OR/ require that it be channelled through members?

Tie votes to amount of funds, or have a one-member-one-vote system? Which incentivises better? Which is better governance?

Spawn contracts to vote on works.

Votes for objectives.

Link to git repo?

Gathering and allocation of Ether. Allow sponsorship & payment.

This all makes a transparent, democratic, self-documenting art group.

Identify members by keys. Document names in the contract, or allow this degree of anonymity?

Manage attribution, dedication, ownership of works through the DAO.

Have SchellingCoin feeds of opinion, build consensus this way.

Certificates of authenticity.

Artworks that incorporate other contracts and messages to them, bringing in a public that way.

Hot Cold

2014   Ethereum DApp

One of the example contracts from ‘Artworld Ethereum’ was a Conceptual Art homage called hot_cold.se. Here’s an updated version:

init:
contract.storage[1000] = “hot”
contract.storage[1001] = “cold”
code:
// Swap
temp = contract.storage[1000]
contract.storage[1000] = contract.storage[1001]
contract.storage[1001] = temp

When it receives a message, it swaps the strings ‘hot’ and ‘cold’ in its memory.

Is Art

2014, 2016/2017   Ethereum Smart Contract; Ethereum DApp

Is Art is an Ethereum smart contract containing the assertion that it either ‘is’ or ‘is not’ art. Anyone can send this smart contract an Ethereum transaction instructing it to nominate itself as art (or not). A web page connected to the Ethereum network displays the state of this assertion to anyone who can access the contract and provides them with a convenient interface to toggle it between states. When they do so this will become a fact secured in Ethereum’s blockchain with the strength of millions of dollars of computing power a day.

This takes the Conceptual Art ideas of dematerialisation (art that is not presented in a fixed physical form) and nomination (something that is art because someone or something says it is) and combines them with the net.art idea of the interactive artwork that exists within or interferes with network protocols. Whoever toggles the contract’s state as art sets it unimpeded until the next person sends a transaction to change it. A more rational system (and one that does not reduce to mere capital) should be used—bidding, voting, or a prediction market.

The Duchampian strategy of aesthetic transubstantiation through artistic nomination is long played out. It is an art-historical found object, as basic as a smart contract with a single boolean bit of state. This combination of nomination and information invokes Conceptual Art and net.art.

Late 1960s Conceptual Art and mid-1990s net.art are useful inspiration for thinking about the blockchain and smart contracts. These art movements stood in critical tension with the systems of communication, law, and commerce of their eras. Each treated rootless information, whether about sense data or network messages, as the critical subject of art and a new potential art world. Their promise and their eventual recuperation by the existing art world chimes with the historical experience of the blockchain. Brought together, the art-historical and the contemporary technological (or their audiences) can mutually animate and interrogate each other.

Is all of this sufficient to determine whether the contract is or is not art? Where and how is the claim really being made and determined? How does this relate to historical examples of such artworks? And how does it relate to other claims of fact stored in other smart contracts?

Art Is

2014, 2017   Ethereum DApp

A contract that allows anyone to define what art is. It contains a single set of twelve statements about art. They are encoded as hexadecimal values which are interpreted as sentences in a simple subset of International Art English and displayed by the UI.

{  ;; Constant values  ;; Price base (wei), doubled for each definition up to DEFS-COUNT  (def ‘PRICE-BASE 10)  ;; Add to the index to get the price base exponent  (def ‘PRICE-FACTOR-ADD 10)  ;; Number of definitions  (def ‘DEFS-COUNT 12)  ;; Range of values for definitions  (def ‘DEF-MIN 0x1)  (def ‘DEF-MAX 0x0F0F0F0F)  ;; Storage locations  (def ‘artist 0x10)  (def ‘defs-base 0x100)  (def ‘theorists-base 0x200)  ;; State  ;; Contract owner/payee  [[artist]] (caller)  (return    0x0    (lll      {      [action] (calldataload 0)       (when (= @action “set”)         {          [index] (calldataload 32)          [definition] (calldataload 64)          [price] (exp PRICE-BASE (+ @index 1 PRICE-FACTOR-ADD))          ;; If the index is in range and the caller paid enough to set it          (when (&& (>= @definition DEF-MIN)                    (<= @definition DEF-MAX)                    (< @index DEFS-COUNT)                    (= (callvalue) @price))            {             ;; Update definition             [[(+ defs-base @index)]] @definition             [[(+ theorists-base @index)]] (caller)             (- (gas) 100) @@artist @price 0 0 0 0             })          })       }      0x0)) }

MYSOUL

2014   Counterparty and Dogeparty assets

I have placed my soul on the blockchain, representing it as a cryptographic asset token.

The MYSOUL token is on the Dogecoin blockchain as a Dogeparty asset.

I’ve divided it up into 100 units. This is more efficient than having a single token to represent the soul and transferring it to a single owner, as competition within the market will both reduce costs and allocate this resource more efficiently than a monopoly could.

To make ownership of my soul more accessible, I’ve also created a MYSOUL asset on the Bitcoin blockchain with Counterparty.

This is also divided up into 100 units. Counterparty is more expensive for transactions than Dogeparty, but is more widespread, so it’s good to have both options.

Note that my wife will not allow me to sell any of the tokens, as she asserts quite reasonably that my soul belongs to her if anyone.

Dogecode

2014   Python code and Dogeparty tokens

Cryptographic asset tokens can represent all kinds of things.

Including computer programs.

Dogecode takes computer programs in the Brainfuck programming language (chosen for simplicity of encoding):

++++++++[>++++[>++>+++>+++>+<<<<-]>+>+>->>+[<]<-]>>.>---.+++++++..+++.>>.<-.<.+++.------.--------.>>+.>++.

and translates them into a CSV file of token amounts using dcc:

INCB,8
JFOR,1
INCP,1
INCB,4
JFOR,1
INCP,1
INCB,2
INCP,1
INCB,3
INCP,1
...

which are then sent to a Dogeparty address (slowly) as a series of token transfer transactions using dcsend:

Sending lots of tokens. Make sure you really want to do this.
Waiting for token state to synch
Row 1: INCB,8
Waiting for token state to synch...........
Row 2: JFOR,1
Waiting for token state to synch.............
Row 3: INCP,1
Waiting for token state to synch........
Row 4: INCB,4
Waiting for token state to synch..................
Row 5: JFOR,1
Waiting for token state to synch.......................
Row 6: INCP,1
Waiting for token state to synch.......................
Row 7: INCB,2
Waiting for token state to synch......
Row 8: INCP,1
Waiting for token state to synch......
Row 9: INCB,3
Waiting for token state to synch......

The transactions encode the program on the address, which can then be fetched and run using dcrun:

> dcrun -u rpcuser -w rpcpassword \
DCvDS9g9VUZ94MSLbWi4zWRtxHrXeEctZ3
Hello World!

There’s also an easier to use runner that uses dogepartychain.info‘s API rather than requiring a local dogepartyd instance to be running, which will fetch the program encoded as Dogeparty tokens at the specified address (DCvD…) and run it locally:

> dcrunw DCvDS9g9VUZ94MSLbWi4zWRtxHrXeEctZ3
Hello World!

Here are some addresses to try:

DCvDS9g9VUZ94MSLbWi4zWRtxHrXeEctZ3
DFZkhTXwAWW7PqBvVkohCFWobipqw3xmAn
DFibwNZvuJaHM9bD6x1WA63urkHiE4tWzF
DQuineX1BTF4ZD6DQxxsbUgVttmR4LvrdY

Warning

Be careful when writing and uploading Dogecode programs. You are responsible for ensuring that programs run as expected and that you send the correct tokens to the correct addresses. Expense and sadness will accompany any errors.

Dogecode White Paper v0.1

Overview

Dogecode is a system for representing computer programs using sequences of Dogeparty tokens in order to store, send, and execute their code via the Dogecoin blockchain.

Tokens

The encoding for programs is a minor variant of the ‘Brainfuck’ programming language, chosen for its minimalism.1

Each Brainfuck programming language command is represented by an indivisible Dogeparty asset.

A single occurrence of a command in a program is represented by the sending of a single token, e.g. + is represented by sending 1 INCB token.

Multiple occurrences are represented by sending multiple tokens in a single transfer, e.g. +++++ is represented by sending 5 INCB tokens.

The order and quantities of sends are significant, not the total number of tokens held by an address or account.

Currently Used Tokens

Reading bytes into the pointer (the ‘,’ command in Brainfuck) isn’t currently supported. To enter data, use ‘+’ and ‘>’.

INCP > (Increment pointer)
DECP < (Decrement pointer)
INCB + (Increment byte at pointer)
DECB - (Decrement byte at pointer)
PUTB . (Write byte at pointer)
JFOR [ (Jump forward if number at pointer is not zero)
JBAK [ (Jump back if number at pointer is not zero)

Reserved Tokens

These tokens are reserved to support future functionality.

GETB , (Read byte into pointer)
IDAT (Identify data to be read by READ. See Input/Output below.)
RUNC (This section of the code is complete, run it.)
BADC (This section of the code has a problem, do not run.)

Encoding Of Programs

Programs are represented as a sequence of quantities of Dogecode tokens sent from a single address. Order, quantity and sending address are all significant to the system.

Ideally, programs would be represented as multiple sends in a single transaction. Until this is implemented, programs must be sent as an uninterrupted sequence of Dogecode tokens from a single address (no other tokens from the same address may interrupt the send).

Single Program Addresses

Single program addresses represent programs as a newly created Dogeparty address holding only the tokens sent to it in sequence that represent that single program’s code.

No additional programs may be stored using that address.

If transferring the tokens fails or the program is found to be incorrect before it runs, a new address must be created and the correct sequence of tokens sent to it.

Program Queue Addresses (Not Yet Implemented)

Programs queued on an address for execution consist of a sequence of quantities of Dogecode tokens sent from a single address.

Since programs cannot be sent as multiple tokens in a single transaction two addresses may send programs at (approximately) the same time, leading to program token sends from two or more addresses becoming interleaved. This requires sorting token sends by address as well as by time when fetching programs to run.

Input/Output

Input Via +/>

Data can be entered into memory using the + and > commands. This is currently the only supported method for entering data into a program.

Input Via IDAT Token (Not Yet Implemented)

Details forthcoming.

Output For Single Program Addresses

Output is determined by running the program locally.

Output For Program Queue Address Runner (Not Yet Implemented)

Output is provided via broadcast messages on the Program Queue Address (details forthcoming).

Running Programs

Single Program Addresses

To execute the program, the system fetches all sends to the address in order and converts their token amounts to runs of Brainfuck commands. It then passes the resulting string to a Brainfuck interpreter. This will provide the final state and output of the program.

Program Queue Address (Not Yet Implemented)

Programs can be sent to an address that you control in order to be run in a queue.

Transactions from other addresses are ignored.

Used tokens can be returned to the sending address or, if the queue address is also the sending address, kept for re-use.

The runner polls the send table for new sends to the queue address. Sequences of tokens sent from a single address constitute programs to be executed. Programs are terminated with a RUNN token to trigger their execution, or an ERRR token if they should not be executed because (e.g.) a bug was found or the upload was corrupted.

The output of the program can either be recorded locally or output via broadcast messages from the runner address (details forthcoming).

To prevent programs that do not complete in a reasonable amount of time from disrupting the operation of the queue, programs should be run on a thread with a short timeout (details forthcoming).

Program Queue Address As A Service (Not Yet Implemented)

For Dogecode as a service, a program queue address that you control can execute Dogecode programs sent from addresses that others control in return for payment.

Tokens sent to the runner address are not returned, they constitute part of the payment for the service. Additional payment is sent as a Dogecoin transaction following the program tokens.

This system does not use RUNN/ERRR tokens to control execution. Receiving the Dogecoin payment acts as the command to execute the program. Should the payment not be sent within two minutes, the program is not run and the tokens are forfeited—unsuccessful or erronious program uploads can have their execution cancelled by not sending payment. Should the incorrect amount of payment be sent, it can be corrected by sending a supplementary payment before any further Dogecode tokens are sent.
If a supplement is not sent within two minutes, the tokens and incorrect payment are forfeited to the runner account.

The output of the program is output via broadcast messages from the runner address (details forthcoming).

To prevent programs that do not complete in a reasonable amount of time from disrupting the operation of the queue, programs should be run on a thread with a short timeout. The length of the timeout can be extended by the sender incorporating an additional supplement into the Dogecoin fee that triggers execution (details forthcoming).

Dogecoin prices for running are announced by the runner account on its broadcast message feed (details forthcoming).

Token Transfer Speed

Dogecode programs must be sent as ordered sequences of individual Dogeparty asset ‘send’ commands. To ensure that the token sends are incorporated into the blockchain in order, each send is performed only after the previous one is confirmed. This means that it can take minutes for each token run to be sent to the receiving account.

Multiple sends from the same account in the same block could be enabled by having multiple inputs available to it, this does not ensure ordering however.

Ideally, Dogeparty would be extended to allow multi-token sends in a single Dogecoin transaction. This would solve both confirmation and ordering.

Notes

1. <http://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brainfuck>.

Facecoin

2014   HTML5 and JavaScript

Bitcoin is a very interesting development in cyberculture. It’s a repository for the hopes and fears of various ideologies, and a frontier or dark space for the imagination and social or economic activity in a nineties-internet way. Its protocol is a communication model of existence, identity, community and proof, with a Ccru-ish market worship at its base. Because of all of this I think it’s worthy of and desperately needs artistic investigation.

Artworks are proofs of aesthetic work, used as unique value identities both in the market (art is used as an investment, signifier of status, and symbolic resolution of lacks in free market ideology by oil oligarchs and trust fund managers) and by organised crime (stolen art is used as a medium of exchange by criminal gangs). They may not be regarded as much more useful than Bitcoin’s proof-of-work, but at least they look good.

Facecoin performs a very simple form of art-making, generating pixelated portraits by using machine pareidolia as its proof of work. This is not much more intrinsically useful than Bitcoin’s proof-of-work algorithm, which searches for successively longer strings of zeros in very long, very random numbers generated by cryptographic hashing functions. But it is easier to contemplate.

This is implemented by applying CCV’s JavaScript face detection algorithm to SHA-256 digests represented as greyscale pixel maps. An industrial-strength version would use OpenCV. Owing to the limitations of face detection as implemented by these libraries, the digest pixel map must be upscaled and blurred to produce images of the size and kind that they can find faces in. The difficulty can be varied by altering the size and blur of the pixmap. Or by only allowing particular detected face bounds rectangles to be used a set number of times.

If Facecoin was turned into an actual cryptocurrency and widely adopted, the two value identity systems of cryptocurrency and art would be trivially but critically mapped onto each other by millions of machines churning out imaginary portraits across Facecoin’s peer-to-peer communication network as part of its financial network, and vice versa.

If you understand Facecoin you understand proof-of-work cryptocurrency’s block production and some of its critiques at a reasonable level of abstraction. I made it to understand this, so hopefully it can help you understand as well.

Monkeycoin

2014   HTML5 and JavaScript

Monkeycoin is the follow-up to Facecoin. It implements a web-page version of a proof-of-work algorithm for Bitcoin-like cryptocurrency that uses trying to write the complete works of Shakespeare as its puzzle.

Literature and drama are proof of aesthetic work.

Monkeycoin uses attempting to write the complete works of Shakespeare as its proof-of-work function. This is implemented by converting Bitcoin-style SHA-256 cryptographic hash digests of block data to ASCII and checking the results against the first few characters of the First Folio (after dedications and credits), which is the start of The Tempest.

The difficulty of finding new blocks is increased by requiring successively more characters from the text of the complete works of Shakespeare to be matched.

Blank Canvas

2016   Ethereum DApp

A blank canvas that anyone can set the colour of on the Ethereum blockchain.

This was a sketch for Democratic Palette, and a borrowing of the history of the blank canvas in modernist art. It is more complex than Is Art despite appearing to contain a similar amount of information. A single colour is a single piece of information, but in computer science terms it is usually more than a single bit of information—one bit can represent black and white, for example, but to get close to the millions of colours that modern computer graphics use takes twenty-four bits.

The Legend of Satoshi Nakamoto: TORCHED H34R7S aka 1Flamen6

2015

(Collaboration with Marguerite deCourcelle)

Marguerite deCourcelle, The Legend of Satoshi Nakamoto, 2015. Oil pastel on canvas.

 

As a child I loved Kit Thompson’s book Masquerade (1979), even though the solution was a mystery to me. I also had the Usborne book The Knowhow Book of Spycraft (1975), which included sections on encoding secret messages in the visual environment. So when I discovered Marguerite deCourcelle’s ‘crypto puzzle trail’ paintings via Twitter I found them both a very new solution to the problem of making art and assembling an audience—particularly for painting—in the crypto space, and a way of engaging that audience using methods I recognised from my childhood.

Marguerite invented and popularised the crypto puzzle trail, a cultural event in which a community follows a sequence of puzzles, competing and/or collaborating to solve them. The puzzles may be contained in artworks, games, encrypted messages, or some combination of the three. The reward for solving each puzzle may be reputation, cryptocurrency, or (in modern crypto puzzle trails) NFTs. Crypto puzzle trails were an important early means of engaging audiences with cryptocurrency beyond its immediate use as a medium of exchange or speculation.

I became one of Marguerite’s collaborators on her crypto puzzle trail ‘The Legend of Satoshi Nakamoto’, helping to encrypt some of its chain of clues in accordance with her vision. For the last puzzle in the series, Marguerite knew the iconography she was going to use, and we had been surprised by how quickly her community had cracked the last couple of puzzles, so we wanted to come up with something slightly more complex encoded in it.

We knew that people were looking for one-bit binary encoded information, so we decided instead on a six-bit encoding that used variations in the colour and shape of the painted flames that bordered the canvas. To add a very obvious clue, we placed into the painting a six-bit key which the data had to be XOR-ed against, encoded as ribbons tied to a key. The key is a key, it’s six bits, and it’s encoded in wavy red forms that are a bit like flames. We thought that transcribing the information from each edge of the painting and arranging it in the right order would provide more of a challenge.

The encoded solution to the puzzle was a Bitcoin cryptographic private key, chosen to produce a Bitcoin ‘vanity address’ starting with ‘1Flame…’ to fit the theme of the painting. This was prefixed with a short message in English to help the reader establish that they were on the right track. I wrote some JavaScript code to turn the solution into a list of written painting instructions, which Marguerite then followed, painting just under two hundred flames without error. Marguerite placed the prize, an amount of Bitcoin worth around 1200 US dollars at the time, into the Bitcoin address generated from the private key. She announced the puzzle on Twitter, and we settled in for what we thought would be a couple of weeks of people trying to crack the puzzle.

Three years later, that 1200 dollars worth of Bitcoin had become 50,000 dollars worth. I had stored the puzzle solution on my laptop for most of that time, before realising that this was a really bad idea and transferring everything to cold storage. Marguerite and I occasionally shared details about the encoding in order to check that we hadn’t made a mistake with it, in emails that we then deleted—no doubt frustrating for future art historians (and Vice journalists), but some indication of how our security concerns grew along with the value of the prize.

Fortunately, it was then that someone solved the puzzle.

Blockchain Aesthetics

2014–2015, 2019   HTML5 and JavaScript; Unique giclee prints, each with accompanying 1:1 NFT

First Send, 2018. Bitcoin Transaction Hash, 8 Bit Palette, Circles.

Genesis Block, 2019. Bitcoin Block Hash, 8 Bit Palette, Squares.

Genesis Block, 2019. Bitcoin Block Hash, 8 Bit Palette, Squares.

The sheer complexity of modern cryptography and the sheer volume of transactions and blocks that utilise it on the Bitcoin network are difficult to imagine. Blockchain Aesthetics uses grids of simple tokens of artistic form to do so.

Rosalind Krauss’s re-enchantment of the explicit modernist grid misattributed its effect to myth, to addition or supplement.1 The grid has the schematic, overarching, guiding character of myth, to be sure, but its interplay of the abstract and the concrete comes from political rationality rather than storytelling. From subtraction or reification. Krauss recognises this in her consideration of the explicit grid’s early utopian associations. The postmodern grid was a critique of this rationality and utopianism, replacing them with contingency and randomness in order to achieve its meaning.

Injecting new content into the postmodern grid cannot restore it to its modernist significance. It can however place modernist rationality and postmodernist play in tension with each other and focus the resulting attention on that content as a subject of the gaze and of contemplation.

Mechanical encoding of messages into aesthetics is the stuff of kitsch where it has no remainder. Blockchain Aesthetics feeds on data from a blockchain explorer web API and visualises it as modernist, postmodernist, and generative art commonplaces—coloured spots or squares, abstract poetry, random lines or grids. The numeric information of transactions or blocks is (mis-)rendered as something more substantial, giving it the task of communicating an impression of the speed and volume and density of that information.

It’s a kind of landscape. Maybe.

In 2019 a set of prints was generated and printed for Kate Vass Galerie, using the same algorithms as Blockchain Aesthetics but from historical data associated with significant events in blockchain history:

• The mining of the first block—the ‘genesis block’—in the Bitcoin blockchain. This marks the start of Bitcoin as running software rather than an idea described in a whitepaper.

• The sending of the first transaction between two parties on the Bitcoin blockchain. This marks the start of Bitcoin as a usable and useful system for exchanging value without intermediaries.

• The first halving of reward payments by the protocol for mining Bitcoin transactions into blocks. This marks the successful operation of Bitcoin’s software as long-term governance and a generator of economic and historical events in itself.

They’re a kind of history painting. Maybe.

Notes

1 R Krauss, ‘The Originality of the Avant Garde’ in The Originality of the Avant-Garde and Other Modernist Myths (Cambridge, MA: MIT Press, 1986).

Critical Coins

2015   Counterparty / Dogeparty Tokens

To make the process of art reviews and criticism more transparent and quantifiable, we can use cryptographic asset tokens to represent critical opinion and valences. Search for the token names provided below in your favourite DogeParty or CounterParty block explorer.

Critical Approval

Here’s a token of simple, uninflected critical approval:

CRITIQUE

Positive and Negative Critique

Positive-only measures can distort valences. Having tokens for both approval and disapproval addresses this:

POSCRIT
NEGCRIT

Star Ratings

We can use a range of tokens to express a traditional star rating:

ONESTAR
TWOSTAR
THREESTAR
FOURSTAR
FIVESTAR

Sending Tokens

We can use three different kinds of recipients for critical opinion represented in the form of tokens. If the artist (or work) has a Dogecoin address the tokens can be sent to that address directly. Otherwise we can create an address (preferably a vanity address) for them to hold the tokens in escrow. Or we can create an unspendable address (again preferably a vanity address) to send the tokens to. This prevents the tokens being transferred, which is either a bug or a feature depending on whether we want this to be possible or not.

Presumably art is the product of aesthetic competence, and if I as an art historian or critic approve of your exercise of this competence I cannot fault you if your exercise of that competence in the evaluation of someone else’s art or critical competences leads you to transfer the tokens of my critical approval to a third party. So transferable tokens make sense and in fact the history of their transferals adds value: it provides a historical record of regard, influence, and the reach of my original opinion.

Democratic Palette

2016   Ethereum DApp

Every vote for every colour is tracked and the top twelve make up the palette. The only cost of voting is ‘gas’ paid for using Ethereum’s native cryptocurrency, Ether.

Participatory, decentralised art and aesthetics interact with barriers to participation and the ability to buy democracy.

Lottery Symbol

2017 Ethereum DApp

A graphical symbol on the blockchain, chosen from options submitted via Ethereum transactions in a simple lottery.

The lottery uses the blockchain itself as a source of entropy, which is known to be insecure. But this is just a symbol rather than millions of dollars.

This is a piece in a series of works that pair methods of allocation or governance with aesthetic properties. It follows on from Democratic Palette.

SchellingFlags

2018   Ethereum DApp

 
 
 
 

SchellingFlags is a blockchain-based system for establishing the aesthetics and allegiances of flags in a decentralised way.

Flags are an intersection and encryption of the political and the aesthetic, an assertion of identity and allegiance. They have proliferated offchain for social phenomena such as nation states and genders.

Focal points, also known as ‘Schelling points’, are a perceptual phenomenon in game theory. They are values or strategies that people will use because they seem obvious or attractive in a given context.

Using the blockchain, SchellingFlags supports the construction and use of flags as ideological focal points for decentralised social coordination. Any individual can create and share a flag, and any individual can support it. The most popular flags are displayed by the SchellingFlags graphical interface to provide a map of its ideological landscape.

This is the meme of ‘…but on the blockchain’ applied to flags, with a touch of Art & Language and Komar & Melamid. The name refers to the game theorist Thomas Schelling, not the idealist philosopher Friedrich Wilhelm Joseph Schelling. Sorry, philosophers.

Secret Artwork

2018   Ethereum ERC721 Token DApp

Secret Artwork (Content) projected on the metal wall of the vaults beneath the old San Francisco Mint as part of Secrets, curated by Sam Hart and Sarah Hamerman, 2018.

An Ethereum contract stores the cryptographic hash (salted SHA-256) of the content (or subject, or...) of an artwork, a unique but meaningless numeric identifier. This proves that the artwork exists (to the extent it can be cryptographically verified) but offers no clue as to the content.

The artwork is ‘ownable’ as an Ethereum ERC721 token, tying the uniqueness and identifiability of the content to a unique (quasi-)property relationship. But the owner knows no more about the content of the artwork than the general public. They own something unknowable, or that which cannot be fully owned.

As each block in Ethereum’s blockchain is layered on top of the last, the cost of the computing power with which the secret and its almost-ownership is asserted quickly exceeds the price of the world’s most expensive artwork. And the certainty with which these facts are established quickly surpasses that of the most secure physical provenance.

The presentation of the corresponding ERC721 token takes the form of a DApp, which reveals all details that can be fetched from the network about the artwork. Its owner, when it was created, even the hash of the content. It displays each of these pieces of data in many ways—as text, as colours, as shapes, as musical notes. The visual register seeks to distract the viewer from the gap in their purchase on (or of) the artwork by relentlessly presenting these different facts.

But still it remains.

Local Maxima

2019   GAN-generated images and giclee prints

In classical AI research, a ‘local maximum’ is a solution to a problem that cannot in itself be improved but that is not the best possible solution. Where a singular mathematical specification of a problem is possible, local maxima can be avoided using search strategies other than simple ‘hill climbing’. Art does not have such a specification, its search space consists entirely of local maxima.

Generative Adversarial (neural) Networks extract aesthetic surplus value from images, rendering that surplus value liquid. Training a GAN deterritorialises their form, generating new images with it reterritorialises it. Contemporary AI burns capital to produce discrimination, here discrimination between images that resemble the learned source material and those that do not.

The source material in this instance is my own previous work, playful compositions from the 1990s. A GAN was trained on them and then applied to simple geometric shapes, transferring the extracted ‘style’ of the source images leavened with the perceptual artefacts of GAN training. Reducing (or promoting) artworks to flows of aesthetics that can be continued indefinitely is deeply challenging to art history and connoisseurship.

GAN-generated images tend to Pollockian all-overness, or to the same local sense but global semantic incoherence as Markov chains or a Francis Bacon painting. The conception of ‘style transfer’ that all of this involves is deeply challenging to art theory.

Tokens Equal Text

2019   Ethereum ERC-998 and ERC-721 tokens

Tokens Equal Text consists of an edition of 32 Ethereum ERC-998 composable tokens each of which owns four child ERC-721 non-fungible tokens. The ERC-721 tokens have short descriptive texts encoded as their ID numbers. This makes the ERC-998 tokens compositions of those text fragments. The content of that composition can be recovered if it is known that the token IDs can be (mis)read as text rather than numbers.

These text fragments describe key visual tropes of the Vaporwave movement. Vaporwave is a 2010s internet-based musical and cultural genre devoted to the ironisation of muzak and digital kitsch aesthetics from the long 1980s. It is named for ‘Vaporware’, future software that is announced but never released. Vaporwave has an inherent melancholy and a sense of lost future potential; in its ironising mashing together of time and desire it is inherently queer and, in leading examples such as Macintosh Plus’s genre-defining Floral Shoppe (2012), trans.

The question of whether a given trope fits the aesthetic of Vaporwave is key to the genre’s discourse, with canonical tropes establishing the content of that aesthetic. A composition of references to canonical elements of that aesthetic at least admits of the question ‘Is this aesthetic?’

Using Ethereum Web3 browser app code to display the texts of tokens in visual compositions using ‘aesthetic’ (in the Vaporwave sense) typography and colours nudges that answer along. It also prevents the content from becoming a trivial puzzle. The encoded information is placed, not hidden. Its easy recovery foregrounds the presence of compositional and indexical structure that rewards reflection.

The ‘Rare Art’ market demands something aesthetic to own. Nothing is more desirable to own than something that cannot be owned. Vaporwave is built on sampled and appropriated audio recordings, video, images, and 3D models. It is unownable, making it ideal common property for an internet cultural scene. This does however make it very difficult to truly claim ownership of most of its products without attracting the ire of corporate copyright holders.

By encrypting recoverable and imaginable references to this unownable aesthetic, Tokens Equal Text loops desire for ownership back through a complexly unownable genre of aesthetics to both satisfy and frustrate this demand and to place blockchain property and the history of art into a state of mutually critical interrogation.

(I was listening to Floral Shoppe pretty much on repeat while I made it.)

Staking Ratio

2019   Ethereum DApp

A ratio of two amounts of Ether cryptocurrency that is staked within a smart contract by its users. Value determines value, further destroying the information that money destroys in order to represent it.

This is the last work in the series of resonant pairings of aesthetic properties and governance or economic allocation methods that began with Democratic Palette. It is the first to deal directly in cryptocurrency value.

Use at your own risk.

Token Grid

2019   Ethereum DApp

The grid is a Modernist icon and the underlying organisational form of many aspects of our daily lives. Whether a North American city street plan, a power grid, the face of International Style architecture, page layout in print or onscreen, or the pixels of that screen, grids pervasively organise our activities and experiences.

This one exists on the Ethereum blockchain with a graphical user interface accessible via the world wide web. In contrast to the fixed grids of city streets and architectural structures, anyone can change it, but only if they are willing to procure and then burn one of the grid smart contract’s ERC-20 digital tokens in order to do so.

Proof of Work Bitmap

2019   Ethereum DApp

A 16×16 pixel monochrome bitmap that lives on the Ethereum blockchain. You can change it if you run a simple proof-of-work calculation and send the result in the update transaction.

Proof of Work Bitmap is the latest in a series of pieces that pair aesthetic properties with methods of economic allocation or social governance (in no particular order, Democratic Palette, Proof of Work Bitmap, Staking Ratio, Hack Line Properties, Pay Previous Path, Lottery Symbol, Token Grid). In computer art and digital culture, a monochrome bitmap is the simplest representation of a discrete image. In blockchain-based systems a ‘proof of work’ is a time-consuming computational puzzle that is impossible to cheat on but simple to check the result of. The result of solving that puzzle in Bitcoin, Ethereum, and Proof of Work Bitmap, is a 256-bit number with a specific number of zeros at the end. This is the same size as a 16×16 bitmap and as a memory cell in Ethereum. There’s a resonance here, as there is in each piece in the series.

Pay Previous Path

2019   Ethereum DApp

Pay Previous Path allows anyone to store a vector drawing (encoded in a style similar to the SVG path line-art format) on the Ethereum blockchain in return for paying a small fee to the previous person who did so.

You may notice that the payment creates a path or line between accounts. This is the penultimate piece in the series of artworks pairing aesthetic objects with economic allocation methods.

Hack Line Properties

2019   Ethereum DApp

Hack Line Properties is a supposedly secure blockchain smart contract designed to allow only its owner to update the line stroke properties that it stores. But a common Ethereum code bug allows anyone who finds it to ‘hack’ the contract and set the line properties themselves.

If code is law in the sense promoted by early Ethereum idealism, any action we can take using code is legal. But if that code is a bug, it embodies no intention and may break the intended operation of the software. Sometimes code has to be rescued by exploiting a bug (the first moon landing is a good example of this). Is this still a form of governance?

2020   ERC-721 Tokens

Certificate of Inauthenticity

Between 2011 and 2012, for the Shareable Readymades project I commissioned the awesome artists Chris Webber and Bassam Kurdali to create three 3D printable models of commonplace objects—a urinal, a balloon dog, and a pipe—that had become part of canonical art history. The artists retained copyright on their work and the results were placed under the Creative Commons Attribution-ShareAlike (CC-BY-SA) licence with the attribution to myself as the person that commissioned the work. I did this in order to critique the production of neo-conceptual art by artists such as Jeff Koons and the processes by which art history cordons off models of commonplace objects as signature works by Jeff Koons, Marcel Duchamp, and others.

Furtherfield gallery in London (who meta-commissioned the Balloon Dog model) exhibited the works with ‘certificates of inauthenticity’ that I détourned from Sol LeWitt’s certificates for their wall drawings. Then at the #BlockchainLive18 show, also in London, Ruth Catlow and Dr. Charlotte Frost of DECAL and Futherfield produced gold castings of the Balloon Dog model and registered the resulting ‘Cryptopuppers’ on Arteia’s blockchain art registry.

I hadn’t planned these developments, but they diagonalised the project in interesting ways. The former recapitulated Conceptual Art’s capture by the very systems of sale and ownership that it had initially sought to escape. The latter mapped this onto the constant development of technosocial phenomena within successive waves of Andy Cameron and Richard Barbrook’s ‘Californian Ideology’ by taking a 3D printing project that was meant to be free-as-in-freedom and making an expression of it exclusively ownable with cryptographic certainty on the blockchain.

As ‘Rare Art’, blockchain token-based digital editions have become the latest expression of ownership of value that would previously have escaped capture. The technology of blockchain-based digital certificates of ‘ownership’ of digital art images was initially developed ironically (‘Rare Art’ comes from ‘Rare Pepe’ trading cards). It was then captured by startup capital that cannot explain its contradictions. In its Rare Art iteration, Ethereum ERC-721 Non-Fungible Tokens (NFTs) for digital image URLs are presented as solving the problem that digital art is infinitely reproducible and therefore cannot be sold as a rare asset. They establish authentic provenance and ownership where this was not previously possible.

It is difficult to represent offchain truth onchain, however. It is prohibitively expensive to put bitmapped digital images onchain. And it is impossible to make sure that a digital image is represented uniquely even within the same smart contract on a single blockchain. This means that we must trust the claim that the image represented by each ERC-721 token is what its creator claims. The authenticity of Rare Art images is threatened by ‘piracy’, where an artist’s images are copied from one Rare Art platform to another and sold without their consent. It is also threatened by ‘trash art’, where the art produced is an undeclared auto-tracing or other mechanical transformation of stock images. Artists who feel entitled to the monetary and reputational value of the art they produce regardless of the method used and collectors who feel entitled to react simply to the price of Rare Art tokens are clashing over matters of authenticity in platforms that were supposed to render such questions moot.

Why not turn this risk into value and make a virtue of inauthenticity?

To make the link between offchain truth and the problem of authenticity explicit I have created a Rare Art edition of Certificates of Inauthenticity for the Shareable Readymades series. If you own one of these tokens you can prove that I have not made the instance of Urinal, Balloon Dog, or Pipe that you have 3D printed or placed in virtual reality (which the CC-BY-SA license on the work gives you full permission to do). Print out the certificate that you own the NFT for and display it proudly in a physical gallery, or display it as a digital image on a virtual wall.

Then if anyone questions the authenticity of the art that you are displaying and your right to own it, you can answer that the art is provably inauthentic and that you have no more ownership of it than anyone else. And that you know this to be true with all the cryptographic certainty of the blockchain.

Shared Secret

2020   TOTP QR Code

Shared secrets are part of the invisible structure of society. They constitute a form of currency.

Computing security culture defines ‘secrets’ as passwords, private keys, as unguessable slices of entropy. They are to be revealed only briefly to cross the threshold of a computing system. The consequences of sharing them with attackers, which is to say other people, are catastrophic—leading to your presence in the system or the system itself becoming compromised, or 0wned.

Such sharing is therefore strongly taboo.

Two secrets are stronger than one when establishing that the person presenting them is authentically the person they claim to be. To lose one secret may be regarded as a misfortune; to lose both looks like carelessness. The second ‘factor’ of many such contemporary systems is generated by software using the ‘Timed One Time Password’ (TOTP) algorithm, which generates a new secret value every thirty seconds from a seed value.

Sharing the secret value compromises your security for the thirty seconds during which it is valid. Sharing the seed value means you can be 0wned permanently.

But if that seed isn’t being used for security purposes, sharing it is harmless. It becomes a shared secret in the sociological rather than the security culture sense, currency rather than taboo.

Doesn’t it?

1 Doge = 1 Doge

2021   Rasterised vector image NFT

Such proof that 1 Doge = 1 Doge. Wow.

A cryptographic ‘proof’ of the assertion that 1 Doge = 1 Doge, rendered in the form of a cryptographic wallet key ‘seed’ and the derivation of a Bitcoin address from it, presented using the deliberately naive typography and colour palette of ‘Doge’ memes such as those that promote the Dogecoin cryptocurrency.

This assertion may resemble the law of identity, as seen in Plato’s Theaetetus (and the writing of Ayn Rand…), but here it is ‘proven’ ironically rather than taken as an axiom, reaching inside a statement of economic fungibility that has become an expression of faith in the lasting social value of cryptocurrency separate from its dollar value.

The ‘proof’ was generated using a modified Bitcoin vanity address generator that selected only seed values starting with 1Doge, and then searched for Bitcoin addresses beginning with the same prefix. Dogecoin addresses could not be used as they start with ‘D’ rather than ‘1’, and structuring the ‘proof’ for use on another chain adds a layer of referentiality and robustness.

Since the private key for this address has been published in the artwork, it should not be used to actually receive Bitcoin as anyone will be able to steal the results.

Glossary

10K Project

A series of mechanically generated profile picture (PFP) images in a shared style sold as NFTs. Usually as an edition of 10,000 after CryptoPunks, which is the original template for such projects.

51% Attack

A network attack on a blockchain that uses the greater share of computing power devoted to mining new blocks to censor or rollback transactions rather than properly extend the chain.

Accelerationism

The strategy of aligning oneself with and increasing the rate of the decoding or deconstructing effects of the system (…capitalism) in order to break down prior restrictive social forms. More generally: intensifying something in order to increase its rate of becoming.

Anarcho-capitalism

A social order without the state and its coercive institutions but with money, contracts, and the commodity form of property. The former is the ‘anarcho’ part, the latter the ’capitalist’.

Anarcho-communism

A social order without the state and its coercive institutions and also without any alienating economic activity or social forms. The former is the ‘anarcho’ part, the latter the ‘communist’.

ASIC

ASICs (Application-Specific Integrated Circuits) are custom-manufactured computer chips that implement the cryptographic hashing algorithms used in mining cryptocur­rency. They are faster than CPUs or GPUs and were regarded as cheating or otherwise unfair by some people historically.

BIP-0039

A Bitcoin Improvement Proposal standard that specifies how to encode cryptographic private keys using a standard list of words rather than numbers.

Block

A collection of transactions or txes from a particular period of time validated by the network.

Blockchain

A series of blocks of validated transactions, each linked to its predecessor by its cryptographic hash. Bitcoin’s innovation was not to produce a blockchain, which is essentially just a Merkle list, it was to produce a blockchain in a securely decentralised way.

Burn

To destroy or render a cryptographic token inaccessible by sending it to an address that has no associated cryptographic key.

Ccru

The Cybernetic Culture Research Unit, an experimental philosophical group founded by Sadie Plant at Warwick University in 1995 that escaped the gravity well of academia to become something increasingly more (self-)mythologising, until its dissolution around 2000.

Copyleft

The use of copyright law in a licence to ironise its restrictions into lasting permissions.

CounterParty

An early cryptographic token platform based on top of the Bitcoin blockchain.

Creative Commons

A copyright reform nonprofit and its eponymous alternative copyright licences (including the copyleft CC-BY-SA) and copyright waiver (CC0).

Cryptoeconomics

The combination of cryptographic systems and the theory of game-theoretic economic incentives.

Crypto Wars

The legal and legislative battles of the 1990s, waged to ensure that the public could access and use effective cryptography without government backdoors.

Cypherpunk

A member of the eponymous ‘cypherpunks’ mailing list in the 1990s, or someone who holds to their principles of techno-anarchism pursued through cryptography.

DAO

Decentralised Autonomous Organisation. A blockchain corporation or co-opera­tive, the technological equivalent of a charitable trust.

DApp

Distributed App. A complete, usable Ethereum application comprising smart contracts and a web-based user interface.

Dark Forest

Science fiction author Cixin Liu’s novelistic universe in which the game theory of first contact with alien races makes any interstellar communication an existential risk. This means that you must either hide perfectly from everyone else in the universe, or destroy them completely as soon as you become aware of them before they can move to destroy you based on the same logic.

Dark Forest Strike

A world- or star-system-destroying attack on one (potentially) interstellar species by another based on the existential risk analysis logic of the dark forest.

DDoS

Distributed-denial-of-service attack. Using many, often hacked, computers to flood a server with so many network requests that it is unable to respond to them all, effectively taking it offline.

Digging

The Dogecoin equivalent of mining.

Discrete Log Problem

Public-key cryptography relies on ‘trapdoor functions’ that provide results that are easy to calculate but impractically difficult to reverse. The function used to derive public keys from private keys, point multiplication on an elliptic curve, can be calculated easily enough. But, although it has not been mathematically proven to be impossible, there is no known method for calculating its inverse, point division on an elliptic curve. This is known as the discrete log problem.

Doge

A dog. Probably a Shiba Inu.

Dogecoin

The first ‘memecoin’, a fork of Bitcoin’s code with a faster block time and different mining algorithm, but mostly lots of doge memes.

Dogeparty

A fork of CounterParty’s code to run atop the Dogecoin blockchain rather than the Bitcoin blockchain.

Dump

To sell off a coin or token that has been pumped, crashing the market for it.

Ethereum

A blockchain supporting general-purpose code execution rather than Bitcoin’s limited transaction scripts.

Ether

The native cryptocurrency token used on Ethereum.

ERC

Ethereum Request For Comment. The ERC-20 standard defines ‘fungible tokens’, essentially new cryptocurrencies that operate on the Ethereum blockchain. ERC-721 defines ‘non-fungible tokens (NFTs)’.

Exchange

Cryptocurrency exchanges allow you to swap between different coins/tokens.

Exploit

A means of hacking a piece of software.

Fees

Cryptocurrency transaction fees paid to miners for inclusion in a block.

Fiat

The act of declaring something to be so by the arbitrary exercise of power—state power, in the case of fiat currency such as paper notes that are not backed by precious metals.

FIFO

First-in-first-out. A data structure like a pile of documents where the topmost one is always the first one to be removed.

Fork

A split in a project. Either of the source code for a program which has been developed into two different versions, or of one cryptocurrency chain into two rival ones.

Fungible/Non-Fungible

Fungibility is substitutability. Two things are fungible if you can swap one for the other without meaningful difference. Like two coins of the same denomination. Non-Fungibiliity is the quality of uniquely identifiable or meaningfully different things, like two different paintings in the same series.

Gas

Ether used to pay for the execution of code by Ethereum.

Genesis Block

The first block mined for a particular blockchain.

GitHub/GIT

git is a program that keeps track of changes and versions of a project’s software source code and other resources in a decentralised manner. It uses cryptographic hashing to keep track of this in a repository or ‘repo’, consisting of a series of ‘commits’ of groups of changes. It looks a lot like a blockchain, with the proof of work algorithm using software developers rather than mining rigs. GitHub is Microsoft’s walled garden for centralising software development, not to be confused with the underlying git version control system (VCS).

GNU

GNU’s Not Unix. A project to create a free-as-in-freedom operating system for all computer users. Producers of the eponymous GNU GPL copyleft licence for software.

Hash

A unique, algorithmically generated, unguessable identifier for a piece of data. As a verb, the act of applying that algorithm.

Hodl

To hold cryptocurrency, named after a typo on a Bitcoin message board.

ICO

Initial Coin Offering. A sale of cryptographic tokens to fund a project.

Iota

An early ‘internet of things’ post-blockchain project.

IPv6

Most recent version of the Internet Protocol (IP) launched in 2012 as an improved and more scalable replacement for the long-standing IPv4, which suffered from various limitations including potential address exhaustion.

Keys

The pair of very long numbers or other mathematical entities that make up the public and private part of a mod­ern cryptographic identity.

Layer Two Network

A faster system for using an existing cryptocurrency using addi­tional, more complex, software and cryptoeconomic incentive structures.

LOIC

Low Orbit Ion Cannon. A DDoS tool.

MAST

Merkleized Abstract Syntax Tree. A Merkle tree containing different code or data at different branches, allowing the sender to commit to them now and use one of them later without having to reveal any of the others.

Markov Chain

A table of lists of elements that are likely to follow one another, allowing a representative sequence to be built up using random choices from them. Popular for creating variations on themes before the advent of generative adversarial networks (GANs).

Merkle Tree

A computer science ‘tree’ structure in which the leaves are data and each branch is the cryptographic hash of those below it. A compact and immutable representation of data. Txes in a blockchain block are arranged in a Merkle tree.

Mining

Validating blocks in return for rewards including mining fees.

Mining pool

Hardware or people sharing in the work and rewards of mining blocks.

NameCoin

The first Bitcoin fork, which added support for publication of name/value pairs to create a decentralised equivalent to the Internet Domain Name system.

net art / net.art

1990s internet art. Add the dot to name a particular movement of that period.

NFT

A Non-Fungible Token. A cryptographic digital asset that can be uniquely identified within its smart contract.

OG

‘Original Gangster’. An appropriated honorific indicating respect for someone who has successfully played the market longer than oneself.

Onchain/Offchain

Information or events that occur within blocks of a blockchain, or out in the real world.

One-Time Pad

The only perfect form of encryption. A unique series of random numbers used to encode each piece of information in a secret message. Only a recipient with the same unique series of numbers can hope to decrypt it.

OpenCV

An image-processing and image-recognition software library.

Oracle Problem

The problem of establishing onchain that an asserted fact holds true offchain with any degree of confidence.

Patch

An incremental modification to a piece of software.

PostScript

Adobe’s ‘page description language’. A 2D graphics programming language used most recognisably in laser printers and industrial printing presses.

Prediction Market

A betting pool for or against the likelihood of future events that is used to measure their probability.

Proof of Stake

An alternative to Proof of Work that uses the ‘staking’ of large amounts of a blockchain’s cryptocurrency to establish who gets to mine the next block. This uses a lot less energy than Proof of Work.

Proof of Work

Performing a computationally-intensive task to prove that the accompanying data is being provided in good faith. Originally developed to prevent email spam. Bitcoin and other early cryptocurrencies use it to secure their blockchains by making it costlier to attack the chain than it is to extend it properly.

Public-Key Cryptography

The use of two separate but mathematically entangled encryption keys, one shared publicly and one kept private,when encrypting or signing messages. Knowledge of the public key does not give anyone any knowledge of the private key, so the public key can be shared to allow messages to be encrypted to its owner and to check cryptographic signatures from them without this being a security risk.

Pump

To artificially inflate the price of a coin or token through nefarious means before dumping it.

Rare Art

An early name for NFT-based digital image art.

Rare Pepes

An early and deeply ironic (quasi-)NFT project based on the CounterParty blockchain, featuring images of ‘Pepe The Frog’. Rare Art stripped out the irony and moved to Ethereum from CounterParty, leaving a historical gap.

Scam

A scam or, if you are being accused of one, simply anything your accuser disagrees with or dislikes.

Schelling Point

A ‘focal point’ value that people might be expected to arrive at independently. Named after the economist, not the philosopher.

SHA-256

A popular cryptographic hash algorithm. A ‘salted’ SHA-256 hash adds extra information to the data to be hashed in order to make it harder to guess from the resulting hash value—this is useful for passwords, for example.

Shard

A smaller partition of a blockchain that is able to communicate with other shards while not having to process all their transactions. This is one way of increasing the number of txes a blockchain can process.

Shibe

From ‘Shiba Inu’, the Dogecoin mascot. A Dogecoin hodler, an excellent person. Like ‘comrade’ or ‘choombatta’.

Shitcoin

A minor cryptocurrency that offers nothing compelling. Possibly a scam.

Sidechain

A smaller blockchain attached to a larger one but isolated from it by different rules.

Side Channel Attack

Using information leaked through contingent sources to break an encryption algorithm or extract secret information from a system. Timing of network responses, electromagnetic or loudspeaker noise, etc.

Smart Contract

A program stored on the blockchain that executes in response to blockchain txes.

Techno-libertarianism

The belief that in order to maximise its liberatory potential, technology should not be restricted by the state.

Token

A cryptographic token built on top of a cryptocurrency blockchain. The representational proxy for an identifiable and quantifiable ownership claim on some other value or object, real or imagined.

Trusted Third Party

An intermediary that can interfere in a social relationship between peers and must therefore be trusted not to.

Tx, Txes

A blockchain transaction or transactions.

Wallet

A piece of software, hardware, or paper used to secure cryptocurrency account keys.

Web 2.0

The social media platform and ecommerce era of the internet. Contrast with Web 1.0, which was more about homepages. And Web 3.0, which is about blockchain-backed identity and commerce.

Wei

The smallest unit of the Ether cryptocurrency, equivalent to a Satoshi for Bitcoin. Named after the cypherpunk cryptographer Wei Dai.

Whale

A hodler of very large amounts of cryptocurrency or NFTs.

Wow

What a doge says when it thinks something is amaze.

Wrapped

A fungible token that can be exchanged for a specific kind of NFT, effectively wrapping the NFT in a currency representing it.

Zero-knowledge Proof

A family of algorithms for encoding and demonstrating that you know a piece of information without having to reveal it or anything else that it describes.

Zk-SNARKs

‘Zero Knowledge Succinct Non-Interactive Argument of Knowledge’, an algorithm that allows the existence of secret knowledge to be proven without revealing its content.

 

For those wishing to explore blockchain technology, the following books are recommended reading:

B. Vickers (ed.), The White Paper by Satoshi Nakamoto (London: Ignota, 2019).

F. Brunton, Digital Cash: The Unknown History of the Anarchists, Utopians, and Technologists Who Created Cryptocurrency (Princeton, NJ: Princeton University Press, 2020).

A.M. Antonopoulos, The Internet of Money (Merkle Bloom, 3 vols, 2017).

A. Narayanan, J. Bonneau, E. Felten, A. Miller, S. Goldfeder, Bitcoin and Cryptocurrency Technologies: A Comprehensive Introduction (Princeton, NJ: Princeton University Press, 2016).

R. Catlow and P. Rafferty (eds), Radical Friends: Decentralised Autonomous Organisations and the Arts (Torque Editions, 2022).

R. Catlow, M. Garrett, S. Skinner and N. Jones (eds), Artists Re: Thinking the Blockchain (London:

Furtherfield, 2017) [Contains earlier versions of ‘Blockchain Poetics’ and ‘Bad Shibe’.]
